Python `Aplic` with the `riscv,aplic` node (`riscv,num-sources`, `msi-parent`, `#interrupt-cells = <3>`, `interrupt_input` as `&label src 1 4`); `interrupt_number(sourceN)` is N | `cmake/patches/`, `cmake/Dependencies.cmake`, `src/socpuppet/CMakeLists.txt` (build `rvi/aplic.cpp`), `models/aplic.{h,cpp}`, `tests/cpp/contracts/aplic_contract.h` + `_test.cpp`, `builtin_components.cpp`, `components.py`, `docs/upstream.md`, `test_devicetree.py` |
| M13 13 | ✅ **The exit test: a shell that answers Python.** `host()` places `aplic` on the IO die at its window offset with `msi-parent` the IMSIC, the IO die's up-router (root complex DMA and APLIC messages to the link), `uart.irq -> aplic.source16` (identities below 16 are the CPU's own in Zephyr's table); devicetree regenerated. Firmware: the APLIC `irq_enable` glue (route source to its identity, enable both), `riscv,aplic` with `msi-parent`, `CONFIG_UART_INTERRUPT_DRIVEN`, and `samples/subsys/shell/shell_module socpuppet_host` added to `firmware/build.sh`; images rebuilt. `tests/python/test_m13_exit.py`: on `host()` with the shell image, run until the prompt is in `uart.output`, `uart.write("kernel version\n")`, run until the version line; the interrupt map says the UART's line goes to the IO die's APLIC and the only lines on the compute die are the timer's and the IMSIC's; the devicetree names no PLIC; and the disk test still passes (M8's test, both message paths). Mutations: the APLIC's `msi` unconnected, the UART's line unwired | `boards/host.py`, generated devicetrees, `zephyr_module/` glue, `firmware/build.sh`, `tests/python/test_m13_exit.py`, `test_host_board.py` |
| M13 14 | ✅ **The show and the docs.** `examples/shell_from_python.py`: Python types, the shell answers, the transcript tells it. Pages: `docs/models/imsic.md` and `aplic.md` (new), `dbt-rise-cpu.md` (the core parameter, supervisor mode, `time`), `ns16550.md` (receive and interrupt no longer left out), `msi-plic-bridge.md` deleted with the model (step 8 does the code; this step makes sure no page links to it), `architecture.md` (diagram, slot table with the message controller and the APLIC, the models list, provenance), `boot-your-firmware.md` ("Typing on the console", "Interrupts as messages"), [upstream.md](upstream.md) (Zephyr: `irq_enable` knows no IMSIC or APLIC; the IMSIC driver's flat identities; the APLIC driver's bit 11; the PCIe MSI entry updated), and plan.md (Decided block, the table with ✅, "What M13 delivered / found / did not do", "Things M14 should know", Status). Not a `/tdd` step | docs, examples, `docs/plan.md` |

What the steps found, as they went:

- **Step 1: the simulator's parameters are numbers, so `isa` and `modes` cross as two flags.** The registry's `Config` maps a name to an integer. The C++ adapter takes `floating_point` and `supervisor_mode`, each 0 or 1, and has its own refusal for a combination with no core. Python has the strings, and refuses in its own words before the simulator exists.
- **Step 2: `time` is read over a socket pair, `cpu.time -> timer.time`, and not a 64-bit wire.** Two things in the borrowed code decided it. The ACLINT wakes itself every tick once its `mtime` output is bound, ten million times a simulated second. And a signal cannot carry how far ahead of the clock its reader is, so `time` would lag the timer's own register by up to a quantum, which DBT-RISE's own reading of it does. A read of eight bytes with the CPU's lead as its delay costs nothing until firmware asks, agrees with the bus to the tick, and needs no new kind of connection. It is the shape step 7 has for the IMSIC's registers too. Both are in [upstream.md](upstream.md), with a third the tests turned up: a write to the ACLINT's `mtime` adds the time since its last update.
- **Step 3: the supervisor-interrupt fault was as it read, and the patch stayed inside its box.** The handler never ran, then ran once with the mask mended and `sie` made a view of `mie`. Two more tests asked for the rest: `sip` read zero in the handler until it was a view of `mip`, and a supervisor's own software interrupt, set through `sip`, came to nothing until the write was one too. Four functions in one file, 135 lines of patch.
- ⚠️ **Step 4 did not go green by itself, and what stopped it was not in the plan.** The host's disk test, image unchanged, took a load fault on `rv64gc_msu` and passed on the old core. DBT-RISE's hart with supervisor mode marks a pending interrupt the way a fault in the current instruction is marked, so an instruction that reads memory or a CSR, and that an interrupt arrives at, stops before writing its result and is never executed. Here it was the `ld ra` of a function's epilogue, under a timer interrupt. It was found by running the test on both cores with instruction tracing on and looking for where 1.3 million instructions first differed. One line of patch, a test that has an interrupt arrive at a load, and an entry in [upstream.md](upstream.md). 💡 The planning check that the images use no PMP and no floating point was right, and was not the whole question: the core itself had never run anything with interrupts.
- **Step 6: the hart reaches its IMSIC by an address that is the CSR's number, twelve bits up, with what `miselect` selected in the low twelve** (`models/hart_registers.h`). So the IMSIC keeps no selector, the hart keeps twelve bits of `miselect`, and a trace of the socket can be read. A claim is a read and then a write, with no wait between, which is what makes it one act. The contract needed the supervisor's registers as well as the machine's, to switch that file on at all, so both are answered now and step 7 has only the hart's half to do. ⚠️ For step 7: `csr` is not a required port yet, and an IMSIC with nothing there can never interrupt. ⚠️ For step 9: Zephyr's driver enables identity N in register `eie0 + N / 64`, which is right only below 64. Above that it selects an odd-numbered register, which a 64-bit hart has not got, and traps. 63 identities is what keeps the host clear of it. ⚠️ For M20: Linux's binding is not this node. It wants one node for each privilege level, `riscv,imsics` in the plural, and no `riscv,hart-id`.
- **Step 7: a claim needs the same yield a bus access has.** The core hears that a line fell from a method, which runs when the core's thread lets it. A handler that claims through `mtopei` and makes no bus access returned with the interrupt still pending and was entered 136 times in a quantum. The adapter yields for up to two delta cycles after a write to the IMSIC, as DBT-RISE is patched to after a bus access. 💡 The test only showed it once its handler touched no memory: a store that goes over the bus yields by itself and hides the fault, and one that goes by DMI does not. Whether there is an IMSIC at the end of `csr` is found by asking, the first time firmware selects a register, because a socket nobody connected is bound all the same. **`smaia` is not listed**, as the plan thought it might be: the name stands for all the AIA gives a hart, and this is three registers of it. `smcsrind` promises more than is there too, and is listed because Zephyr looks for it.
- **Steps 8 and 9 landed as one change, and Zephyr's IMSIC driver worked the first time it met the model.** A board and its firmware cannot be changed apart: the old images write to a PLIC that is no longer there. So the board, the module and the rebuilt images are one commit. `hello_world`, the synchronization sample and the disk test passed on the first run, with the SSD's firmware and across the real link too. The glue was then checked the other way: with `--wrap` taken out of the module, the disk test prints nothing at all, waiting for an interrupt that is pending in the IMSIC and not enabled. **Measured**, as M8 measured it (Intel Mac, three firmwares, 2 MiB drive, default quantum, three runs): 0.44 s of wall clock and 606.8 ms simulated to the verdict, against 0.44 s and 603.6 ms with the PLIC and the bridge. 69 interrupt messages cross the link, as before, and now every one is a write to the IMSIC's page, of identity 32 or 33. 💡 `docs/models/imsic.md` came with step 8 and not with the docs step, because the address-map tables link every block to its page and are checked.
- **Step 10: the borrowed UART does not hold what it receives, so the adapter does.** The PULPino model asks its serial line for a byte each time the receive register is read, and is told on a second socket only whether one is waiting, which its line status then says until it is told otherwise. So the adapter keeps the queue and tells it again after every byte. `Write` shows in the registers a delta cycle on, from a process of the adapter's own, since a script calls it between runs. ⚠️ For step 11, from reading the same model: its interrupt is added up from events and not worked out from state. Enabling the transmit interrupt while idle raises nothing, the identification register can say two things at once where Zephyr's driver wants the one with priority, and two reads with no yield between leave the line stuck high. Step 11's contract is written to show each, and the fix is a patch.
- **Step 11: the borrowed UART's interrupt was patched, and its line needs an edge.** The PULPino model folded a queue of "set" and "reset" events into its identification register and lost track: enabling an interrupt whose cause was already there raised nothing, which is how every driver starts to transmit. `vpv-peripherals-uart-interrupt-from-state.patch` works the line and the register out from what is true at that moment, with one bit of state for "the transmit register has emptied and firmware has not been told". The devicetree seemed to need nothing new, since Zephyr's driver reads the interrupt with `DT_INST_IRQN`, which `interrupts-extended` serves. ⚠️ That was half the story, and step 13 found the other half: Zephyr's Kconfig cannot see that spelling. ⚠️ The review found what step 12 has to answer. Zephyr's shell makes one pass of its handler for one interrupt. With a received byte and the transmitter both pending, it sees to the byte and returns with the line still high for the transmitter, as a real 16550's would be. An APLIC that delivers by message sends on a rising edge, so no message follows, and the console is dead. Linux's driver asks the APLIC to look again after each handler (a write of the source's number to `setipnum_le`), and Zephyr's does not. So the APLIC has that register, and socpuppet's glue in Zephyr writes it.
- **Step 12: the borrowed APLIC delivers by message, and a few things came with that.** `vpv-peripherals-aplic-msi-mode.patch` is the feature: the message address registers, the MSI form of `target`, a socket for the messages and one thread that sends them, so that neither a wire's method nor a register's writer waits on the road to the hart. Three things the plan did not list came with it. `setipnum_le`, for the level that is still there after a handler (step 11's warning). `clrienum`, which the model had as a register that did nothing. And a source that is set up while its line is already asking is pending at once, so a byte typed during boot is not lost. The adapter holds the model in MSI mode whatever is written, since it has no line to a hart and Zephyr never sets the bit. It brings out 31 sources, ties the model's other 992 inputs low, and answers for the registers of the sources it does not bring out, since a source that asks while its line is low would otherwise ask for ever on a tied input. Its direct mode's line is not brought out, as planned. The contract's tests were written beside the code and checked by mutation: two dozen changes to the patch and the adapter, and each made a test fail. Two reviews then found what mutation could not: tests that could only fail by crashing, a source's mode written a second time sending a second message (which is what Zephyr's `irq_enable` would do), and the message-mode bit cleared by a write of one byte. 🧵 In Python the node's `msi-parent` is not written by hand: `DeviceNode.messages` names the port the messages leave by, and the generator follows it to whatever says it `takes_messages`, the way it follows an address map. It refuses if the sender finds the page at another address than the CPU does, since firmware tells the sender the CPU's. For M20: Linux's binding for an APLIC has two interrupt cells where Zephyr's has three.
- **Step 13: the shell answers, and three things stood between Zephyr's drivers and that.** `host()` has an APLIC on the IO die at `0x100C_0000` with the UART's line at source 16, and a router for what the IO die sends up, the APLIC's messages and with a drive the drive's, so the way back across the link is always there. Zephyr's APLIC and IMSIC drivers then carried the console with three pieces of glue, each in [upstream.md](upstream.md). (1) Nothing in Zephyr sets a source up, so the module's `irq_enable` does: a number from 16 to 31 is a source of the APLIC, given the mode "high level", routed to hart 0 as the identity of its own number, and enabled at both controllers. (2) Zephyr never has the APLIC look at a level again, so the module puts each source's handler behind one of its own that writes `setipnum_le` afterwards. Without it the shell prompts and is then deaf, to a single command and not only to one typed while it prints. (3) Kconfig cannot see `interrupts-extended` at all, and the 16550's Kconfig asks it whether the UART can interrupt, so the first shell image polled and passed every test but the one that counts interrupt messages. A UART's node is now written with `interrupt-parent` and `interrupts` (`DeviceNode.short_interrupts`). The machine timer's binding requires the other spelling, so the generator has both. The shell needs no option in the board's defconfig: with the node right it chooses the interrupt-driven console itself. Checked by taking things out: the UART's line unwired, the call that sets the source up, and the look-again write, each of which fails the exit test. And by putting things in: with Zephyr's assertions on (`CONFIG_ASSERT`) the shell image passes it too, which the glue's first order of doing things would not have, as the review found by reading. Zephyr's 16550 driver has a switch of its own for a controller that only hears edges, and with it in place of the look-again write the exit test passes as well: the module keeps its write, which is for every device and not one driver. Measured with `examples/shell_from_python.py`: three commands answered in 44.3 ms of simulated time, and 817 console interrupts, each a message at the IMSIC's page.

Order: 0, then 1 to 14. Steps 10 and 11 depend on nothing in a or b, so they can come sooner if a step stalls. Step 3's `supervisor_irq` is what step 6's supervisor file drives.

If Zephyr's AIA drivers turn out too thin to carry the disk test, the fallback is one more milestone with the PLIC and the IMSIC side by side, and the reasons in [upstream.md](upstream.md).

What Linux will ask: the supervisor's interrupt file and its CSRs (`siselect`, `sireg`, `stopei`), which its IMSIC driver cannot do without, and `ssaia` in the ISA list. An APLIC domain for supervisor mode. The fix to supervisor interrupts above, which step 3 made, and a `time` CSR that counts.

### M14 — Boot ROM, flash, system controller, watchdog

Until now a CPU has woken up with its firmware already in RAM, because Python put it there. No silicon boots that way. A real CPU's reset vector points into a small **boot ROM**, written when the chip is made, whose code finds the next stage in a **flash**, copies it to RAM and jumps to it. That first stage is often called a ZSBL, a zero-stage bootloader. This settles a decision the plan had left open since M0: the bootchain has a ROM stage. 🎭 Python's loader stays, as the stand-in for it, and is still the quickest way to try an image.

- **ROM and flash, copy and jump.** The ROM does no more than that, and it checks a CRC so that a bad image is a sentence on the console and not a leap into the dark. Checking a signature, which is what makes a boot a secure one, is left open below.
- **a) The ROM, the system controller and the watchdog, on one CPU.** `sp.Rom` is a memory that refuses a write, and `platform.load_elf` fills it with the ROM's own image before its CPU first reads it. The **system controller** is a small block of ours with a register map: which die this is, where to boot from, why the last reset happened, and a register that asks for one. The **watchdog** resets its CPU when firmware stops feeding it. The ROM's code is `firmware/rom`, a little assembly and C built by `firmware/build.sh`, once for each kind of CPU. Exit: `hello_world` on the host with its ROM in front of it, and a host that stops feeding its watchdog starts again and can read why.
- **b) The flash and the image header, on all three CPUs.** `sp.SpiFlash` is VPV-Peripherals' SPI controller and its flash chip behind one adapter, mapped so that the flash reads like memory (execute in place, or XIP), and `platform.load_binary` fills it from a file. An image starts with a header: a magic number, where to load it, where to enter it, how long it is, and a CRC. Exit: M8's exit test with nothing loaded into RAM, a flash with a damaged image refused by name on the console, and M8's exit test still passing the old way.
- **The system controller owns each CPU's reset.** A wire takes one driver, and the host's reset has one already: the link's end on the compute die, since M7. A watchdog wants the same wire. So the link's release and the watchdog's bite both become inputs to the system controller, which drives the CPU. M7's wiring changes, and M7's test that the release comes over the sideband is what holds it.
- **The header is written once, in SystemRDL**, as a register map is: `regs/boot_image.rdl`, from which the C header the ROM reads, the Python module the tool writes with and the table in the docs are generated. A header is a file format and not a block's registers, and it is still a list of named 32-bit words at offsets, which is all the generator needs to know.
- **The host and the manager boot from one flash on the IO die, and the SSD has its own.** The manager boots first, then lets the host go, and the host's ROM reads its image across the link. The system controller's boot register says where in the flash each CPU's image is.

**Decided on 2026-10-11, planning M14.**

- **Both ways to boot stay, and CI runs both.** The full one is reset, ROM, flash, copy, jump. 🎭 The quick one is Python's loader putting the image in RAM, the ROM's stand-in, and it stays the default: `host()` is unchanged. `boot=from_rom` is a description function, as `drive=add_ssd` is, and it is each CPU's own choice: `host(boot=from_rom, manager=partial(add_manager, boot=from_rom), drive=add_ssd)` boots two CPUs from flash and has the SSD's image loaded by hand. M8's exit test runs both ways (step 12). M20 will want the quick one, to boot Linux without paying for the copy.
- **A second APLIC, on the compute die**, at QEMU `virt`'s address `0x0C00_0000`, which is also where every 32-bit socpuppet CPU has its PLIC. The watchdog's interrupt goes to it, and so can any later device with a wire there. A line of the CPU's own was the other way, and would have been a number no real board gives a watchdog. Zephyr's APLIC driver already makes an instance for each node. socpuppet's `irq_enable` glue is what assumed one, and it learns which APLIC has a number from the devicetree.
- **The image header is `regs/boot_image.rdl`** (the bullet above, which first said a C header by hand). ⚠️ The sideband packet, which that bullet took for its pattern, is not written once: its layout is in the C++ and again in `python/socpuppet/ucie.py`, and nothing checks that they agree.
- **The watchdog is borrowed.** VPV-Peripherals' `sifive/aon` is given its behaviour by a patch, an offer of a feature as the APLIC's message mode was, and Zephyr's `wdt_sifive` driver runs on it unchanged, now that the compute die has a controller for its interrupt.
- **The kitchen is cleaned first.** Four refactors, each a commit of its own before step 0, in the table below.
- **Step 0 is a spike**, build-only, half a day.

What planning found:

- **`Memory` has no read-only kind and nothing loads a file into one.** Its page has said "no ROM variant yet" since M0.
- ⚠️ **A ROM cannot tell loading from simulating by asking the kernel.** `Platform.build()` finishes elaboration, so the simulation has in SystemC's eyes started before anything is loaded. A ROM takes a debugger's write until its CPU first reads it and refuses it afterwards, which also means GDB cannot patch a ROM, as it cannot on a board.
- ⚠️ **A ROM must not be a `memory` node in the devicetree.** The generator gives `zephyr,sram` to the lowest memory, and a ROM at a reset vector is below everything.
- **The borrowed flash maps cleanly and loads fussily.** The controller's XIP socket hands a read straight to the chip, with no SPI command in between, so a ROM that reads the flash as memory needs nothing more of it. The chip's own preload takes only a 32-bit ELF or an Intel HEX file and silently ignores a plain binary, and Python does not use it: a debugger's write through the XIP window goes the road a read does and lands in the chip, so `platform.load_binary` fills the flash and nothing is patched. Step 0 checks that. The chip has no read command either, which only a driver that speaks SPI to it would miss (M18).
- ⚠️ **One flash, two CPUs, one address, and one more router.** The host sees the IO die behind a window and the manager does not, so the manager's bus maps the flash where the host sees it, `0x1020_0000`, and each ROM finds its flash in the same place with only the offset of its image differing. That is a choice and not a rule: a platform refuses two addresses only for a port that firmware is told the address of from outside (the root complex's window, which is what M7 ran into) and for a message's destination. What is a rule: a target socket takes one connection, and a bus's mapping is one. So the flash has a small router of its own in front of it, `io.flash_bus`, with the IO die's bus and the manager's as its two inputs. The manager and the host then share one thing, where M7's test says they share nothing, and that test changes to say so.
- **The borrowed watchdog is registers and nothing else.** VPV-Peripherals' `sifive/aon` has the SiFive watchdog's registers with no behaviour behind them, and Zephyr has a driver for that layout (`wdt_sifive`). The first answer is to give the borrowed block its behaviour and offer it. A watchdog of our own is the second.
- **A CPU can be reset twice**, by a patch to DBT-RISE ([upstream.md](upstream.md)). Nothing has reset one in the middle of a run with its devices in whatever state they were left, and what a reset reaches besides the CPU is step a's to decide.
- ⚠️ **Zephyr's `wdt_sifive` driver cannot be built without an interrupt**, and cannot switch its watchdog off. Its binding requires `interrupts`, which is why the watchdog's node needs the short spelling (`short_interrupts`), and its `wdt_disable` only sets the longest period there is, 65535 s. So the model has to take that write, and a bite has to put the count back to zero, or firmware that starts again has no time to set up.
- **Zephyr has a `syscon` driver and no `syscon-reboot`.** Its `sys_arch_reboot` for RISC-V is an empty weak function for a SoC to fill, so asking the system controller for a reset is a dozen lines in socpuppet's SoC.
- ⚠️ **A devicetree node cannot carry a property its binding does not declare.** Zephyr's devicetree reader refuses it. So an APLIC that says which sources are wired on it needs a binding of ours in front of Zephyr's, `"socpuppet,aplic", "riscv,aplic"`, as the root complex has. ✅ Step 6 found the devicetree says it already, and no property was needed.
- **The CRC is zlib's.** Zephyr's `crc32_ieee` and Python's `zlib.crc32` are the same sum, and the ROM's is fifteen lines of C with a table of sixteen.
- **Reset reaches the CPU and nothing else.** The link's end drives the host's CPU, and the 32-bit CPUs' resets have no driver at all. An input nobody drives is tied low by its own model, not by the platform, so the system controller ties its own two.

What the spike found ([boot-spike.md](boot-spike.md)), which the steps below it now assume:

- **Yes to all four questions**, and two went past building. The spike's ROM, 180 bytes built with the SDK's gcc, booted Zephyr on the host with nothing in RAM. Zephyr's shell answered Python on a host given a second APLIC by hand, with the glue taught to find a source's APLIC from the devicetree.
- **The flash is filled by a debugger's write through its window**, and needs no patch for it. ⚠️ A write with time is taken too, so the adapter refuses it. ⚠️ An access past the chip's fixed 256 MB ends the process, so the adapter refuses that before the chip sees it. ⚠️ SCC's SPI sockets are taken as headers: the library they are in has AXI and CHI in it and does not compile here.
- ⚠️ **The ROM's linker script is given to gcc with `-T`.** Through `-Wl,-T` the SDK's gcc ignores it for picolibc's own, in silence.
- **An ELF is all the image tool needs.** Its segments laid out flat are `zephyr.bin` but for the fill between sections.
- ⚠️ **A copy a byte at a time is a microsecond a byte** of simulated time: 12 ms for `hello_world`. The ROM copies words.
- ⚠️ **An APLIC with nothing wired to it** is step 6's to write: the macro that walks `socpuppet,sources` does not compile for a node without the property. ✅ Step 6 dropped the property, and the question with it.

The addresses, which the step that places each block settles, with [address-map.md](address-map.md) generated again:

| Block | Where | In whose view | Why there |
|---|---|---|---|
| `rom` | `0x0000_1000`, 60 KiB | every CPU with `boot=from_rom` | QEMU `virt`'s `mrom` |
| `syscon` | `0x0010_0000` | every CPU | `virt`'s reboot device (`test`) is there |
| `wdt` | `0x0010_1000` | every CPU | beside the system controller |
| compute `aplic` | `0x0C00_0000` | host | `virt`'s APLIC; where every socpuppet CPU has its wired-interrupt controller (the kits' PLIC) |
| `flash.socket` (qspi registers) | IO offset `0x0D_0000` | host | beside the IO die's APLIC |
| `flash.xip` | IO offset `0x20_0000`, 4 MiB → `0x1020_0000` | host **and** the manager's bus at the same address; the SSD's own flash at `0x1020_0000` on its bus | "one flash, two CPUs, one address" |
| images in the IO die's flash | host at `+0`, manager at `+0x20_0000` | | `SystemController(boot_address=)` tells each ROM |

The kitchen, before step 0. Each is one commit, changes no behaviour, and is held by the tests that exist. K4 adds a function, so it is a `/tdd` step.

| # | Change | Held by / where |
|---|---|---|
| K1 | **`host()` in pieces.** `_compute_die(...)`, `_io_die(...)`, `_drive(...)` with one `return Host(...)`; `boards/host.py:242-384` | `test_host_board.py` (checked-in `socpuppet_host.dts` == generated, overlay, reset wiring, interrupt map), M3-M13 exit tests, `docs/address-map.md` unchanged by `lint.py --fix` |
| K2 | **One boot helper for the exit tests.** `tests/python/cast.py`: `HELLO_WORLD` and the other image names as constants (`disk_access.IMAGE`, `manager_firmware.IMAGE`, `ssd_zephyr_firmware.IMAGE` stay where they are and it imports them), and `boot(board, *, host=, manager=, ssd=) -> Transcript` that builds, loads each image through its CPU, and returns the transcript. `test_m8_exit.py:88-101, 200-213`, `test_m13_exit.py`, `test_m7_exit.py`, `examples/full_bootchain.py` use it. Step 12 teaches it the ROM tier | the exit tests themselves |
| K3 | **The root complex's node finds its own `msi-parent`.** `PcieRootComplex.device_node` passes `messages="dma"`; `drive_overlay()` (`host.py:387-436`) stops writing it by hand. The checked-in overlay changes shape (the generator writes `msi-parent` inside the node, `devicetree.py:233-238`, where the overlay had it in the `&io_rc {}` fragment) and not meaning, so it is regenerated and no image is rebuilt. Ticks the to-do at plan.md:1039 | `test_host_board.py:105-127` |
| K4 | **The sideband printed from one place.** `sp.render_sideband(trace)` in `python/socpuppet/ucie.py`, in `transcript.render`'s shape; `examples/io_manager_hello.py`, `chiplet_host_hello.py`, `full_bootchain.py` lose their loops and `die_of`. Ticks plan.md:1037 | `tests/python/test_ucie.py`, `test_examples.py` |

The steps. Step 0 is a spike and step 14 is the docs. Each of the others is one `/tdd` session. A test that passes the first time is checked with a mutation. Every step that changes a board generates its devicetree and [address-map.md](address-map.md) again.

| # | Step | Where the behaviour goes |
|---|---|---|
| M14 0 | ✅ **The boot spike.** Build-only, half a day, four questions: (1) does a debug write through `qspi.xip_sck` → `spi_channel` → `spi_mem` land, and does a timed read come back, in a C++ rig; (2) does the Zephyr SDK's gcc build a bare-metal `start.S` + C + linker script for rv32 and rv64 inside `firmware/build.sh`'s Docker flow on the Intel Mac; (3) does Zephyr's `hello_world` for `socpuppet_host` boot when its `.bin` is poked into RAM and the CPU started there, i.e. is the image position-exact and `.bss`-clean after a copy; (4) does one `socpuppet_host` image build with two `riscv,aplic` nodes and a `sifive,wdt` under the second. Not a `/tdd` step | `spikes/boot/`, which goes when the steps that use it land. The report is [boot-spike.md](boot-spike.md) |
| M14 1 | ✅ **`sp.Rom`.** `RomContract`: reads back what was loaded; a timed write is refused and changes nothing; a debug write is taken before the CPU has touched it and refused after (by transaction and by DMI request); DMI is granted for reading only; an access past the end is an address error. Registry, Python `Rom(size)` with no devicetree node; a board test that a ROM below the RAM does not take `zephyr,sram` | `src/socpuppet/models/rom.h`, `platform/slots.h` (reuse `MemorySlot`), `tests/cpp/contracts/rom_contract.h` + `_test.cpp`, `builtin_components.cpp`, `components.py`, `docs/models/rom.md` (the address-map tables link every block to a page), `test_catalogue.py`, `test_devicetree.py` |

| M14 3 | ✅ **`sp.SystemController`.** `regs/system_controller.rdl`; `core/system_controller_logic.h` unit tests: power-on cause; `hold` high keeps `reset` high and low lets it fall; a bite pulses `reset` and the cause reads `WATCHDOG`; a write to `RESET_REQUEST` pulses it and the cause reads `SOFTWARE`; `DIE` and `BOOT_ADDRESS` read what the block was built with; `RESET_CAUSE` and the two are not writable. SystemC model with one process driving `reset`; platform test that a CPU under it restarts at its reset vector after a request (a program counts its own starts in RAM, `riscv_program.h`); registry; Python with its node (`socpuppet,system-controller`, `syscon`), binding, vendor prefix | `regs/`, `src/socpuppet/core/system_controller_logic.h`, `models/system_controller.h`, `tests/cpp/unit/`, `tests/cpp/platform/system_controller_test.cpp`, `builtin_components.cpp`, `components.py`, `zephyr_module/dts/bindings/`, `docs/models/system-controller.md`, `src/socpuppet/CMakeLists.txt` |

Tests: each CPU's reset is driven by its own system controller and by nothing else; M7's "the release crosses the sideband before anything crosses the mainband" and M8's boot order still hold; the devicetrees of all three boards gain the node and `test_zephyr_module` sees its binding. Held by step 11's test: the firmware calls `sys_reboot` and starts again with the cause `software` | `python/socpuppet/zephyr_module/soc/socpuppet/reboot.c`, `soc/socpuppet/Kconfig`, `soc/socpuppet/CMakeLists.txt` |

M13's shell exit test passes with the host's images rebuilt: two instances of Zephyr's APLIC driver. ⚠️ Not the `socpuppet,sources` property, compatible and binding that were planned: see what step 6 found, below the table | `boards/host.py`, `devicetree.py`, `zephyr_module/drivers/interrupt_controller/imsic_irq.c`, `test_host_board.py`, `test_devicetree.py`, `docs/models/aplic.md` |

Adapter with one process for each line, registry, Python node `sifive,wdt` with `interrupts`; the host wires `wdt.irq -> compute aplic.source17`, `wdt.bite -> syscon.bite`; the kits wire `irq` to the next PLIC source and `bite` to their syscon. upstream.md entry. Mutation checked as M13's APLIC patch was: a dozen single changes to the patch each fail a test | `cmake/patches/`, `cmake/Dependencies.cmake`, `src/socpuppet/CMakeLists.txt` (`vpv_aon`), `models/watchdog.{h,cpp}`, `tests/cpp/contracts/watchdog_contract.h` + `_test.cpp`, `builtin_components.cpp`, `components.py`, boards, generated `.dts`, `docs/upstream.md`, `docs/models/watchdog.md` |

A boot flash of 4 MiB on every board, read at `0x1020_0000`: the IO die's behind `io.boot_flash_bus`, in the IO window for the host and at the same address on the manager's bus, and one of their own for the SSD and the IO manager board. `load_binary` fills one from a test. `test_host_board.py` now says the manager and the host share the flash and nothing else. The images are rebuilt here, with steps 4, 6 and 7's nodes. ⚠️ No devicetree node, which was planned: nothing in firmware reads the flash until M18 | `src/socpuppet/CMakeLists.txt`, `models/spi_flash.{h,cpp}`, `tests/cpp/platform/spi_flash_test.cpp`, `builtin_components.cpp`, `components.py`, `boards/cpu_kit.py`, `boards/host.py`, `boards/ssd.py`, `boards/io_manager.py`, `test_host_board.py`, `docs/models/spi-flash.md` |

The ROM's C header is generated beside it. `socpuppet.elf` is the one ELF reader, for `load_elf` and this | `regs/boot_image_header.rdl`, `python/socpuppet/boot_image.py`, `python/socpuppet/elf.py`, `cli.py`, `tests/python/test_boot_image.py`, `tests/python/test_boot_image_command.py`, `docs/models/boot-image.md` |

Mutations: the flash left empty (the ROM says so and halts), the wrong `boot_address` | `firmware/rom/`, `firmware/build.sh`, `boards/boot.py`, `boards/host.py`, `boards/cpu_kit.py`, `tests/python/test_rom.py`, `tests/python/test_m14_exit.py` |
| M14 11 | ✅ **Exit a's second half: the watchdog resets, and the CPU reads why.** `firmware/watchdog/` (a socpuppet app, for `socpuppet_host` and `socpuppet_ssd`) prints `reset cause: <name>` from the system controller each time it starts. From power-on it has the watchdog interrupt and not reset (`WDT_FLAG_RESET_NONE` with a callback), says so when the interrupt has come, and calls `sys_reboot`. Reset by that (`software`), it has the watchdog reset when it runs out (`WDT_FLAG_RESET_SOC`), feeds it six times, a third of a period apart, and stops. Reset by the watchdog, it says so and is done. Tests, in the ROM tier: the causes are `power-on`, `software`, `watchdog`; the ROM loads the image again before each start; the interrupt comes (through the compute die's APLIC on the host and the PLIC on the SSD); the watchdog does not bite while fed for two periods; it bites one period after the last feed. **A reset reaches the CPU and nothing else**, and nothing more was needed. In the quick tier the image does not come up a second time: see the note below | `firmware/watchdog/`, `firmware/build.sh`, `python/socpuppet/zephyr_module/soc/socpuppet/`, `tests/python/test_m14_exit.py`, `docs/models/system-controller.md` |
| M14 12 | ✅ **Exit b: M8's bootchain with nothing in RAM.** `add_manager(boot=)` and `io_manager(boot=)`, as the host and the SSD have, refused where a script is in the CPU's place. `tests/python/cast.py`'s `boot()` gives each CPU its image where that CPU will find it: in RAM, or, for a CPU with a ROM, the ROM's program in the ROM and the wrapped image in the flash at its system controller's `boot_address`. `host(boot=from_rom, manager=partial(add_manager, boot=from_rom), drive=partial(add_ssd, boot=from_rom))` is three ROMs and two flashes, the IO die's holding the host's image and the manager's. M8's exit test runs both ways, every assertion unchanged. Of its own: nothing is in any CPU's RAM before it runs, and the first thing each CPU says is its ROM naming that CPU's image. `load_elf` of a RAM image through a CPU that starts in a ROM is refused with where the image goes instead. Measured below | `python/socpuppet/boards/manager.py`, `boards/io_manager.py`, `components.py`, `platform.py`, `tests/python/cast.py`, `tests/python/test_m8_exit.py`, `tests/python/test_m14_exit.py`, `tests/python/test_elf.py`, `tests/python/test_io_manager_board.py` |
| M14 13 | ✅ **A bad image is a sentence on the console.** The ROM checks what it copied by its CRC-32 (`crc32.c`, sixteen remainders and two steps a byte, as Zephyr's own does) and the image's `XLEN` against its CPU's, and its trap vector speaks. With the last byte of the program changed in the flash: `rom: hello_world_socpuppet_host: bad CRC: its header says 0x031fc435, and its bytes come to 0x7418f4a3`, and nothing starts. With an image for the other kind of CPU, on the host and on the SSD: `rom: <name> is a 32-bit image, and this is a 64-bit CPU`. With an image to be loaded where there is no memory: `rom: stopped by a trap: mcause 0x7, mepc 0x..., mtval 0x84000000`, and the same vector says so for a program that traps with no handler and no stack of its own. An empty flash was already `rom: no image at 0x10200000` (step 10). `firmware/rom/README.md` has the table | `firmware/rom/` (`main.c`, `crc32.c`, `crc32.h`, `start.S`, `README.md`), `firmware/build.sh`, `tests/python/test_m14_exit.py` |
| M14 14 | ✅ **The show and the docs.** `examples/boot_from_flash.py`: the host boots the watchdog firmware from its ROM and flash, the ROM's reads are counted out of the trace, and the console tells the three starts and why. `boot-your-firmware.md` has "Booting from flash", with the two ways to boot side by side, and "When the watchdog bites". `architecture.md` has the ROM, the flash, the system controller, the watchdog and the second APLIC in its picture, two more contracts in its table, and what was borrowed. `address-map.md`, `development.md`, `dbt-rise-cpu.md` and the README follow. `spikes/boot/` is deleted. Each block's own page was written with its step. Not a `/tdd` step | docs, `examples/boot_from_flash.py`, `tests/python/CMakeLists.txt`, `tests/python/test_examples.py`, `docs/plan.md` |

What the steps found, as they went:

- **Step 1: a ROM is sealed by its bus master's first transaction, and a refused poke says so.** `sp.Rom` is `MemoryStore` behind a socket that answers reads, refuses writes with a command error, and grants direct memory access for reading only, which DBT-RISE honours: it keeps a table for reads and one for writes. A debugger's write is taken until the bus master first comes, by any transaction or a request for direct memory access, and never after. A latch of its own for each ROM is what step 12 wants: the host's ROM can still be loaded while the manager is already running. The review found the gap in the first version: a refused `poke` said nothing was at the address, which sends the reader to check an address that is right. A component can now say why it turned a poke away, and `poke` raises `PermissionError` with the ROM's name where it had `LookupError`. `test_rom.py` is the component's tests, so the file that holds the ROM firmware's addresses to the boards wants another name in step 10.
- **Step 2: `load_binary` is a file read and a poke, and the two loaders share their refusals.** A missing file is refused in one set of words by both, and a piece that nothing takes names its file. ⚠️ For step 10: it takes a path. An image wrapped in memory is bytes in hand, and those go in with `platform.poke`, which was always there.
- ⚠️ **Step 3: a reset is a pulse, and the borrowed CPU could not see a short one.** The system controller pulses its `reset` for 1 µs when the watchdog's line rises or firmware asks. A CPU that asks for its own reset started over, because a store over the bus yields. A CPU asleep in `wfi` started over when bitten, because the reset wakes it. A CPU that was busy did not: it runs up to a quantum ahead of the clock, the whole pulse came and went between two of its looks, and DBT-RISE then took the interruption for the end of the program and stopped the simulation. `cmake/patches/dbt-rise-riscv-reset-pulse.patch` has the reset callback note that the line rose, and the thread act on the note. It is a commit of its own, with a test in `cpu_test.cpp`, before the system controller's. 💡 This is what "a watchdog resets its CPU" always meant, since a CPU that needs its watchdog is a busy one, and it would have shown itself in step 11 as a run that ended early with nothing wrong. Writes to the three registers firmware only reads are refused, as the link's are. A debugger's write is refused too, since the one register that can be written asks for a reset. `boot_address` and `die` are 32 bits, and Python refuses a number that does not fit.
- **Step 4: every CPU's reset is its system controller's, and the boot order did not move.** `add_system_controller()` in `boards/cpu_kit.py` places one at `0x0010_0000` and connects its `reset` to the CPU. The 32-bit kit calls it, and so does the host's compute die, which is the first thing the two have shared but constants. With a manager, the link's end holds the host through the controller's `hold`. The controller adds one delta cycle between the link's line and the CPU's, and the CPU looks at its reset in its third: M7's and M8's order tests pass, with no delta to spare. The die numbers (`COMPUTE_DIE` 0, `IO_DIE` 1) and the two images' places in the IO die's flash (`HOST_IMAGE_OFFSET` 0, `MANAGER_IMAGE_OFFSET` 2 MiB) are side by side in `cpu_kit.py`, with `BOOT_FLASH_BASE` `0x1020_0000`, where step 8 puts the flashes. The SSD is die 0 of a board of one. Three devicetrees gained a `syscon` node and nothing else. The images in `build/firmware` were not built again: an older image runs on a board with one more device. ⚠️ On the IO manager board 🎭 the compute die's script reaches the manager's system controller across the link, as it reaches the manager's UART: that board has the manager's kit on the IO die's one bus.
- ⚠️ **Step 6: the host has two APLICs, and the devicetree already said whose source a number is.** The compute die's is at `0x0C00_0000`, with its messages onto that die's own bus, and nothing wired to it until step 7. The plan and the spike had each APLIC's node list its wired sources in a property of ours, behind a compatible and a binding of ours and a hook in the generator. It was built that way, the images rebuilt and M13's test passed. The design review then asked why: every device's node names its APLIC already. The glue now builds its table of number to APLIC from `DT_FOREACH_STATUS_OKAY_NODE` and each node's interrupt parent, and the property, the second compatible, the binding and the hook are gone before they were committed. Zephyr's shell answers through it, with two instances of Zephyr's APLIC driver. What the review also found is kept as a rule: two APLICs of one CPU with a line at the same source number would be told apart by nothing, and the C would take whichever came last in silence, so `platform.devicetree()` refuses such a platform and names both lines. 💡 The decision to write the list down was made in planning, by reading the old glue and asking what it lacked. Reading the devicetree first would have found it there.
- **Step 7: the borrowed block has a watchdog now, and the first version of it could hang a simulation.** `vpv-peripherals-aon-watchdog.patch` is the feature: a count worked out on demand and one event for the moment it runs out, so that a watchdog that is fed costs nothing but the feeds, the key before every write, the feed, `zerocmp`, the interrupt bit and a new optional output for it, a bite that is a pulse of `rst_o` one tick long, and the compare value out of reset as `0xFFFF` where the model had zero. The adapter binds the block's two outputs straight to its own `bite` and `irq`, since the patch has the block write each from one process. Every CPU has one at `0x0010_1000`, biting its system controller: source 17 of the compute die's APLIC on the host, and the source after the board's own at a kit's PLIC. ⚠️ The patch was drafted before the contract ran, passed it whole the first time, and was then found wanting three ways. Mutation found four lines no test asked for. The test review found that four tests of "does not bite" looked at the line once, long after a bite one tick long would have come and gone, so could not fail: the contract now counts the line's rises. And the design review found by reading what neither had: a compare value of zero with `zerocmp` rescheduled itself in no time for ever, a watchdog started again inside its own bite's pulse never bit again, which is where a CPU that has just been reset is, and the reset button and the bite each reset half of the watchdog. All three are contract items now. 💡 What the watchdog does not do is on its page: it goes by the simulation's clock and not by how far ahead its CPU is, which the machine timer does count in.
- **Step 8: the borrowed flash needed no patch, and three things kept from it.** `sp.SpiFlash` is the borrowed controller, channel and chip behind a window of the adapter's own, which refuses a write, ends where the flash does, and clears the hint that direct memory access is worth asking for. Each has an entry in [upstream.md](upstream.md): the borrowed window takes a write, the chip ends the process at an access past its 256 MiB, and the hint it passes on is one it cannot honour. Every board has a boot flash at `0x1020_0000`. The IO die's is shared, through `io.boot_flash_bus`, since a socket takes one connection: the IO bus maps that router into the compute die's window, and the manager's bus maps a second input of it at the same address. A script in the manager's place reaches it too, since `host()` cannot tell a script from a CPU. ⚠️ The flash has no devicetree node, where the plan had one: no firmware reads it yet, and a node nobody has built a driver against is a guess. M18's driver brings its node. The helper that every adapter reaches a borrowed model's socket through was called `RegistersOf`, and the flash is the first to use it for something that is not registers: it is `BorrowedSocket` now. The images were built again, and the whole suite passes with them required.
- **Step 9: the header is `boot_image_header`, 64 bytes, and Python packs it by the generated names.** Eight words and a name of 32 bytes, so that a refusal can say `disk_access_socpuppet_host`, which 16 would have cut. The map is called for the header and not for the image, as the review asked: C then has `BOOT_IMAGE_HEADER_SIZE`, the header's 64, beside `BOOT_IMAGE_HEADER_LENGTH`, the word that says how long the program is. The first version packed the eight words with one `struct` format, in an order written out by hand, under a docstring that said the two sides could not disagree. Each word now goes where its generated name says. ⚠️ The CRC is of the program's bytes and not of the header: a damaged load address or entry is not caught. The page says so. Covering the header is a small change to make before anything outside socpuppet writes these.
- **Step 10: the host boots from its ROM, and it worked the first time it was run.** `firmware/rom` is 660 bytes for the 64-bit CPU and 550 for the 32-bit: a start in assembly that sets a stack up at the top of RAM, and C that reads `BOOT_ADDRESS` from the system controller, checks the header's magic number, copies the program a register's width at a time, says `rom: hello_world_socpuppet_host: 12104 bytes to 0x80000000, jumping to 0x80000000` and jumps. `firmware/build.sh` builds it first, with the SDK's gcc and a header for each kind of board (`board_rv64.h`, `board_rv32.h`), which `test_rom_firmware.py` holds to the boards with the linker script. `boot=from_rom` (`socpuppet.boards.boot`) puts an `sp.Rom` at `0x1000` and starts the CPU there, on `host()`, `ssd()` and the kit. The devicetree is the same either way, which is why one image boots both ways. `tests/python/test_m14_exit.py`: the host greets with nothing in RAM beforehand, the ROM's line comes first, every byte of the program is read across the link before the ROM speaks, an empty flash is `rom: no image at 0x10200000`, and the SSD's 32-bit CPU boots the same way. **Measured**: `hello_world` greets at 2.4 ms of simulated time from flash, against 1 ms loaded by Python. (Step 13's CRC check makes that 25.6 ms.) The reviews found what a first run does not: the two images tried were whole numbers of words long, so the copy's last loop had never run, and a name of all 32 bytes had never been printed. A made-up image of 291 bytes with such a name holds both now, and each loop taken out fails it. ⚠️ Three things are for later steps. A trap in the ROM stops the CPU in silence, which step 13 gives a sentence. `load_elf` of a Zephyr image on a board with a ROM is refused in words that send the reader the wrong way, to the linker, and wants a refusal of its own. And the CRC is not checked yet: step 13 brings it with its test.
- **Step 11: the firmware is reset twice and reads why, and the reset needed to reach nothing but the CPU.** `firmware/watchdog` is Zephyr's own API from end to end: `wdt_install_timeout`, `wdt_setup` and `wdt_feed` on Zephyr's `sifive,wdt` driver, `syscon_read_reg` for `RESET_CAUSE`, and `sys_reboot`, whose last step Zephyr leaves to a SoC and ours now fills in (`reboot.c`, step 5, folded in here). The host's console, in milliseconds: the ROM's line at 1.5, `reset cause: power-on` at 2.8, `watchdog: ran out, and interrupted` at 33.1, the ROM again at 34.6, `software` at 35.9, six feeds from 66.2 to 218.0, the ROM a third time at 309.4, which is one period of 90 ms and the ROM's own 1.5 after the last feed, and `watchdog` at 310.9. The SSD's 32-bit CPU tells the same story with a PLIC. **What a reset reaches** was the open question, and the answer the test gave is: the CPU, and that is enough. The first start leaves the watchdog running in its interrupting mode, an interrupt enabled at the APLIC and an identity enabled at the IMSIC, and the second start finds all of that as it was left and comes up, because Zephyr's drivers set up what they use and the ROM uses none of it. So `syscon.reset` fans out to nothing, and no model gained a `reset` input. ⚠️ That is firmware being careful and not the hardware being reset: a firmware that trusted a peripheral to be at its reset value after a watchdog bite would be wrong here, as it would be on many real chips whose watchdog resets the core alone. 🎓 **The quick tier does not survive its first reset, and that is the lesson of the ROM.** With the image put in RAM by Python and no ROM, the same firmware says `power-on`, is interrupted, asks for its reset, starts again at the reset vector on a RAM nobody reloaded, and takes a `Store/AMO access fault` at address 0 before it can say why it started: its `.data` holds what the first run left there, and not what the ELF file says. A reset is survivable only when something puts the image back, which is what a boot ROM and a flash are for. No test holds this, since it is what the stand-in leaves out and not a behaviour to keep. The first test of the interrupt came from a mutation: with the watchdog's source number changed on the board, the tests of the reset still passed, because a watchdog told to reset never interrupts. The firmware's first start was added to take that interrupt. The reviews then found the fed stretch was exactly one period long, so a watchdog deaf to feeding would have bitten 1 ms before the last feed and nearly passed; it is two periods now, and the firmware with its `wdt_feed` taken out fails. They also had the tolerance on the bite's time cut from 15 ms to 0.2 ms by taking the ROM's measured time off, and `reboot.c` built only where the devicetree has a system controller.
- **Step 12: all three CPUs boot from their ROMs, and it costs six milliseconds (before step 13 has them check what they loaded).** M8's bootchain passed in the ROM tier the first time `boot()` knew where to put the images, with no change to a board, a ROM or a firmware: the manager's ROM finds its image 2 MiB into the IO die's flash and brings the link up, the host's ROM is then let go and copies its 58 KiB from the same flash across the link, and the SSD's ROM reads its own. **Measured**, three runs each, the same every time in simulated time: the host's disk test gives its verdict at 607.3 ms with the images loaded by Python and at 613.5 ms from the ROMs, and the run takes 0.50 s of wall time against 0.55 s. The first lines move more: the manager speaks at 1.3 ms or 2.7 ms, the SSD at 3.2 ms or 3.8 ms, and the host, whose first line was Zephyr's at 142.4 ms, now has its ROM's at 12.4 ms. So the quick way saves a tenth of the wall time on this boot, and the difference will be the copy: a kernel of megabytes, a word at a time across a link, is what M20 will want to skip. 💡 What a test that boots this way loads is two things for each CPU, neither into RAM, and `cast.py`'s `_give` is those two calls. It stayed in the tests: a board function that loaded files would have to know where a firmware build puts them. The refusal that step 10's review asked for is here: `load_elf` of an image linked for RAM, through a CPU that starts in a ROM, used to say to link it at `0x1000`, and now says the image goes in the flash, with the call. A CPU learns what is at its reset vector from the platform (`refuse_image(..., what_is_at=)`), so a platform put together by hand gets the same sentence as a board. The reviews found the test of the ROMs' first lines waited for the firmwares to speak and did not check that they had, so it stops at the three first lines now and says so; and that three comments told the reader to fill the flash at its first byte, which is not where the manager's ROM looks.
- **Step 13: the ROM says what is wrong with an image, and checking one costs more than copying it.** Four sentences, each from one test that failed first: until this step an image with a changed byte booted, and one for the wrong kind of CPU, or with nowhere to go, stopped the CPU with nothing said. The ROM is 1310 bytes for the 64-bit CPU and 1102 for the 32-bit, twice what it was. ⚠️ **The CRC changed step 12's numbers.** Half a byte at a time is about nineteen instructions a byte, and a socpuppet CPU is given 100 ns for each, so `hello_world`'s 12 KiB are 23 ms of checking after 1.5 ms of copying: it greets at 25.6 ms from flash, where step 10 measured 2.4 ms, against 1 ms loaded by Python. M8's bootchain from three ROMs gives its verdict at 746.5 ms of simulated time and not 613.5 ms, against 607.3 ms the quick way, in about 0.7 s of wall time against 0.5 s. The manager now speaks at 26.2 ms, so the link is up at 29 ms and not 6 ms, and the host's ROM, with 58 KiB to check, speaks at 146 ms. That is what a real ROM is like, and it is the honest price of the full tier: the quick tier is a quarter faster on this boot, and would be far more so on a kernel. A table of 256 would halve it for a kilobyte of ROM, and was left for whoever needs it (the ROM's README says so). The sum is of the copy in RAM and not of the flash, because the copy is what runs, and the flash is not read twice across the link. 🎓 **The trap vector serves more than the ROM.** A program the ROM has jumped to keeps the ROM's vector until it sets its own, so a firmware that traps in its first instructions gets a sentence too, with `mepc` in RAM and not in the ROM. The handler starts on a new stack for that case, and a test holds it with a program of two instructions, of which the first loses the stack and the second is all zeros. The reviews found the eight digits of a CRC were held only because `hello_world`'s happens to start with a zero, so a test now writes a CRC of 1 into a header; that the handler reached its `halt` by falling through a `nop` the assembler had put there for alignment; and that the bound on the watchdog's bite, 0.2 ms, had passed by two microseconds and failed when the ROM's time changed: a console's times are good to a quantum each, and the bound is three of them now. **Not done**: the ROM does not check that an image fits below its own stack (the README says so), and has no second place to look for an image.

Order: K1 to K4, then 0, then 1, 2, 3, 4, 6, 7, 8, 9, 10, 11, 12, 13, 14, with 5 folded into 11 unless it wants a step of its own. Steps 1 and 2 (the ROM, loading) and 8 and 9 (the flash, the header) depend on nothing in 3 to 7 (reset, the APLIC, the watchdog), so either chain can run while the other stalls. 10 needs 1, 2, 3, 8 and 9. 11 needs 4, 6, 7 and 10. 12 needs 10 and K2.

What Linux will ask: the system controller as a `syscon` with `syscon-reboot` on top, so that `reboot` works. The boot ROM's next stage becomes OpenSBI, and the image after it a kernel and its devicetree. The quick way to boot is `load_elf` for OpenSBI's `fw_jump` at the reset vector and `load_binary` for the kernel and its devicetree, since the check that an image starts where its CPU does applies to the one loaded through the CPU. OpenSBI wants the devicetree's address in `a1`: the ROM passes nothing today, and the header has room for an argument when a test asks. The compute die's APLIC in Linux's binding, which has two cells where Zephyr's has three, and the flash as `jedec,spi-nor`.

### M21 — Twister and ztest: Zephyr's own tests on the puppet

Until now Zephyr has been the firmware and not the examiner. Two of its own tests had ever run on a socpuppet CPU, `disk_access` and `flash/common`, both wrapped by hand, their verdicts read by matching substrings in pytest, and nothing from Zephyr's `tests/kernel`, `tests/arch/riscv`, `tests/drivers/watchdog`, `tests/subsys` or `tests/lib` had ever been built for a socpuppet board. Zephyr's tree holds hundreds of suites that test what socpuppet models, the CPU, the timer, the interrupt controllers, the UART, the watchdog and a reset, and **twister**, Zephyr's test runner, is how Zephyr runs them on a thousand boards. **ztest** is Zephyr's test framework, the thing that prints `PASS - [suite.case]` and `PROJECT EXECUTION SUCCESSFUL`, and how twister judges a run.

- **Twister runs socpuppet as it runs QEMU.** Twister has a custom-emulator mode: a board's platform file names a program, its `board.cmake` has a `run_custom` target that calls it, twister reads the program's standard output a line at a time and judges it. socpuppet meets that with two files per board in the Zephyr module and one command, `socpuppet run <image> --board <board> [--shield <shield>]`, which describes the board, gives the CPU its image and prints the console as the simulation runs. A Zephyr developer with a workspace then runs `twister -p socpuppet_host -T tests/kernel` as they would for `qemu_riscv64`, and that is the point: a socpuppet board is a board.
- **Zephyr's own suites become a board-compliance suite in CI**, thousands of test cases nobody at socpuppet had to write. The spike's first run of fourteen of them, 2581 cases across the three boards, found two bugs of ours in how a CPU is woken and kept on time, and none in the firmware (below).
- **ztest unit-tests socpuppet's own drivers and firmware from inside Zephyr.** The module's seven drivers and the SSD firmware's FTL and admin path are tested today only end to end through the exit tests. A ztest suite beside each driver, built for the board it lives on and run by twister, tests it one call at a time.
- **A ztest verdict is read in one place.** `socpuppet.ztest` parses a console into suites, cases and a verdict, and the pytest tests that copy case names and `PROJECT EXECUTION SUCCESSFUL` by hand read a report instead.
- **The CPU boots from its ROM under twister.** A test that resets its CPU, as Zephyr's watchdog test does three times, carries on from memory the reset does not touch, and only a ROM reloads the image. `socpuppet run` boots from the ROM and the flash by default, and `--boot ram` is the quick tier.
- **What twister does not replace**: pytest with `Platform` for anything with more than one CPU, a trace, a Python stand-in or a wire poked from Python, which is M6 to M14's exit tests. Twister has one device under test and one console. `firmware/build.sh` stays the image builder for those tests.

**Decided on 2026-10-11, planning M21.**

- **It starts with a spike, and the spike is done.** Everything else in M21 arranges things that exist. Whether twister would take socpuppet as an emulator, and what Zephyr's suites would say of the models, nobody knew. Step 0 found out. The report is [twister-spike.md](twister-spike.md); its code was in `spikes/twister/`, and went when step 10 landed, the steps before it having rebuilt it.
- **The runner is a command of the package, `socpuppet run`**, not a script of the tests', because `board.cmake` in the module names it and the module ships in the wheel.
- **The twister files go in the module.** `<board>.yaml` and `board.cmake` beside each board's `board.yml`. The spike kept them in a copy of the module so that nothing in `python/` named a script in `spikes/`; the steps put them where they belong.
- **The board-compliance run is one ubuntu job.** (A `macos-15` leg was added on 2026-10-12, see step 6's note.) Twister needs the SDK, Zephyr, its venv and the simulator in one place, so the job is `firmware/build.sh`'s environment plus a build of socpuppet, which CI's `firmware` and `test` jobs today keep apart. The spike measured a minute of twister a scenario, nearly all of it CMake and the compiler: fifteen suites in 25 minutes for the host and 37 for the two 32-bit boards, on an Intel Mac through Docker. A Linux runner with no bind mount should be faster, and by how much is step 6's to measure.
- **Twister runs in CI, and on demand here; not in the inner loop.** Decided on 2026-10-11 after the spike's first pass. An image is built once and run natively with `socpuppet run` when a test is being worked on, which takes a second or two; twister is for the whole list, in CI on every push and on this machine when a change touches a board, a driver or the CPU.
- **On an Intel Mac it runs from a Docker volume, in one invocation, with no ccache.** The spike's second pass timed it (its question 7): the compiler reading Zephyr and the SDK through Docker's file sharing was the whole cost, a Docker volume holding them makes the same scenarios ten times faster (eight in 35 s against 328 s), one invocation for every suite saves 40% over one a suite, `--no-clean` halves a rerun but is for retrying a failure and not for a judgment, and ccache costs more than it saves once the files are local. `firmware/twister.sh` inherits the recipe: a volume `socpuppet-twister` with Zephyr, the SDK, a venv and the output, copied again when the versions change, and only verdicts and logs brought back.
- **The CI job is estimated at a quarter of an hour, and the estimate is the first thing step 6 checks.** At four jobs, a GitHub runner's share, the spike's rate was eight seconds a scenario, so sixteen minutes for the 126 scenarios the spike ran, before fetching or restoring the SDK and Zephyr (1.6 GB) and building socpuppet. Measured on a laptop, not a runner.
- **Excluded suites go in a quarantine list**, twister's own YAML of scenarios to skip with a comment each that it copies into its reports, so that the watchdog suite's exclusion and its reason travel with the results.
- **The ROM is a build product and stays one.** `socpuppet run --boot rom` finds it in `SOCPUPPET_FIRMWARE_DIR`, as the tests do. Shipping the two ROM images in the wheel, so that a user with no `firmware/build.sh` can boot from flash, is left open: the images are a kilobyte each and change when `firmware/rom` does.

What the spike found ([twister-spike.md](twister-spike.md)), which the steps below assume:

- ✅ **Twister takes socpuppet as an emulator with nothing patched.** Fifteen suites built and ran on all three boards through `run_custom`: 80 of 126 scenarios passed, 2413 test cases of 2581. A ztest suite kept beside the drivers was found and run the same way, and passed on all three.
- ⚠️ **A sleeping CPU misses an interrupt line that is already high.** Zephyr's `tests/kernel/common` and two scenarios of `tests/kernel/sched/schedule_api` stopped in the middle of one test each with the simulation out of work: the CPU asleep in `wfi`, `mtimecmp` behind `mtime`, the timer's line high, and nothing pending. Zephyr's timer driver writes a compare it computed, which can be in the past, and the borrowed CLINT raises its line at once for one; DBT-RISE's `core_complex` tells the core about an interrupt line only when the line *changes* (`clint_irq_cb` looks at `event()`), and a core that goes to sleep after the change never hears of it. Poking a compare one millisecond ahead woke the CPU and both tests passed. It is a bug in socpuppet's CPU path that no socpuppet test had found, and the kernel's own tests found it in their first run. Step 1 fixes it test-first in the bus-master contract and a DBT-RISE patch ([upstream.md](upstream.md)). ⚠️ Step 1 found that this explanation stops one link short: the rise is heard, and it is the firmware that then clears the bit. See what the steps found, below the table.
- ⚠️ **The 32-bit boards wake late, by more than a quantum, and nobody knows why yet.** On `socpuppet_ssd` and `socpuppet_iomgr`, and not on the host, every test that measures a wait fails by a little: `timer_api` ("expected wakeup at 87, got 91"), `sleep`, `context` ("idled for 113 ticks, expected 100"), one case of `workq`, and the four time-slicing scenarios of the scheduler. A tick is 100 µs on every board, which is the default quantum. Run by hand, the idle test is 13 ticks late at a quantum of 100 µs and passes at 10 µs and at 1 µs. Thirteen quanta is not the "up to one quantum late" this plan has promised since M3, and the host passes with the same timer, tick and quantum. One difference is known and untested: a 32-bit CPU writes `mtimecmp` in three bus accesses and reads `mtime` in two, where a 64-bit one does each in one. Step 2 finds the cause before anything is fixed. ✅ It was the three writes, and the host has a third of the same fault: see what the steps found, below the table.
- ⚠️ **Zephyr's watchdog test wants a callback before a SoC reset, and the SiFive watchdog has none.** `wdt_basic_api` resets the CPU three times and came back each time from the ROM (the quick tier came back once and not twice). Its third case installs a callback with `WDT_FLAG_RESET_SOC` and expects the callback to have run before the reset; Zephyr's `wdt_sifive` says in a comment it "cannot callback" in that mode and sets the watchdog up to reset without interrupting, which is what the model does. The test provides for a driver that returns `-ENOTSUP` here, and the driver does not. That is an entry for [upstream.md](upstream.md) under Zephyr, not a model bug, and the suite is excluded until it lands.
- **The rest passed as they stand.** On every board: `thread_apis` (42 cases), the stack sentinel, the shell (41), libc but for two scenarios, both samples. On the host besides: `kernel.timer`, `workqueue`, `sleep`, `context`, five of the scheduler's seven scenarios, and all four `arch/riscv/fatal` cases with their register dumps. On the 32-bit boards besides: `kernel.common`, all eleven scenarios, which is the suite the host hangs in. Two libc scenarios fail to build for want of the FatFs module, which `firmware/build.sh` does not fetch, and `arch/riscv/fatal` is a 64-bit suite that matches 64-bit register dumps, so it is the host's alone.
- **PMP is in the model and user mode is not on the core.** DBT-RISE's `rv*imac_mp` cores, socpuppet's default, already stack a PMP layer; the hart behind them is machine-mode only. `CONFIG_USERSPACE` wants the `_mu` hart with the PMP layer, a change to socpuppet's core creator and not a model, and is a to-do.
- ⚠️ **The emulator has to exit 0 at its cap.** Twister counts a non-zero exit as a failed configuration even when the console harness passed, if the process ended before twister killed it; a sample never prints ztest's verdict, so the runner reaches its cap first.
- ⚠️ **Twister's `exec` must be on twister's own `PATH`**, or every instance is silently built and not run.

The steps, each one `/tdd` session unless marked. Step 0 is done.

| # | Step | Where the behaviour goes |
|---|---|---|
| M21 0 | ✅ **The spike.** Six questions, fifteen suites, three boards. Not a `/tdd` step | `spikes/twister/`, [twister-spike.md](twister-spike.md) |
| M21 1 | ✅ **A CPU cannot sleep through an interrupt whose line is high.** ⚠️ Not the cause the spike gave, so not the patch that was planned: see what step 1 found, below the table. `cpu_test.cpp`: a program sleeps until its timer line is high, clears the interrupt's bit in `mip` as Zephyr's interrupt entry does, sleeps again and runs on; the same for the external interrupt; each on the core with supervisor mode and the one without. The fix is `cmake/patches/dbt-rise-riscv-mip-read-only.patch`, with its entry in [upstream.md](upstream.md). Bus-master contract item: a master told to wait for an interrupt whose line is already high carries on, which both masters did already and a mutation of each fails. Then the machine-timer contract's item, where the cause is: a writer ahead of the clock has its own time back from a write of the compare value. The fix is `cmake/patches/vpv-peripherals-aclint-write-ahead.patch`, with its entry in [upstream.md](upstream.md). Checked with the spike's images: `context`, `sleep`, `timer_api`, `workq` and every scenario of `schedule_api`, 26 scenarios of 26 on the two boards, where 8 passed. The quantum was doing what it says, so there is no decision to bring back | `tests/cpp/platform/cpu_test.cpp`, `tests/cpp/support/riscv_program.h`, `tests/cpp/contracts/machine_timer_contract.h`, `tests/cpp/support/bus_driver.h`, `cmake/patches/`, `cmake/Dependencies.cmake`, `docs/upstream.md`, `docs/models/machine-timer.md`, `examples/shell_from_python.py` |

Tests: the three platform files validate against twister's schema and name their board and `socpuppet`; the `run` target of a stand-in for Zephyr's build runs the command, on the image, after the image is built; the devicetrees are generated again and the images rebuilt. ⚠️ Zephyr's documented `run_custom` does not build the image first: see what step 4 found, below the table | `zephyr_module/boards/socpuppet/`, `components.py`, `devicetree.py`, `tests/python/test_zephyr_module.py`, `tests/python/test_devicetree.py`, `docs/upstream.md`, `docs/models/watchdog.md` |

A `twister` job in `ci.yml` on ubuntu-24.04: restore or fetch the SDK, Zephyr and the venv (cached as `firmware` caches images, keyed on the two version strings), build socpuppet as `test` does, run, upload `twister.json` and the logs, and fail on a failed scenario. ⚠️ First measure it: the spike's sixteen-minute estimate is from a laptop. If the job is much longer, the list is cut to what earns its place before anything else is tuned; if `zephyrproject-rtos/action-zephyr-setup` would set the runner up better than our caching, it wants a `west.yml`, which changes how a user builds the firmware too and is a decision to bring back. Not a `/tdd` step | docs |

What the steps found, as they went:

- ⚠️ **Step 1: the line's rise was heard, and the firmware then un-heard it.** The spike read the hang as a core that is told of a line only when it changes, and so misses one that is already high when it goes to sleep. That is half of it. Run again with a print at each place the pending bit can change, the last timer interrupt before the hang goes: the line rises, the core sets bit 7 of `mip`, **Zephyr writes `mip` with that bit cleared**, and the next `wfi` finds nothing pending. Zephyr's interrupt entry clears the bit of whichever interrupt it is about to handle (`soc_irq.S`: `csrrc t1, mip, t0`). 🎓 On a real hart that write does nothing: the timer's bit and the external interrupt's are read-only there, each saying what its line says, and firmware ends the interrupt at the timer or the interrupt controller. DBT-RISE's harts took the write. Most of the time nothing came of it, because the handler's new compare value dropped the line and the next rise set the bit again. When the compare value Zephyr had worked out was already in the past, the line never fell, there was no change for the core to hear of, and the bit stayed clear for good. `cmake/patches/dbt-rise-riscv-mip-read-only.patch` takes bits 7 and 11 out of what a write to `mip` may change, and `wfi` itself needed nothing: it sleeps only while nothing enabled is pending, which the new contract item holds for both masters. Four tests, each seen to fail first. **Checked with the spike's own images, run natively**: all nine scenarios of `kernel.common` and all seven of `schedule_api` give their verdict on the host, where none and five did. 💡 Two things the reviews found. The first draft of the upstream entry said the hart without supervisor mode did not have this, from the name of its mask, `clint_custom_irq_mask`, which is not the custom bits; the design review ran the test on that core and it failed, so the patch covers both harts. And that leaves why the 32-bit boards passed, which is by reading and not run: a 32-bit Zephyr writes the compare value in three halves, the high one to all ones first, so the line falls and a value in the past raises it again. ⚠️ **For step 2**: that same three-part write is the suspect there. **Not done**: bit 3, machine mode's software interrupt, is read-only in the specification too and still writable here, since no line of socpuppet's reaches it; and bit 9 wants the OR of what software wrote and what the line says ([upstream.md](upstream.md)).
- **Step 2: every write of the compare value cost the CPU as much time as it was ahead by, and a 32-bit CPU writes it three times.** 🎓 A CPU model runs ahead of the simulation's clock, by up to a quantum, and says how far ahead it is in the delay of each bus access. The borrowed timer takes a write of `mtimecmp` by waiting that delay out, so that the clock catches up with the writer, which is a fair way to do it. It then handed the delay back as it came, where it was spent and should have been zero, and DBT-RISE took a clock that had moved on and a delay that still said the same for a write that took that long. The CPU's time jumped by its lead with nothing executed, and it was still as far ahead as before, so the next write cost the same again. **Measured before anything was changed**, in a rig the size of a test: a CPU 19.7 µs ahead reads the count, sets the compare value and reads the count again. On the 32-bit CPU the count moved on by 595 ticks across four instructions, which is three times 197 and the four that are due, and on the 64-bit CPU by 199 across two. So it was never a fault of the 32-bit boards: the host loses a third as much, up to a tick each time where they lose up to three. That a tick is within what Zephyr's tests allow, and three are not, is what their verdicts suggest and was not looked into further. Firmware reads the count, adds its wait and sets the compare value, so what goes missing in between is a wait that ends late, which is every failure the spike listed. The fix is one line, `d = SC_ZERO_TIME` after the wait, in `cmake/patches/vpv-peripherals-aclint-write-ahead.patch`: it is what the same project's UART and GPIO models do, and what SystemC's own socket does. The machine-timer contract holds it where the cause is, with a writer 2 µs ahead whose write came back at 9 µs of its own time and now comes back at 7 µs. **Checked with the spike's images, run natively** on `socpuppet_ssd` and `socpuppet_iomgr`: `context`, `sleep`, `timer_api`, `workq` and all seven scenarios of `schedule_api`, 26 scenarios of 26, where 8 passed before. ✅ The quantum was doing what it says all along, a CPU does see an interrupt up to one quantum late and no more, and the decision this step might have had to bring back, a smaller quantum or a longer tick for the 32-bit boards, does not arise. ⚠️ One thing broke, and it was a test's own: `examples/shell_from_python.py` waited for the console to end with the shell's prompt, which it does only for the three bytes' time before the shell switches its colour back, and a step of the simulation had always happened to fall there. With things a few microseconds earlier none did. It now waits for the answer and a prompt after it, in a commit of its own before the fix. **Not done**: the callback for `msip` has the same two lines and is left, since nothing here writes it.
- **After steps 1 and 2, every ztest suite the spike built gives its verdict, and all but the watchdog's pass.** The 118 images the spike left were each run natively, from the ROM, as `socpuppet run` will run them: 97 say `PROJECT EXECUTION SUCCESSFUL` (36 on the host, 26 on the SSD, 35 on the IO manager), the three of `drivers.watchdog` say `FAILED`, which is Zephyr's test and Zephyr's driver disagreeing and is step 6's first quarantine entry, and the other eighteen are the samples and `arch/riscv/fatal`, which have no ztest verdict and are judged by twister from the console. ⚠️ That is the runner on this machine and not twister: twister's own judgment of all 126 scenarios, with the two that do not build, is step 6's to run, and the Linux tree in `build/linux` has to fetch its two patched dependencies again first, since the list of patches changed.
- **Step 3: the runner is a command, and it says how a run ended.** `socpuppet run` describes the board with as little around its CPU as the board allows (🎭 an idle host for the SSD, a compute die that makes one round trip for the IO manager, the stand-in drive for the host's shield), gives the CPU its image, and prints each console line as it becomes whole. Twenty tests, each seen to fail first, eight of them on images from `firmware/build.sh` and twelve on nothing but a file the test wrote. **Decided on the way, with the maintainer**: a verdict of `PROJECT EXECUTION FAILED` ends the command with a failing status and no more words, since the console has said which test; the spike exited 0 whatever the verdict. A run that reaches its cap, or whose simulation runs out of things to do, still exits 0 with one line on standard error that says which, because a sample never gives a verdict and twister counts a failing status as a failed configuration. And an image of the wrong word size is refused before anything is built, for `--boot ram` as well, where no ROM would have seen it. 💡 **What the tests needed that the firmware did not have**: nothing `firmware/build.sh` builds fails its tests, and nothing it builds goes quiet (Zephyr's idle thread keeps a timer armed, so `hello_world` runs to the cap, and the disk test without its drive reads an address nothing answers and waits). So `tests/python/riscv_program.py` is a dozen lines of assembler, enough for a program that prints a text through the UART and then sleeps or spins, and three endings of a run are tested with no toolchain and no skip. ⚠️ **One thing was wrong outside the runner**: `Platform.run_until` asked the kernel to run for no time when its deadline fell exactly where the simulation already was, and SystemC answers that with a warning on standard output, which twister would read as a line of the console. It is fixed in a commit of its own, with a test in `test_scripts.py`.
