The design review then had the spike's loop, a look at the console every millisecond, give way to `Transcript.run_until`, which was measured first: 0.45 s for the disk test either way. So the time in the "nothing left to do" line is the moment the board went quiet and not the end of a slice. ⚠️ **For step 4**: the ROM's program is looked for in `SOCPUPPET_FIRMWARE_DIR`, or in `build/firmware` under the directory the command is run from, and ninja runs it from a build directory, so `board.cmake` or twister's environment has to say where. ⚠️ **For step 6**: a model's own warning, such as the router's for an address nothing answers, goes to standard output as SCC logs it, between the console's lines. Twister's harnesses matched round the ROM's first line in the spike and would match round these, but a suite that compares the whole console would not. **Not done**: the ROM images in the wheel, which stays open.
- **Step 4: a board is two more files, and the run target that Zephyr's documentation gives does not build what it runs.** Each board has `<board>.yaml`, the platform file twister reads, and a `board.cmake` of one line that includes `boards/socpuppet/run_with_socpuppet.cmake`, where the `run_custom` target is written once. 🎓 A node in a devicetree can now take an alias as it fills a `chosen` role (`DeviceNode.aliases`), and the watchdog takes `watchdog0`, so the spike's overlays are not needed: of two watchdogs on one CPU's bus, the one at the lower address has the name. The platform files say `exec: socpuppet`, the RAM's size for `ram` and for `flash` because an image runs where it is loaded, and `supported: [watchdog]`, each held to the board's own files by a test. **Not there, on purpose**: the spike's `testing: timeout_multiplier: 2`, which no test asks for, and which step 6's run will ask for if it is needed. ⚠️ **Found by the design review**: the first `board.cmake` had `DEPENDS ${logical_target_for_zephyr_elf}`, as Zephyr's documentation of a custom emulator has, and a test that the image is built first, which passed. In a real build the variable is empty: Zephyr reads `board.cmake` before its own directory has named the image's target. The test's stand-in for Zephyr's build had set the variable first. With the stand-in in Zephyr's order the test failed, and the target now adds its dependency at the end of the configuration (`cmake_language(DEFER ...)`), which a real build's `build.ninja` shows: `run_custom` after `zephyr/zephyr.elf`. Twister would never have noticed, since it builds before it runs; `west build -t run` would have run a stale image. It is an entry in [upstream.md](upstream.md). Fifteen tests for the two files, five of them on the `run` target of a small CMake project: three with a stand-in for the command that writes its arguments down, one that hears a line while the command still runs (which is what `USES_TERMINAL` is for, and fails without it), and one that goes the whole way, the command itself and the boot ROM and the console of a dozen instructions that say so. ⚠️ **For step 6**: the test of the schema reads Zephyr's tree and is skipped where there is none, which is CI's `test` job, so the `twister` job has to run `tests/python/test_zephyr_module.py` as well as twister. And `socpuppet` has to be on twister's `PATH`, and `SOCPUPPET_FIRMWARE_DIR` in its environment, since ninja runs the command from the image's build directory.
- **Step 5: ztest's console is read in one place, and a suite can be in the summary without ever having begun.** `socpuppet.ztest.parse` makes a `Report` of what a console printed: suites in the order they were first named, each with its cases and how many times it began, and the run's verdict. 🎓 ztest says how a case went twice, on the line the case ends with and again in a summary when every suite is over. The first is all a run that was stopped has. The second is the only place a suite that was left out appears: a suite can say it is not to be run, ztest never prints that it began, and the summary lists its cases as skipped. The design review found that from ztest's source, and the report has such a suite with `starts == 0`. A suite that resets its CPU twice, as the watchdog's does, has `starts == 3` and its case once. Twenty-five tests on consoles written in the test, their shape checked against two of the spike's logs. `verdict_of` is the verdict of a line or of a whole console, which is what `socpuppet run` asks of each line and what the exit tests ask while they wait. The six test files that each had their own copy of the two verdict lines have none, and `assert_it_read_and_wrote` in `disk_access.py` names the disk test's two cases once and fails with the console. **Not done**: the two examples that print a verdict in colour keep their own two strings, since they are shows and read as prose, and the sample in [boot-your-firmware.md](boot-your-firmware.md) is step 10's.
- **Step 6: every scenario of the list passes or is left out with its reason, and none fails.** `firmware/twister.sh --suites` on this machine, with the module's own board files and nothing of the spike's: 97 scenarios pass (32 on the host, 28 on the SSD, 37 on the IO manager), 2073 test cases, and twenty are quarantined. That is twister's own judgment, which step 3's note could not give. The quarantine list has three entries: `drivers.watchdog`, which is Zephyr's test and Zephyr's driver disagreeing and now an entry in [upstream.md](upstream.md); `arch.riscv64.fatal.*` on the two 32-bit boards; and `libraries.libc.common.stdio.*`, three scenarios and not the spike's two, which want the FatFs module. **Measured here**: 7 min 40 s for the whole list, on sixteen cores through Docker with the volume, and 12 minutes once before it to build socpuppet for Linux. No `timeout_multiplier` was needed: the slowest scenario takes 38 s of the 180 its suite allows. **What changed from the spike's recipe**: a run of twister has a directory of its own under `build/twister/out`, named `suites` or after the suite that needed its own flags, and replaces the last one of that name; `handler_stderr.log`, where `socpuppet run` says how a run ended, comes back with the console; the Linux build is made again by the script when `cmake/patches` has changed, which [development.md](development.md) had as a thing to remember; and the two versions are in `firmware/versions.sh` for both scripts. Four tests in `tests/tooling` hold the two lists: every suite is a directory with a suite in it, the quarantine list is one twister's schema accepts, every entry says why, and every scenario an entry names is one the run has, so that an entry cannot outlive what it was for. **The CI job passed the first time it ran, in twenty minutes, and the second, in thirty-three**, both on 2026-10-12: the same 113 scenarios as here, none failing. Zephyr, the SDK and the venv come from a cache keyed on `firmware/versions.sh` and saved as soon as they are fetched, the boot ROMs from the `firmware` job's artifact, and socpuppet is built with the `dev` preset. Twister's own part was eleven minutes on a runner's four cores, where the spike's estimate was sixteen; fetching Zephyr was two, building socpuppet five. The second run's runner was half as fast at everything, and twister's part was nineteen. No scenario used more than a sixth of the time twister allows it, even there. So the list stays as it is. **On an Apple silicon Mac it runs on macOS itself, and CI holds it to that**: the SDK has a toolchain for one, both scripts already took that path, and nobody had run it. On 2026-10-12, on a ten-core M4 with nothing changed in either script: every image in a minute, a clean build of socpuppet in three, and the whole list in 6 min 31 s, the same 113 scenarios and 2153 test cases. The `twister` job is now a matrix of `ubuntu-24.04` and `macos-15`, with a cache of Zephyr and its SDK for each, since the compiler is a program for one machine. The Mac's leg passed the first time, in thirty-nine minutes on three cores, twenty-three of them twister's; the Linux leg beside it took thirty-three, nineteen of them twister's. [twister.md](twister.md#in-ci) has the four places in a table. **Decided on the way**: no `west.yml` and no `action-zephyr-setup`, since the cache does the job with the script users already have.
- **Step 7: four files, and the watchdog's application runs on a board it had never been built for.** Each application under `firmware/` has a `testcase.yaml`, and `firmware/twister.sh -T firmware` passes six scenarios of six: `flash_test` by ztest's verdict, the other three by the lines they print. `firmware/build.sh` builds `firmware/watchdog` for the host and the SSD, one board for each kind of CPU; a suite costs twister one more small build, so it is allowed on the IO manager too, and passes there. **Not as planned**: no `expect_reboot` for the watchdog's application, because twister's console harness never reads it. It is for a ztest suite that begins again after a reset. And five lines where the plan had the three causes: with the lines between them, in order, a watchdog whose interrupt never comes or one that bites while it is fed fails the scenario. Five tests in `tests/tooling` hold our suites: every Zephyr application of ours is one, each file is one twister's schema accepts, each scenario says which boards it is for and they are boards the module has, and each is under a line of the list, so that steps 8 and 9 add suites without touching it.
- **Step 8: six suites of the drivers' own, 37 cases, and the SSD has a host to speak to.** Each suite is a Zephyr application under the module's `tests/drivers/`, on the board its hardware is on, and the module's `tests` is a line of the list: with them the whole run is 111 scenarios, none failing, in 7 min 44 s here. **Decided here, as planned**: the SSD's board takes a shield, `socpuppet_ssd_host`, and with it `socpuppet run` puts 🎭 a host in front of the SSD that says a host's first words to a drive: it finds it and lets it reach the host's memory, enables it, waits to be told it is ready, asks for a pair of I/O queues and how big the drive is, and writes one block. It is a script of the command's own, built on `bring_up_the_drive`, with nothing of `NvmeHost` changed. The shield's overlay adds nothing, which is the true devicetree change: what differs is on the other side of the frontend. Making that host the default would have failed every image that never says ready, which is all of Zephyr's kernel suites on the SSD. A host that is never told the drive is ready ends the run in one line that names the shield, the reason and the remedy. ⚠️ It waits a second of simulated time, so a suite built with the shield has that long. 🎓 **Found by running them**: the DMA engine copies nothing until a host has switched the drive's bus mastering on, which a host does when it finds the device, so `dma_engine` needs the shield too and its setup waits for a copy to be made; the three cases that expected a refusal had passed before that for the wrong reason. And the frontend has eight pairs of I/O queues, where the test first said one. **The loop, since the drivers existed**: each case passed when written and was then held to a one-line mutation of its driver, in batches, by the cases that failed. Eighteen mutations, all caught in the end. Three are caught by a crash and not by a case: a command read short and a PCIe driver that leaves memory decoding off both end in a bus fault, and a DMA length one short fails the suite's setup. One was not caught at first: `ucie_link_retrain` swapped for a plain training passed, because a link that is up says so either way. A retrain takes 5.1 ms and a training of a link that is up 17 µs, and UCIe's four milliseconds of reset hold is now a case. **What the reviews changed**: the frontend's suite first had the host stop after its three admin commands, which left `nvme_frontend_create_queue` called and never seen to work, and every command coming from queue 0, which is what a queue number that was never read would say; the host now writes a block, and that command arrives from queue 1 only if the queue was created. The DMA round trip is held to the pattern and not to the buffer it was sent from, which a copy made the wrong way overwrites. And the numbers the C has of the stand-ins beside it, the host's memory and the compute die's word, are held to the Python by three pytest tests. Three of the suites do their hardware's one-time sequence in the suite's setup and write down what they saw, a case to each thing seen, because ztest promises no order and a link is brought up once. **Not done**: a case that takes an interrupt through `imsic_irq`'s handler that looks again, which the shell's suite and the watchdog's application show end to end; and the frontend's reset path (`DISABLED`), which wants a host that disables a controller it has enabled.
- **Step 9: the firmware's own sources, built into a test in the firmware's place.** `firmware/ssd/tests/ftl` and `tests/admin` are Zephyr applications whose `CMakeLists.txt` names the firmware's `ftl.c`, `admin.c` and what they call, and whose `main.c` has the cases and says which devices are which, as the firmware's own `main.c` does. Nothing in `firmware/ssd/src` changed to make that possible: the firmware's parts already met only through `ssd.h`. Seven cases for the flash translation layer and fourteen for the admin commands, where the test makes up each command as a host would write it and hands it to `admin_command()`. Identify's page is copied to the host's memory and fetched back, so the admin suite takes the SSD's host shield for the bus mastering and for nothing else. Sixteen mutations of `ftl.c`, `admin.c` and `io.c`, each caught by the case meant for it. 💡 **What the review changed**: Identify's block count was first held to `io_drive_blocks()`, which is where `admin.c` gets it, so a drive that counted its bytes as blocks passed; it is held to the NAND's size. And each of the FTL's cases stores fillings of its own, because the table is emptied before a case and the NAND is not. **Not done**: creating a queue and creating it twice, which want the controller enabled first, and which the frontend's suite and the exit tests show from the host's side.
- **Step 10: the docs, and the spike is gone.** [twister.md](twister.md) is "Run Zephyr's tests on the puppet": one command, how twister comes to run socpuppet, what passes, our own suites and the shields they are built with, what is left out and why, and what to do when a scenario fails. [boot-your-firmware.md](boot-your-firmware.md) has "Test your firmware with twister", [development.md](development.md) has where these tests are and when to run them, the README links the page, and [upstream.md](upstream.md) has the two entries the milestone found in Zephyr. `spikes/twister/` is deleted, and its report says what replaced each part.

Order: 0 is done; 1 and 2 first, because every later run of Zephyr's tests wants a CPU that wakes and keeps time; then 3, 4, 5 in any order; 6 wants 3 and 4; 7, 8 and 9 want 4; 10 last. Left open: whether `firmware/build.sh`'s image list becomes a `twister --build-only` run; `CONFIG_USERSPACE` on the `_mu` hart (a to-do); whether the ROM images ship in the wheel.

## Phase 5: The road to Linux

The host's firmware is about to change from Zephyr, which runs in machine mode with the hart's memory as it is, to Linux, which runs in supervisor mode under OpenSBI, with page tables, with user programs below it, and with its drivers written against bindings of its own. Phase 4 built each block to what Linux's driver for it will ask, and each milestone there ends with that list. What no milestone has done is run the hart the way Linux will run it: nothing in socpuppet has ever turned paging on, dropped to user mode, or taken a supervisor's interrupt through the supervisor's own registers, because Zephyr needs none of it. ⚠️ A borrowed model is trusted only as far as its contract, and M13 found that a core which had never run with interrupts had a bug in how it took one; M21 found two more the first time Zephyr's own suites ran. The same will be true of paging, and a kernel is the worst place to find out.

So this phase goes in steps, each one a smaller thing to debug than the next. First the hart alone, with bare-metal programs that do what Linux does and nothing else (M22). Then a spike that times the hart, since a kernel's boot is hundreds of millions of instructions and nobody has measured what this core makes of one (M26). Then OpenSBI with its own test payload, which is the first supervisor-mode program and the first reader of a devicetree in Linux's bindings (M23). Then UEFI, EDK2's RISC-V port, which is the first program to turn paging on from supervisor mode, take a timer interrupt there and read the devicetree whole, and does each the way Linux will, in a program that stops at a prompt and answers questions (M28). Then a spike of 🎭 virtio stand-ins, so that when the kernel comes it can be given a console, a disk and a network that Linux's own drivers know by heart, and a real block that misbehaves can be swapped out while it is looked at (M25). Then the kernel, with its root filesystem in memory so that nothing but the console stands between it and the prompt (M20). The drive comes after the prompt (M24), and Ethernet, the IOMMU and the AI processor after that, each under Linux's own driver (Phase 6).

**Decided on 2026-10-12, planning Phase 5.**

- **The first Linux exit test is a prompt and nothing more.** It used to be a prompt, a `ping` and an inference. The `ping` is M17's last line now and the inference M19's, and both come after M20, so that each lands under Linux with the kernel already known to boot.
- **The IOMMU comes after the drive is Linux's root filesystem.** Linux does not need an IOMMU to boot, and the drive's DMA goes untranslated under Linux first, as it does under Zephyr. M15 then puts the IOMMU in that path under Linux's own driver.
- **OpenSBI is a milestone of its own.** It is where machine mode meets supervisor mode, where the devicetree is first read by something that is not Zephyr, and where SBI's timer, console and reset calls meet the timer, the UART and the system controller. Each of those is a thing to see work before a kernel is on top of it.
- 🎭 **Virtio stand-ins are spiked before the kernel, and not planned as blocks.** Asked by the maintainer on 2026-10-12: a console, a disk and a network that every Linux drives are worth having in hand before the first boot, as the stand-ins the plan has used since M0 are: to hold a real block's place while it is brought up, and to tell a kernel that does not boot from a device that does not work. No real SoC has them, so they stay stand-ins, and M25's report says which of the three earned a place.
- **Every fix to the borrowed CPU is a patch with its entry in [upstream.md](upstream.md)**, as M13's and M21's were, and every test of M22 is checked with a mutation, as M8's were.
- **The step tables come later.** Each milestone below has its decisions, what planning found and its exit test. Its steps are written when it is planned, one `/tdd` session each, as Phase 4's were. M22's were, on 2026-10-12.

**Decided on 2026-10-15, adding M28.**

- **UEFI is a milestone of its own, before the kernel, though the kernel does not need it.** Asked by the maintainer: a kernel finds a fault in the model by hanging, hundreds of millions of instructions in, and the tools for it are a debugger and a trace. EDK2 finds the same fault earlier and says so, since it prints every driver's progress to the console as it goes, and what boots stops at a shell that can be asked what it found. The rehearsal is cheaper than the performance, and it uses the same stage: `fw_jump`, `a0` and `a1`, the devicetree in Linux's bindings, the 16550 and the timer.
- **It is EDK2 and not U-Boot.** Both run in supervisor mode on OpenSBI, read the devicetree and drive the 16550, and U-Boot is the smaller build with an EFI loader of its own. But U-Boot never turns paging on, which is the thing most worth rehearsing, and a RISC-V server boots EDK2. 🚧 The embedded board's chain (U-Boot SPL, then OpenSBI, then U-Boot) is a line for Phase 7 if anyone asks for it.
- **The kernel's road does not change.** M20's exit is still `fw_jump` with the kernel where it jumps, in the quick tier. UEFI loading the kernel through its EFI stub is a tier of M20 and of M24, the way a server does it, and not their exit.

### M22 — The hart Linux needs

A **hart** is RISC-V's word for a CPU as software sees it, and the host's is DBT-RISE's `rv64gc_msu`: machine, supervisor and user mode, floating point, atomics, and an MMU. Zephyr uses machine mode and nothing of the rest. This milestone runs the rest, with bare-metal programs in `tests/cpp/support/riscv_program.h` and tests in `tests/cpp/platform/cpu_test.cpp`, the way M13 tested a supervisor's interrupt and M21 a sleeping CPU: a program that does one thing Linux does, and a test that sees it done. Where the borrowed core is wrong, the fix is a patch.

- **a) Paging.** A program in supervisor mode builds Sv39 page tables in RAM, the three-level page table a RISC-V CPU's own MMU walks, turns them on through `satp`, and runs through them, its code as well as its data, with the memory behind direct memory access as the host's RAM is. A page that is not mapped is a page fault with `stval` saying which address; a page whose accessed bit is clear, or whose dirty bit is clear when it is written, is a page fault too, which the specification allows and the borrowed MMU does; `sfence.vma` has the MMU look again; `sstatus.SUM` and `MXR` do what they say.
- **b) User mode.** A program drops to user mode, makes an `ecall`, which `medeleg` sends to supervisor mode, and comes back; `sstatus.SPP` says where it came from; a user program under paging touches a supervisor's page and faults; and an interrupt that arrives at the `ecall` is taken after it and not inside it.
- **c) The supervisor's way to the IMSIC.** `siselect`, `sireg` and `stopei`, which M13 left undone, answered from the IMSIC's supervisor file as the machine mode's three are from its machine file (`models/hart_registers.h`, registered in `end_of_elaboration` as M13 step 7 did); a supervisor's handler claims an interrupt through `stopei`; `ssaia` joins the ISA list when the supervisor's file is reachable.
- **d) The counters.** `mcounteren` and `scounteren` decide whether supervisor mode and user mode may read `time`, `cycle` and `instret`; Linux's vDSO reads `time` from user space and counts on it. A read that is not allowed traps.
- **e) Floating-point state.** `mstatus.FS`: with floating point off, an instruction traps; with it on, the first use marks it dirty. Linux's context switch saves the registers only when it is told to. And a single-precision load or store reaches an address above 4 GiB, where a user program's stack is.
- **f) Atomics.** `lr`/`sc` and the `amo*` instructions, through direct memory access and over the bus. One hart, so a reservation is never contended, and a test is cheap.
- **g) Misaligned loads and stores**, which the core traps on: the trap says which instruction and which address, since OpenSBI makes the access on the kernel's behalf from those, and it can be handed down, since a newer kernel asks for it.
- **h) Speed** is not here any more. It is a spike of its own, [M26](#m26--the-fast-hart-time-boxed-spike-only-m20-waits-on-it), since this milestone was planned.
- **The APLIC question**, settled by reading and written down for M23: QEMU's `virt` has an APLIC domain for machine mode and one for supervisor mode, and socpuppet has one domain in message mode. What OpenSBI's `fdt_irqchip_aplic` and Linux's APLIC driver each do with one domain decides whether M23 adds a second or describes the one it has.

**Decided on 2026-10-12, planning M22.**

- **Speed leaves this milestone and becomes a spike, M26.** Item h was to measure DBT-RISE's other backends. Planning read them first (below, under M26): one makes code for x86-64 only, one has no RISC-V core that could be the host's, one would want a patch before it could be asked for, and QBox is ten times faster than any of them and is GPL. Asked by the maintainer: socpuppet may well want a CPU that is good enough and ships in the wheel, and a faster one beside it that a user builds from source, and which is which wants experiments and a report, not a step inside a milestone about being right. Nothing in a to g waits for it: their programs are a few hundred instructions each.
- **The accessed and dirty bits stay as the borrowed MMU has them, and a test pins it.** 🎓 A page-table entry has an **accessed** bit and a **dirty** bit, so that a kernel can tell which pages were used and which must be written back. The specification lets a hart set them itself as it walks, or raise a page fault when one it needs is clear and leave the setting to the kernel; the second is the extension called **Svade**. DBT-RISE does the second, Linux copes with either, and a walk that writes memory would be a larger patch for nothing Linux asks. One hole in it is mended (step 3).
- **Instruction fetches are translated, by a patch, and the first paging test is the shape of a kernel**: code at a high virtual address, mapped onto the RAM it was loaded in, so that a fetch that is not translated cannot pass.
- **The two faults in how a trap is taken that [upstream.md](upstream.md) has had since M21, found by reading and never run, are item b's**, and the one in `flw` and `fsw` is item e's. Each is a test and a patch.
- **A patch to a generated file is a patch all the same.** DBT-RISE's interpreter is written by a generator from a description in CoreDSL, which the build does not fetch. Two fixes are in what it wrote (steps 6 and 8), and their entries in [upstream.md](upstream.md) say that the description is where the fix belongs.
- **The assembler grows with the steps.** An instruction `riscv_program.h` cannot write yet is added by the test that first needs it. 🎓 A 64-bit constant, a page-table entry or a value for `satp`, cannot be made by `lui` and `addi`, which give 32 bits with the sign carried up; the programs keep theirs in a **literal pool**, a few words of data after the code that `ld` reads by an address worked out from the program's own. And the paging programs get a fixture with pages in it: 64 KiB of RAM where the others have four.
- **`ssaia` is listed beside `smcsrind` when the hart's `csr` is connected.** Linux's IMSIC driver looks for it before it will touch `stopei`. M13 left `smaia` out because the name promises all of the AIA for machine mode and three registers were there; the same is true of `ssaia`, and what is still missing (`stopi`, which nothing here reads) goes in the CPU's page under what it leaves out.
- **The APLIC question is read, not run**, as the milestone said, and its answer is a line of M23.

What planning found, in the borrowed core as the build has it (`build/dev/_deps/dbt_rise_riscv-src/`), which the steps assume:

- ⚠️ **Instruction fetches never go through the MMU.** The interpreter fetches from a memory space of its own (`IMEM`, `src/vm/interp/vm_rv64gc.cpp`, `fetch_ins`), the hart hands the space on, and the MMU translates an address only when its space is `MEM` (`src/iss/mem/mmu.h`, `needs_translation`). The layer for physical memory protection beside it takes both and says so in a comment (`pmp.h`). So with paging on, supervisor and user mode fetch a virtual address as if it were physical: a program that maps itself where it already is runs, and a kernel at a high address does not. It is the largest thing planning found, and the first paging test is written to meet it.
- **The MMU answers to more than Sv39, and stops the simulator for anything else.** `update_vm_info` takes `satp`'s mode 8 (Sv39), 9 (Sv48), 10 (Sv57) and 11, and calls `abort()` for any other. 🎓 `satp` also has an **ASID**, an address-space identifier, a number a kernel gives each process so that a switch of process need not empty the **TLB**, the cache of translations an MMU keeps. `write_satp` keeps the whole value, so an ASID reads back as written; the TLB is a map from a page's number to its translation, with no ASID in it, and is not emptied when `satp` is written. Linux finds out how many ASID bits a hart has by writing ones and reading them back, and then trusts them. A hart that says sixteen and keeps none hands one process another's translations. A register whose fields may be written with anything and read back as what is legal is **WARL**, and the fix is to make the ASID read as zero, which is a legal number of bits to have.
- **The accessed and dirty bits are checked on a walk and not on a hit.** A walk faults when the accessed bit is clear, or the dirty bit for a store, with the code that would set them commented out. A translation found in the TLB is used without either check, so a store through a page that a load cached goes through with the dirty bit clear, and a kernel that counts on the fault to know the page was written never hears of it.
- **A read that crosses a page translates the same address twice** (`mmu.h`, `read_mem`: the second half asks for `addr.val` where the write beside it asks for the split address). Data never gets there, since a misaligned load traps first. A four-byte instruction two bytes before the end of a page does, once fetches are translated.
- ⚠️ **The counter-enable registers are stubs.** `riscv_hart_msu_vp.h` answers `mcounteren` and `scounteren` with `read_null` and `write_null`, and a counter's read checks only the privilege its number implies, which for `cycle`, `time` and `instret` is none. So every mode reads them, whatever was written. Linux's vDSO needs user mode to read `time`, which works today by accident.
- ⚠️ **`mstatus.FS` is a field and nothing else.** No floating-point instruction looks at it: none traps when it is off and none marks it dirty. `write_mstatus` sets the summary bit `SD` when FS is written as dirty and never clears it. Linux saves a process's floating-point registers at a context switch only if FS says dirty, so two processes that both use floating point would share one set. The interpreter decodes every instruction to a number before one `switch` runs it, which is one place to check.
- ⚠️ **`flw` and `fsw` drop the upper half of the address on a 64-bit hart** (`vm_rv64gc.cpp`: the address is cast to `uint32_t`, where `fld` and `fsd` beside them cast to `uint64_t`). A user program's stack under Sv39 is above 4 GiB.
- **The two faults [upstream.md](upstream.md) has had since M21 are on item b's path.** `enter_trap` clears the pending interrupt only when an interrupt is what it took, so an interrupt that arrives at an `ecall` is taken one instruction into the `ecall`'s handler, with interrupts off, over the top of `sepc` and `scause`. Under Linux that is a timer interrupt at a system call. And `sret` with `mstatus.TSR` set raises an illegal instruction that the next line overwrites.
- **An `ecall` from user mode to supervisor mode is wired**: the cause is 8 plus the mode it came from, `medeleg` sends it down, and `sepc`, `scause` and `sstatus.SPP` are written. `medeleg` takes bits 4 and 6 (misaligned), 8 (the `ecall`) and 12, 13 and 15 (page faults), and not 3 (a breakpoint). `leave_trap` does not clear `mstatus.MPRV` on a return to a lower mode, which the specification asks.
- **Misaligned loads and stores trap** in the supervisor hart, with the address in `tval`. That is allowed by the specification, and it is OpenSBI's job to emulate them: item g sees that the trap can be delegated. An `amo*` at a misaligned address raises the load's cause, 4, because it reads first; the specification says the store's, 6.
- **`lr`, `sc` and every `amo*` are in the interpreter**, in both widths. A reservation is an entry in a map, made by `lr` and taken out by `sc`'s look at it, and nothing else ever removes one: not a store, not a trap. They are loads and stores to the memory path, so they go through the MMU and direct memory access as any other does.
- **`sfence.vma` empties the whole TLB**, whatever address or ASID it names, which is always allowed.
- **The IMSIC already answers the supervisor's registers.** `hart_registers.h` has `kSireg` and `kStopei`, `Imsic::Answer` sends them to the supervisor's file, and the message-controller contract drives them (M13 step 6). What is missing is the hart's half: the adapter registers `miselect`, `mireg` and `mtopei` and keeps one selection. The hart has nothing at 0x150, 0x151 or 0x15C, and it works out who may read a CSR from its number, so user mode traps without being told.
- **The page-table walk goes through the same memory path M13 step 4 found a bug in**: a pending interrupt that stopped a load from writing its result. A walk is several loads, and a test that has an interrupt arrive during one is the shape of test that found it.
- **What the tests have to stand on.** Every CPU fixture in `cpu_test.cpp` has 4 KiB of RAM with the result at 0x200 into it, so a program longer than 128 instructions writes over its own answer, and a page table needs pages. `riscv_program.h` cannot write `ld`, `jalr`, `ecall`, `sfence.vma`, an `lr`, `sc` or `amo*`, a floating-point load, store or sum, or an `add` of two registers. A test can read nothing of a CPU but its memory, so every program stores what it saw.
- **The exit line named `docs/dbt-rise-cpu.md`, and the page is `docs/models/dbt-rise-cpu.md`.** Its "What we changed" counts thirteen patches where the build applies fifteen, and `THIRD_PARTY_NOTICES.md` and `development.md` are behind in the same way. Step 10 counts again.

The steps, each one `/tdd` session unless marked. Every fix to the borrowed core is a file in `cmake/patches/`, a line in `cmake/Dependencies.cmake` and an entry in [upstream.md](upstream.md), and where a row names a patch those three come with it. A test that passes the first time is checked with a mutation, as M8's were.

| # | Step | Where the behaviour goes |
|---|---|---|
| M22 0 | ✅ **The plan.** This table, what planning found and decided, and M26. Not a `/tdd` step | `docs/plan.md` |
| M22 1 | ✅ **The hart reaches its IMSIC's supervisor file through `siselect`, `sireg` and `stopei`.** `siselect` is kept by the hart, twelve bits of its own, apart from `miselect`'s; `sireg` and `stopei` are answered from the IMSIC by the addresses in `hart_registers.h`, as M13 step 7 answers the machine's. With no IMSIC all three stay illegal instructions. The test's platform gains the supervisor's page on the bus and `imsic.supervisor_irq -> cpu.supervisor_irq`. A program in supervisor mode enables delivery and one identity through `siselect` and `sireg`, a device writes that identity to the supervisor's page, and the supervisor's handler claims it through `stopei`: once, at a quantum of zero and at 100 µs. A message to the supervisor's page does not run machine mode's handler. With `csr` connected the CPU's devicetree node lists `ssaia`, and the host's checked-in devicetree is generated again | `models/dbt_rise_cpu.*`, `models/hart_registers.h`, `components.py`, `tests/cpp/platform/cpu_test.cpp`, `tests/cpp/support/riscv_program.h`, `tests/python/test_devicetree.py`, `socpuppet_host.dts` |
| M22 2 | ✅ **Supervisor mode runs through Sv39 page tables, its code as well as its data.** A platform with 64 KiB of RAM behind a probe that counts transactions, and page tables that the test lays out in it: three levels down to the first 512 pages of the top half of memory, where Linux's kernel is. Machine mode writes `satp` and returns to supervisor mode at an address up there, with nothing mapped where the program was loaded, and the program stores where it is running through a mapping of a page of data (red: the fetch was not translated). An `addi` across two pages that are not neighbours in the RAM is read a half from each (red), and a two-byte return in a page's last two bytes runs with nothing mapped after it (red). Supervisor mode turns paging on for itself, through a page of 1 GiB that maps the RAM where it is, and then jumps up. A load, a store and a jump to an address no page is mapped at are causes 13, 15 and 12, handed down through `medeleg`, with the address in `stval`. `satp` reads zero until it is written, an address-space identifier written to it reads back as zero (red), and any kind of paging but Sv39 leaves it as it was (red: the simulator ended, or the hart took Sv48). A loop under paging still runs by direct memory access. ⚠️ Not as planned in three ways: see what step 2 found, below the table | `cpu_test.cpp`, `riscv_program.h`, `cmake/patches/dbt-rise-riscv-translate-fetches.patch`, `dbt-rise-riscv-fetch-ends-page.patch`, `dbt-rise-riscv-satp-warl.patch` |
| M22 3 | ✅ **The page table's bits mean what they say, and a walk survives an interrupt.** Svade, pinned: a load from a page whose accessed bit is clear is cause 13, and a store to one whose dirty bit is clear is cause 15, each with the address in `stval`. The hole: a load from a page with its dirty bit clear and then a store to it is cause 15 as well (red: the store went through). `sstatus.SUM`: supervisor mode's load from a user page is cause 13 with it clear and is made with it set. `sstatus.MXR`: a load from a page that may only be executed is cause 13 without it and is made with it. `sfence.vma` has the MMU look again: a program changes the entry of a page it has just read through, executes it, and reads from where the entry now leads. An interrupt arrives at a load from a page the hart has no translation for yet, and the load still stores its result | `cpu_test.cpp`, `riscv_program.h`, `cmake/patches/dbt-rise-riscv-tlb-dirty.patch` |
| M22 4 | ✅ **User mode makes a system call, and a trap takes nothing with it.** A program hands `ecall` from user mode down in `medeleg` and drops to user mode, and the user's code executes `ecall`: the supervisor's handler sees `scause` 8 and `sstatus.SPP` cleared, returns past it, and the user's code runs on. User mode reading `sstatus` is an illegal instruction, taken by machine mode or by supervisor mode as `medeleg` says, and so are `sfence.vma`, `sret` and `mret` in user mode (red, all three: none trapped). Under paging, a program in user mode on a user page makes a system call that supervisor mode takes; its load from a supervisor's page is cause 13 with the address in `stval`, and its jump into one is cause 12. An interrupt that is pending at an `ecall`: supervisor mode's call to machine mode is taken as it was made and the supervisor's interrupt once, by the supervisor (red), and the same on the core with machine mode only (red). `sret` in supervisor mode with `mstatus.TSR` set is an illegal instruction (red). An `mret` to supervisor mode clears `mstatus.MPRV` (red). ⚠️ Five patches where the plan had one: see what step 4 found, below the table | `cpu_test.cpp`, `riscv_program.h`, `cmake/patches/dbt-rise-riscv-xret-privilege.patch`, `dbt-rise-riscv-sfence-user-mode.patch`, `dbt-rise-riscv-interrupt-inside-exception.patch`, `dbt-rise-riscv-sret-tsr.patch`, `dbt-rise-riscv-mprv-on-return.patch` |
| M22 5 | ✅ **`mcounteren` and `scounteren` say who may read the counters.** Both become registers that keep what is written. Machine mode reads `cycle`, `time` and `instret` whatever they say. Supervisor mode reads one only with its bit in `mcounteren`, and otherwise takes an illegal instruction. User mode needs the bit in both. `time`, on a CPU connected to its timer as M13 step 2 has it, read from user mode with both bits set, is one tick behind the timer's own count, as it is for machine mode: Linux's vDSO. Red on the stubs: every refusal, and the registers reading back | `cpu_test.cpp`, `riscv_program.h`, `cmake/patches/dbt-rise-riscv-counter-enables.patch` |
| M22 6 | ✅ **Floating point is off until it is turned on, and its first use says so.** With `mstatus.FS` off, a floating-point move, load, store, sum, fused multiply-add, each of the four compressed loads and stores, and a read or a write of `fcsr` is an illegal instruction, taken at that instruction (red). Supervisor mode sets `FS` in `sstatus` and reads it back there, and machine mode in `mstatus` (red: `sstatus` neither showed it nor took it). With it on and not dirty, one floating-point instruction or a write of `fcsr` leaves `FS` dirty and `SD` set in both registers (red), and `FS` marked clean again clears `SD`. Supervisor mode cannot set `MPRV`, `TVM`, `TW` or `TSR` through `sstatus` and does not read them there (red). Under paging, `flw` through an address above 4 GiB loads what is there, and `fsw` stores there (red, each). ⚠️ More than the plan had: see what step 6 found, below the table | `cpu_test.cpp`, `riscv_program.h`, `cmake/patches/dbt-rise-riscv-sstatus-view.patch`, `dbt-rise-riscv-fp-status.patch`, `dbt-rise-riscv-flw-address.patch` |
| M22 7 | ✅ **Atomics, by direct memory access and over the bus.** `lr.d` and then `sc.d` to the same address stores and answers 0. `sc.d` answers 1 and stores nothing with no `lr` before it, after an `sc.d` that stored, and with another cell reserved. And, red both ways: after an `sc.d` to another cell, which must take the reservation with it, and for a cell that was reserved before another was. `amoswap.d`, `amoadd.d`, `amoor.w` and `amomax.d` each give back what was there and leave what they should, the 32-bit one with its sign carried up and the upper half of the cell left alone. All of it again with direct access to the memory taken back, so that each goes over the bus | `cpu_test.cpp`, `riscv_program.h`, `cmake/patches/dbt-rise-riscv-one-reservation.patch` |
| M22 8 | ✅ **A misaligned access traps where it can be caught.** A `lw` at an odd address is cause 4 and a `sw` cause 6, taken by machine mode with the instruction in `mepc` and the address in `mtval`, and an `amoadd.w` there is cause 6 as well (red: it raised 4). With causes 4 and 6 set in `medeleg` the supervisor's handler takes them, with the address in `stval`, and the program runs on past the instruction. A handler makes the store itself a byte at a time, steps past it and returns, and the word is where it was to go | `cpu_test.cpp`, `riscv_program.h`, `cmake/patches/dbt-rise-riscv-misaligned-amo.patch` |
| M22 9 | ✅ **The APLIC question, by reading.** What OpenSBI's `fdt_irqchip_aplic` and Linux's APLIC driver each do with one domain in message mode and no domain for machine mode, and so whether M23 adds a second or describes the one it has. Its answer is a line of M23's "what planning found". Not a `/tdd` step | `docs/plan.md` |
| M22 10 | ✅ **The docs.** `docs/models/dbt-rise-cpu.md`: paging, user mode, the counters and floating-point state in what it does; the supervisor's three registers out of what it leaves out, and `stopi` in; "What we changed" counted again, and `THIRD_PARTY_NOTICES.md` and `development.md` with it. [upstream.md](upstream.md) read whole. Here: the table's marks, "What M22 delivered", the milestone map and Status. Not a `/tdd` step | docs |

What the steps found, as they went:

- **Step 0: the plan.** Written on 2026-10-12, with speed moved out to M26.
- **Step 1: three more registers and one more selection, and a core that must not have them.** The hart answers `siselect`, `sireg` and `stopei` as M13 step 7 answers machine mode's: the window and the claim go to the IMSIC over `csr`, where the supervisor's file has answered since M13 step 6, and the selection stays in the hart. A program in supervisor mode switches its file on, a device writes to the supervisor's page, and the handler in `stvec` runs once and claims the identity, at a quantum of zero and of 100 µs. The first code to pass that kept one selection for both modes, and the test that reads `miselect` and `siselect` back found the monitor's moved by the operating system's, so each mode has its own. ⚠️ **Found by the design review**: the three were registered on every core, so the plain core, which has no supervisor mode, answered them once it had an IMSIC, where a real hart traps. They are registered only on the core with the mode, and `ssaia` is listed only for it. 💡 `sireg` with nothing selected is refused by the IMSIC, which has no register numbered zero, so of the three it is the one that trapped before that fix. Three tests passed when written and each is held by a mutation of the adapter: no yield after a claim through `stopei` (the handler is entered again and again within a quantum, as M13 step 7 found for `mtopei`), a selection kept with no IMSIC, and `ssaia` for a core without the mode. 🎓 Zephyr reads one name out of the CPU's list of extensions, `smcsrind` (`arch/riscv/Kconfig.isa`), and takes any other as it comes, so the host's devicetree gained `ssaia` and Zephyr builds from it as before. **Not done**: `stopi` and `mtopi`, which nothing run here reads and which the CPU's page now names under what it leaves out.
- ⚠️ **Step 2: a kernel could not have fetched its first instruction, and two more faults were waiting behind that one.** The fetch was as planning read it: with `needs_translation` taking the interpreter's space for instructions as well as the one for data, a program runs at an address in the top half of memory with nothing mapped where it was loaded. A translated fetch is then the first thing ever to read across a page under paging, because a fetch is always four bytes and may begin two before a page's end, and it met two faults in `read_mem`. The second half of such a read was translated from the first half's address, so an `addi t0, zero, 0x123` across two pages executed as `addi t0, t1, 0x29`, its first two bytes read twice. And the page after was read even when the two bytes that end a page are a whole compressed instruction, so a `ret` in a mapping's last two bytes took a page fault for a page it never needed. Three patches, since the design review had the last made one of its own (`fetch-ends-page`): its fix upstream is in the interpreter, which knows how long an instruction is, where ours is in the MMU so that one change covers every core. **Not as planned, in three ways.** (1) The test lays the tables out, and the program does not build them: a table is data, the MMU cannot tell who wrote it, and a test that says `Map(address, page, bits)` reads as the specification does. A program that changes a table it is running on is step 3's, where it matters. (2) The program is not mapped where it was loaded. The plan had a 2 MiB page for that, and with one the first test would have passed on a core that fetched nothing through its tables at all. Machine mode writes `satp`, which translates nothing of machine mode's, and `mret` lands at the high address. 💡 No monitor does that: OpenSBI hands over with paging off. The way a kernel really does it is a test of its own, supervisor mode writing `satp` while it runs through a page of 1 GiB and then jumping, and that one passed when written. (3) **Decided on the way: this hart has Sv39 and no other kind of paging.** The borrowed MMU also takes Sv48, Sv57 and a mode 11, none of which anything here has run; Linux asks for Sv57 first and keeps whatever reads back; and a kernel is the wrong place to run a five-level walk for the first time. The `satp` patch takes those cases out, which is ours and not a fault of theirs, and [upstream.md](upstream.md) says how to have them back. Most chips have Sv39 only. The rest of that patch is what planning read: an identifier that reads back as zero, a mode with no case that changes nothing where it called `abort()`, and a register that starts at zero. 🎓 **A mutation that survived**: the big-page test first stored its result through the high mapping, and passed with the arithmetic that puts a big page's address together broken, because the whole program sat in the page's first 4 KiB, which every page puts in the same place. It stores some way into the big page now. Fifteen tests. Those that passed when written are each held by a mutation: the three faults by `stval` left unwritten, by a store's fault thrown as a load's and by `medeleg` left unwritten, the direct-access one by the probe taking direct access back (8220 transactions against 2048). The test assembler has a pool of 64-bit constants (`riscv::Assembler`), which is how a program names an address in the top half. ⚠️ **For step 3**: writing `satp` does not empty the TLB, which the specification leaves to software, and Linux does it. ⚠️ **For M20**: a reset leaves `satp` and the TLB as they were. The specification promises nothing of `satp` after a reset, and OpenSBI writes it before it hands over. ⚠️ **For M26**: the asmjit backend fetches with a debug read, which the MMU now translates as a load and not as a fetch. **Not done**: a page of 2 MiB, which Linux maps its kernel with and which is the same code as the 1 GiB one a level down; a page fault in the second half of an instruction across two pages; Sv48 and Sv57.
- **Step 3: the bits were right on a walk, and one of them was not looked at on the way back.** The MMU takes a page fault at a page that is not marked accessed, or not marked dirty when it is written, and at a use the entry's other bits do not allow, and `sstatus.SUM` and `MXR` each lift the one fault they are for. All of that passed when written: six tests in two tables, and six mutations of the checks in `virt2phys`, each of which fails exactly one of them. The hole was as planning read it. A translation is kept once the tables have been walked for it, and a store that found one kept was not asked about the dirty bit, so a load from a clean page and then a store to it went through. `tlb-dirty` has a store that finds its translation kept look at the dirty bit it was kept with. 🎓 **Which kernel would have met it**, from the design review's reading of Linux 6.12: not one writing a file back, which takes write permission away as it cleans the page, and a write to a page that may not be written was stopped either way. A page read back from swap is mapped writable and clean (`do_swap_page`), and so is one given up with `MADV_FREE`: there the store would have gone through unseen, and the page been dropped as clean with what was written to it. Linux executes `sfence.vma` for a page once it has marked it, so the kept translation does not fault for ever. The review also read what else a kept translation skips, and it is nothing: the other checks of a walk are all made before a translation is kept, and whose page it is and what may be done there are worked out again at every use from the mode and `sstatus` as they then are, so neither a change of mode nor of `SUM` needs the TLB emptied. **`sfence.vma`**: the test holds the half that is a promise, that a changed entry is seen after it. That it is not seen before is what a TLB does and not what the specification asks, so nothing pins it; with `sfence.vma` made to forget nothing, the test fails. **The interrupt**: it arrives at a load that is a walk of three loads and one more, the load completes and the handler runs once. With the line M13's patch took out put back, this test and M13's own fail together. The paging platform has a device that can interrupt for it. 💡 **A mutation put back has to look newer than the build that had it.** One run after a mutation was undone still failed: the saved file was moved back with its old time, the build saw nothing to do, and the mutation was still in the program. The script that makes them touches the file now. **Not done**: `sfence.vma` for one address or one address space, which empties everything here as the specification allows; `sfence.vma` in user mode, which is step 4's if it is anyone's; and the one check in one place that [upstream.md](upstream.md) proposes, where the patch adds a second.
- ⚠️ **Step 4: user mode worked, and the way into and out of a trap had five faults in it, one of them on the cores Zephyr runs on today.** A system call from user mode, a read of `sstatus` there, and a program on a user page under paging that calls, loads from the kernel's page and jumps into it: all as they should be, and held by mutations. **Then what reading found.** (1) User mode could execute `sret` and `mret`: `leave_trap` never asked which mode the hart was in, so a program could put itself in whatever mode the last trap had come from. Planning had this as a note, and no step asked for it until the test of what user mode may not execute was being written. (2) And `sfence.vma`. (3) **The one that would have stopped Linux.** An interrupt that is pending when the instruction it arrives at raises an exception was kept as pending, and taken one instruction into the exception's handler, with interrupts off there and whether or not it was that mode's to take. The test has supervisor mode call machine mode with an `ecall` while a delegated interrupt is pending, which is every call a kernel makes of OpenSBI while its timer is due: unpatched, machine mode's handler was entered again by the supervisor's interrupt, over `mepc` and `mcause`, and the supervisor never ran on. After any trap is taken, what is pending is now worked out again for the mode the hart is then in. ⚠️ **Found by the design review: the hart with machine mode only has the same fault**, and [upstream.md](upstream.md) had excused it since M21 with "Zephyr on the host makes no `ecall`", which is not so: `irq_offload` is one. A test on the plain core was red in the same way, and the same line mends it. Nothing of Zephyr's had failed for it, which says how narrow the window is and not that it was shut. (4) `sret` under `mstatus.TSR` raised its illegal instruction and threw it away, as read. (5) `mstatus.MPRV` stayed set across a return to a mode below machine mode. Neither of the last two is on Linux's road, since OpenSBI sets no `TSR` and clears `MPRV` itself, and both are a line. 🎓 **What the test review caught**: the interrupt test was first named for an order, the call and then the interrupt. The specification has an interrupt outrank an exception, so a real hart takes the interrupt at the `ecall` and makes the call afterwards, and this interpreter finishes the instruction first. Both are a hart's own affair, and the test holds what is not: the monitor finds its registers as the call left them, and the interrupt is taken once, by the mode it belongs to. It also had a trap to supervisor mode made to clear `SPP`, where the test had found it clear because nothing had ever set it. **The patch script learnt two things**: to rebuild a patch that has later ones stacked on it, by taking those out for the diff and putting them back, and that a list of nothing is still a list. **Not done**: `wfi` in user mode, which the specification makes illegal on a hart with supervisor mode unless it ends in bounded time; the interrupt arriving at a system call from user mode, which is the same line of the core and has no way to be timed from a test, since user mode cannot mask an interrupt and then let it through; and the one stale case the review named, an instruction that masks the very interrupt that arrives at it.
- **Step 5: the two registers were stubs that let everything through, and now they are asked.** As planning read it: `mcounteren` and `scounteren` read as zero whatever was written and nothing looked at them, so every mode read every counter. A table of reads that are allowed, a table of reads that are not, each of the second allowing every counter but the one it reads or allowing that one in one register only, and the two registers written and read back: every refusal was red, and the read-back. The check went into the hart's read of a register, ahead of the callback that answers for it, because socpuppet's adapter answers for `time` from the machine timer and a check in DBT-RISE's own `time` would never have been reached. 🎓 **What makes it work on purpose under Linux**, from the design review's reading of both: OpenSBI writes all ones to `mcounteren`, and Linux allows its programs `time` in `scounteren` and nothing else, so a program's `rdcycle` traps there, as it does on a chip. **What the test review asked for**, which the plan had asked for too: the allowed rows show that a read did not trap and not what it read, so a hart that let the read through and answered zero would have passed. `time` read from user mode on a CPU connected to its timer is a test of its own now, with M13's program dropped to user mode first: one tick behind the timer's own count read by the next instruction, as machine mode sees it. It fails with the timer left unconnected. And machine mode reading all three with nothing allowed is three rows, which fail when machine mode is made to ask. **Not as planned**: the registers keep every bit that is written, where the plan said three and the specification says thirty-two, since nothing reads the rest. **Not done**: the `hpmcounter`s, which read as zero and are refused or allowed by the same check; and `csrrwi` with `x0` as its destination, which reads the register where the specification says it must not ([upstream.md](upstream.md)).
- ⚠️ **Step 6: a kernel could not have seen the floating-point state at all, never mind trusted it.** The plan had two things, that an instruction traps with `FS` off and that its first use marks `FS` dirty. Reading the masks before writing the first test found a third under both. **`sstatus` had no `FS` in it.** It is supervisor mode's view of `mstatus`, made by a mask, and the mask had bits 17 to 22 where the specification has `SUM` and `MXR`, 18 and 19, and nothing for the state of the floating-point registers. So a kernel read `FS` as off whatever it was and could not write it, and could set for itself `MPRV`, `TVM`, `TW` and `TSR`, which are the monitor's. `sstatus-view` puts the mask right. ⚠️ It broke two tests of step 4 on the way, and that was the interesting part: machine mode had only ever been able to write those four bits because the wrong mask was added to its own, which has none of them. They are in machine mode's now, on a hart with supervisor mode. **Then the plan's two.** The interpreter is generated code instantiated on the bare register set, and cannot see `mstatus`: a first attempt at a check where it dispatches an instruction did not compile. But every instruction it executes it first fetches through the hart, and the hart can see both. So the hart looks at what was fetched: an instruction of the F or D extension is an illegal one with `FS` off, at the right address for a kernel to switch floating point on and return to, and leaves `FS` dirty otherwise. `SD` had been kept and never cleared, and masked out of every read, so nothing had ever seen it: it is worked out from `FS` when either register is read. `fp-status` is the hart's own file and nothing generated. 🎓 **What Linux needs of it**, from the design review: it gives every program floating point from the start, so the trap is not on its road, and it saves a program's registers when it stops running it only if `FS` says dirty, which is. **`flw` and `fsw`** cut their address to 32 bits where `fld` and `fsd` do not: two casts, in generated code, each held by a test of its own once the review had the one test made two, since the load's fault had hidden whether the store worked. **What the reviews changed besides**: the gate for `fcsr` was first in the code all three harts share, where a debugger's read cannot be told from a program's, and a debugger attached at reset would have been told the hart has no `fcsr`; it is in the hart's `read` and `write`, as step 5's check is. Six kinds of floating-point use had no row and have one each, held by taking its case out of the check. And a test of supervisor mode's view could pass with supervisor mode trapping before it ever read: it asserts the call that follows the read now. ⚠️ **For M26**: the check is made where the interpreter fetches, and a backend that translates blocks reads its instructions another way, so under one floating point would again be never off and never dirty with no test to say so ([upstream.md](upstream.md)). **Not done**: an instruction that only reads a floating-point register marks the state dirty too, which the specification allows and a kernel pays for with a save it did not need; `UXL` in `sstatus`; and the vector extension's state, which the same checks would have to learn.
- ⚠️ **Step 7: the atomics were right, and the reservation under two of them was not one reservation.** The plan had this step down as cheap: one hart, so a reservation is never contended. Reading the core for it found what contends with a reservation on one hart, which is a kernel. DBT-RISE kept a reservation for every address a hart had loaded reserved, in a map, and a store-conditional looked up and removed its own address and no other. The specification gives a hart one reservation, and has any store-conditional end it whether or not it stored. 🎓 That second half is what Linux uses: a program interrupted between its `lr` and its `sc` must not finish the pair when it runs again as if nothing had happened, so on its way out of every trap the kernel executes an `sc` to its own stack, to give up whatever the hart holds. Here that `sc` gave up nothing. If what happened in between was a plain store or an `amoadd` to the same word, the program's `sc` then stored over it. Two rows were red, by direct access and over the bus: an `sc` to the reserved cell after one to another cell, and an `sc` to a cell that was reserved before another was. `one-reservation` has an `lr` replace what the hart held and an `sc` empty the map once it has looked. The other nine uses passed when written and each is held by a mutation of the core that fails its rows and no others, both ways: an `sc` that always finds a reservation, an `lr` that reserves nothing, and one for each of the four operations. **What the test review changed**: one suite named "the result and the cell are as the instructions say" became three named for what they hold; the row for an `sc` with another cell reserved looked at a cell no instruction had aimed at, and was turned round; and the 32-bit `or` could not tell 32 bits from 64 until its cell had something in the upper half. ⚠️ **For the 32-bit boards**: the hart their cores are built on has the same map, unpatched. No image of ours executes an `lr` or an `sc`, since Zephyr's atomics are the compiler's `amo*`, and Zephyr has no `sc` on its way out of a trap to rely on. **Not done**, each in [upstream.md](upstream.md): a reset leaves the reservation; the reservation is on the virtual address; and `lr` with `x0` for its destination makes none.
- **Step 8: the traps were there, and one of them had the wrong name.** A load or a store of a word at an odd address traps, with the instruction in `mepc` and the address in `mtval`, and with the two causes handed down supervisor mode takes them. All of that passed when written, and is held by three mutations: a misaligned load let through, a store that does not say its address, and a `medeleg` that cannot take the two causes. An `amoadd.w` at an odd address raised the load's cause, because it reads before it writes, where the specification has the store's, so that whoever makes misaligned loads on a program's behalf does not take an atomic for one and make half of it. The first plan for this step was to write that down and leave it, since nothing on the road executes one. The test review asked for the row or for the plan to change, and the row was cheap: the hart already looks at each instruction where it is fetched, for step 6, so it remembers whether the last was an atomic memory operation, and refuses its misaligned read as a store. 🎓 **Who catches these, which the plan had half right**, from the review's reading of both: OpenSBI does not hand the two traps down. It takes them in machine mode and makes the access itself from `mepc` and `mtval`. Linux from 6.16 asks OpenSBI for them and does the same in its own handler. So the test with a handler that makes the store a byte at a time stands in for Linux's, and the test of what machine mode is told stands in for OpenSBI's, and which one M20 meets depends on the kernel it pins. **Not done**: a handler that reads the instruction to learn its width and its register, as both real ones do (this one is told); and a misaligned fetch, which a hart with compressed instructions cannot make.
- **After step 8, one more test, for M23.** OpenSBI makes a misaligned access on a kernel's behalf by reading the kernel's memory from machine mode, with `mstatus.MPRV` set, so that its loads and stores are made as the mode it was entered from would make them: through the kernel's page tables. M23's exit test runs exactly that, and nothing here had. Machine mode, entered by a call from supervisor mode under paging, sets `MPRV`, loads from an address only the tables lead to and stores to another. The core did it, and the test fails when `MPRV` is made to change nothing.
- **Step 9: one domain is enough, and nobody tells it where to send.** Read on 2026-10-12 in the sources of OpenSBI v1.9, Linux v7.2 and QEMU v11.1.2, and not run. 🎓 An APLIC can be split into **domains**, one for each privilege level, with the machine-level one handing sources down to the supervisor-level one, which is what QEMU's `virt` has. socpuppet's APLICs are one domain each. **Both accept one domain described as supervisor-level**, its `msi-parent` the supervisor's IMSIC node. OpenSBI decides a domain's level by following `msi-parent` to an IMSIC and looking at which of the CPU's interrupts that IMSIC names, 11 for machine mode or 9 for supervisor mode (`fdt_parse_aplic_node`). It resets every APLIC it finds whatever its level (`domaincfg` to zero, every source disabled), and writes the address messages go to, and the delegation of sources to a child, only into a machine-level one. It needs no APLIC source of its own: its console is polled. Linux in message mode drives every APLIC node that is not disabled, with no check of level at all (`aplic_probe`), and never writes the address registers outside a build for machine mode. ⚠️ **The catch**: so with one supervisor-level domain nobody writes where its messages go. OpenSBI writes that only for a machine-level domain, and Linux leaves it to firmware. The model sends to what `mmsiaddrcfg` holds, which would be zero. QEMU has the same case when it runs under KVM, where it builds the supervisor-level APLIC alone, and answers it by telling the model the address when it is made (`riscv_aplic_set_kvm_msicfgaddr`). M23 does the same: a setting of `sp.Aplic` for where its messages go, used when the description is for Linux. 💡 It is the shape QEMU gives a guest under KVM and not the shape of a machine with firmware in machine mode, where the root domain is machine-level and hands sources down. Two domains with delegation is the faithful step after, and it is what OpenSBI's `aplic_init` is written for.
- **Step 10: the docs.** [The CPU's page](models/dbt-rise-cpu.md) says what the core with supervisor mode gives an operating system, what it leaves out (Sv48 and Sv57, address-space identifiers, marking pages itself, making a misaligned access), where to read paging, and what was changed by kind and not by count: the counts in it, in `THIRD_PARTY_NOTICES.md` and in [development.md](development.md) had each gone stale at a different number, so none of the three has one now. [development.md](development.md) has how a patch to a borrowed project is made when others are stacked on the same file.

Order: 0 is done; 1 first, because it is M13 step 7 again and the pattern is known; then 2, 3 and 4 in that order, each on the pages of the one before; 5, 7 and 8 any time after 1; 6 after 2, for its address above 4 GiB; 9 any time; 10 last. Zephyr's suites are run again under twister after step 8, the last patch to the core, since Zephyr runs on the same hart. Left open: `CONFIG_USERSPACE` on the `_mu` hart (a to-do, and nearer once step 4 has seen a hart's user mode work); bit 3 of `mip` and the OR that bit 9 wants, from M21 step 1; and everything about speed, which is M26's.

Exit: each of a to g is a test that fails on the unpatched core where the core is wrong and passes on the patched one, with every patch an entry in [upstream.md](upstream.md).

What Linux will ask: all of the above, which is why it is here.

### M26 — The fast hart (time-boxed spike; only M20 waits on it)

A kernel's boot to a prompt is hundreds of millions of instructions. The host's core is DBT-RISE's interpreter, which ran a counted loop at 31 to 44 million instructions a second in M1's spike ([iss-spike.md](iss-spike.md)), with no paging, and that report said of its own figure that a loop of two instructions flatters an interpreter. Nobody has measured what paging costs it, what any other way of running the same instructions gives, or what a boot will take. This spike finds out, and it is a spike because the answer may change what socpuppet ships.

🎓 An interpreter reads one instruction, works out what it is, does it, and goes round again. A **translating** core, a **JIT**, turns a run of the guest's instructions into the host's own once and then runs that, which costs more the first time and much less every time after. DBT-RISE has three translating backends beside its interpreter (asmjit, LLVM and TinyCC), and QEMU, which is what QBox puts inside a SystemC simulation, is a fourth translator of its own.

The question, as the maintainer put it on 2026-10-12: socpuppet may want two CPUs. One that is good enough and goes in the wheel, so that `pip install socpuppet` boots Linux with nothing else to build. And one that is fast, offered beside it and built from source, which is what QBox would have to be, since QEMU is GPL-2.0. Whether that is so, and which CPU is which, is what the report recommends.

- **The harness comes first, and stays.** A test with the label `speed`, outside the `dev` preset as the spikes' are, that times with the host's clock and prints a table and asserts nothing about time: the counted loop of M1's spike, a loop that walks page tables (pages touched in turn under Sv39, the TLB emptied each round, so that every access is a walk), and the same loop with the TLB left alone. M1's harness went with the spike (its last commit is in [iss-spike.md](iss-spike.md)); the loop itself is `riscv::CountDownThenSleep`.
- **The rows.** DBT-RISE's interpreter at the default quantum. Each translating backend that can be built here. QBox, behind the CPU slot as M1 had it. For each: the three loops, whether it passes the CPU's tests and the bus-master contract, and what it adds to the build and to the wheel.
- **The projection.** How many instructions a boot to a prompt is, counted on QEMU's `virt` machine where QEMU can count them, and that number at each row's rate.
- **What is not in it**: making any of them the default, which is a decision; QBox as a supported optional CPU, which is the to-do it already is and becomes a milestone if the report says so; and the 32-bit boards, whose firmware is small.

What planning found, reading the backends on 2026-10-12 when this was item h of M22:

- **asmjit makes code for x86-64 and nothing else.** DBT-RISE's asmjit backend is written against asmjit's x86 compiler (`iss/asmjit/vm_base.h` includes `asmjit/x86/x86compiler.h`, and `vm_rv64gc.cpp` names `x86::` 465 times). It registers `rv64gc_msu`, the host's core. It cannot run on an Apple silicon Mac, which this is, or on two of the wheel's three runners.
- **TinyCC has no core that could be the host's.** `src/vm/tcc/` has one file, for DBT-RISE's `tgc5c`; nothing of it is registered with the SystemC wrapper; DBT-RISE-Core fetches TinyCC on Linux only; and it is LGPL.
- **LLVM has the core and does not offer it.** `src/vm/llvm/vm_rv64gc.cpp` exists. `register_cores.cpp` registers `rv32imac_m`, `rv32imac_mu` and two `tgc5c` cores for LLVM and no 64-bit one, so asking for `rv64gc_msu:llvm` wants a few lines of patch first. LLVM makes code for the machine it runs on, so it is the one backend of DBT-RISE's that can be measured here. It is found as a system package (`WITH_SYSTEM_LLVM`, LLVM 19) or through Conan, and is a large thing to link into a wheel of two megabytes.
- **No translating backend has a core with physical memory protection**, which is what the 32-bit boards run (`rv32imac_mp`). Whatever is chosen for the host, they stay on the interpreter.
- **The backend is a parameter nobody sets.** DBT-RISE's `core_complex` has `backend`, `"interp"` by default, and makes its core from the core's name and that. socpuppet's adapter sets the core's name and leaves it.
- **The helper the backends share is left out of every build, not only the Mac's** (`cmake/Dependencies.cmake` removes `vm_jit_funcs.cpp`), for the `wait()` that collides with POSIX ([upstream.md](upstream.md)). A backend needs it back, with the helper renamed, which is what that entry proposes.
- **QBox measured 455 million instructions a second** on the counted loop behind M1's CPU slot, in its repeatable configuration, and about 3,800 million threaded, which is not repeatable. Its recipe in `spikes/iss/qbox/` fails at configure today, on a tag of QEMU's that is not published; M12's spike builds the commit before it (`spikes/npu/hexagon/build.sh`). What it still needs to be a CPU of socpuppet's is in its [to-do](#to-do).
- ⚠️ **A translating backend fetches another way.** asmjit reads its instructions with a debug read in the interpreter's space for instructions (found by M22 step 2's design review), which the MMU translates as a load since that step: it wants a page readable where a fetch wants it executable, and it does not stop at the end of a page. A backend that is to run a kernel has that to settle first. And for the same reason it would not see M22 step 6's check of `mstatus.FS`, which is made where the interpreter fetches: under it floating point would be never off and its state never dirty.
- **M1's "16.8 million" was iterations.** The loop is two instructions, so the figure was 33.5 million instructions, and the rates are right.

What planning found on 2026-10-13, when it was planned:

- **The question is where to do the work, as well as what ships.** Asked by the maintainer: the spike is a synthetic benchmark, not a boot, and its first answer is which machine, which environment and which CPU the development that gets Linux booting (M23, M25, M20) is best done on. The two decisions about what ships come second. So every row is measured on every machine it can run on, and each machine's development loop is timed beside it: a build from nothing, a build after touching one model, and the `dev` suite.
- **There are three machines, and two of them are x86-64.** A 2019 Mac Pro (Intel Xeon W-3223, 16 threads, macOS), a MacBook Air (Apple M4, 10 cores, macOS) and a Ubuntu 26.04 box (AMD Ryzen 5 7600X3D, 12 threads, GCC 15). Each has Docker: the Mac Pro's runs x86-64 Linux, the Air's runs arm64 Linux and, through Rosetta, x86-64 Linux. So asmjit, which makes x86-64 code only, can be measured after all, on five of the seven environments: both Macs' x86-64 containers, the Mac Pro and the Ubuntu box natively, and the Ubuntu box's container.
- **The environments have fixed names**, so that a result says where it came from: `native-macos-x86_64`, `docker-amd64-on-macpro`, `native-macos-arm64`, `docker-arm64-on-air`, `docker-amd64-rosetta-on-air`, `native-linux-x86_64` and `docker-amd64-on-ubuntu`. The Ubuntu box's container is the same hardware as its native column with CI's compiler, GCC 13, so it tells the compiler from the machine.
- **LLVM registers its core under another spelling.** `vm_rv64gc.cpp` registers `rv64gc|msu_vp|llvm`, where the SystemC wrapper looks up `rv64gc_msu:llvm`, so a patch of two lines adds the second. DBT-RISE-Core links a target called `llvm-core::llvm-core` that only DBT-RISE-RISCV's `WITH_SYSTEM_LLVM` makes, from a path written into it, `/usr/lib/llvm-19`. Ours is made from the LLVM that `find_package` finds. The Mac Pro has Homebrew's LLVM 22 and the Ubuntu box has 21 and can have 19, so LLVM 19 goes first.
- **The helper's `wait` is named in four files across two repositories**: DBT-RISE-Core's `vm_jit_funcs.h` and `.cpp`, the asmjit core, which takes its address, and the LLVM core, which looks it up by its name in a string. The rename is two patches.
- **QBox does not need Docker.** M1 built it in a container so that nothing was installed on the Mac. On the Ubuntu box it builds from the packages its Dockerfile lists, natively; on the Macs it runs in their containers.
- **The projection needs no kernel built.** Debian's riscv64 installer publishes a kernel and an initrd, and QEMU's `virt` boots them with `rdinit=/bin/sh`, so the first process is the shell. Under `-icount shift=0` QEMU's clock is a nanosecond an instruction, so the last timestamp in `dmesg` is the count of instructions to the prompt, with no plugin, which is as well: Ubuntu's QEMU ships none.
- **Nothing new ships by default.** The backends are behind a CMake list, `SOCPUPPET_DBT_RISE_BACKENDS`, empty by default, and the build chooses the backend a CPU runs with, `SOCPUPPET_DBT_RISE_DEFAULT_BACKEND`, `interp` unless it says otherwise. That is how one build directory runs the whole existing suite under a backend with no test touched. ⚠️ Planning had it a parameter of the CPU's, and step 3 found that a parameter in the registry is a number: a backend is a name, so it is the build's, and a C++ caller can still name one to `DbtRiseCpu`.
- **The time box is M1's**: four patches of about twenty lines and six attempts a step, for each backend. LLVM 22 gets two hours once 19 works. A translating backend that fails under paging gets an hour to name the cause, and then its row says so and the counted loop, which pages nothing, still gives its rate.

The steps. Only step 3 is a `/tdd` session: the harness is a spike's, in `spikes/speed/`, and stays, unlike M1's, since every later milestone that changes the core wants to know what it cost. Every patch to the borrowed core is still a file in `cmake/patches/`, a line in `cmake/Dependencies.cmake` and an entry in [upstream.md](upstream.md).

| # | Step | Where |
|---|---|---|
| M26 0 | ✅ **The plan.** This table and what planning found. Not a `/tdd` step | `docs/plan.md` |
| M26 1 | ✅ **The harness.** Three programs made of `riscv_program.h` and nothing else: the counted loop (`CountDownThenSleep(1 << 24)`, 33.5 million instructions), a loop in supervisor mode under Sv39 that loads from 32 pages in turn and empties the TLB each round so that every load is a walk, and the same loop with a `nop` where the `sfence.vma` was. One test for each, timed with the host's clock, asserting only that the program finished and that it ran by direct memory access, and printing one line of JSON. A script runs them three times in a named environment and keeps every line in `spikes/speed/results/`. Not a `/tdd` step | `spikes/speed/`, `CMakePresets.json` |
| M26 2 | ✅ **The interpreter on every machine, and the development loop with it.** All seven environments, three runs each, and in each the build from nothing, the build after touching `dbt_rise_cpu.cpp`, and the `dev` suite's wall time. M1's 44 million instructions a second on the Air is checked first. Not a `/tdd` step | three machines, `spikes/speed/results/` |
| M26 3 | ✅ **The CPU's `backend` parameter.** With nothing said the CPU is what it was. `interp`, named, runs a program.
