A backend this build has not got is `std::invalid_argument` from the constructor, naming the ones it has (red: DBT-RISE ends the simulation) | `models/dbt_rise_cpu.*`, `cpu_test.cpp`, `cmake/Dependencies.cmake`, `docs/models/dbt-rise-cpu.md` |
| M26 4 | ✅ **asmjit.** Fetched with `OVERRIDE_FIND_PACKAGE`, the helper back in the build with `wait` renamed. The three loops, the CPU tests and the bus-master contract with it as the default, and the host's boot from Python, in the five x86-64 environments. Not a `/tdd` step | `cmake/Dependencies.cmake`, `cmake/patches/`, [upstream.md](upstream.md) |
| M26 5 | ✅ **LLVM.** Found with `find_package(LLVM CONFIG)`, its core registered under the name the wrapper looks up. LLVM 19 on the Ubuntu box first, then 22 on the Mac Pro, then the containers. The same tests as step 4. Not a `/tdd` step | the same |
| M26 6 | ✅ **QBox**, at the commit M12's spike builds: natively on the Ubuntu box, in the containers on the Macs, and one attempt natively on the Mac Pro. The three loops behind M1's CPU slot, repeatable and threaded. Not a `/tdd` step | `spikes/iss/qbox/` |
| M26 7 | ✅ **The projection**, boxed at an hour: the count of instructions to Debian's kernel's shell on QEMU's `virt`, and that count at every rate measured. Not a `/tdd` step | `spikes/speed/boot_count.py` |
| M26 8 | ✅ **The report and the plan.** The tables made from `results/` by a script, [speed-spike.md](speed-spike.md) opening with the setup to develop on, then the two decisions. Here: the marks, what the steps found, Status and the to-dos. Not a `/tdd` step | `spikes/speed/collect.py`, `docs/speed-spike.md`, docs |

Order: 0, 1, 2 and 3 in that order; 4 and 5 after 3; 6 any time after 1; 7 just before 8.

What the steps found, as they went:

- **Step 0: the plan.** Written on 2026-10-13.
- **Step 1: the harness.** Three loops and a runner, `spikes/speed/run.sh`, that does one environment's column in a build tree of its own and keeps every line in `spikes/speed/results/`. 💡 The default quantum changes nothing for the interpreter: the counted loop runs at the same rate at 100 µs and at M1's 1 ms. ⚠️ The extension is built into `python/socpuppet/`, whichever tree built it last, so the runner keeps the one that was there and puts it back.
- **Step 2: the interpreter on every machine.** All seven columns, three runs or more each. **The Air is the fastest machine at everything**, natively: 41 million instructions a second on the counted loop, 24 on the walking one, a build from nothing in 75 s, a rebuild after touching the CPU's adapter in 4 s, and the `dev` suite in 61 s. The Ubuntu box is second (33 and 20 million, 104 s, 9 s and 66 s), and its container is the same to within a few per cent, so GCC 13 against GCC 15 makes no difference. **The Mac Pro is last at everything**: 17 and 7 million, 180 s, 19 s and 171 s natively, and in its container the build takes 473 s and the suite 267 s. 🎓 Its C++ tests take 146 s where the Ubuntu box's take 4 s and the Air's 9: each test is a process of its own, and starting one costs far more there. The Air runs the x86-64 container under Rosetta, with an emulated CPU, faster than the Mac Pro runs natively (24 million against 17). Paging costs the interpreter about 40 % when every load walks the tables, and a quarter when the TLB hits. M1's 44 million on the Air is 41 now, after M22's fifteen patches.
- **Step 3: the backend is the build's, not a parameter's.** A parameter in the registry is a number, and a backend is a name. So `DbtRiseCpu` takes one from a C++ caller and refuses one the build has no core for, naming those it has (red: DBT-RISE ended the simulation), and the registry passes `SOCPUPPET_DBT_RISE_DEFAULT_BACKEND`, `interp`, which configuring refuses to be anything else until a backend is built. 🎓 **Found by the design review**: a default of `asmjit` would reach the 32-bit cores and the plain one, which no translating backend has a core for, so the build's default has to give way to the interpreter there. That and the test that a CPU asked for `interp` by name runs, which says nothing while every CPU runs the interpreter, are step 4's.
- **Step 4: asmjit, four patches.** The JIT helper's `wait` is `iss_wait` in Core and in both generated cores that name it; the generated cores' `__int128` traits are guarded for libc++; `wfi` called the helper with a 1 where the core's pointer goes. On the x86-64 machines it runs the counted loop four times faster than the interpreter (133 million instructions a second natively on the Ubuntu box, 69 on the Mac Pro) and the walking loop twice as fast, and fails 29 of the hart's tests and pytest, on everything M22 mended in the interpreter: the floating-point state (11), interrupts and the IMSIC (9), the reservation (4), a fetch from an unmapped page and one across a page's end (3), misaligned accesses (2). Under Rosetta on the Air it is twice the interpreter on straight code and no faster under paging, and its suite could not be completed: ctest died at the same test in three runs. ⚠️ A test that hangs on a backend outlived ctest's timeout: four were found five hours later at 100 % of a core, under the next column's timings, and `run.sh` sweeps them now.
- **Step 5: LLVM, five patches, versions 19 to 23.** The core registered under the name the wrapper looks up, its JIT initialised, block arguments typed as pointers where LLVM 19 refused bytes, `wfi` as in asmjit, and for LLVM 23 `CreateGlobalStringPtr` given back to the builder in ten lines of Core where the generated cores call it 661 times. Debian's package wants `FindPkgConfig` loaded for it. It is the one translating core every machine has, and the fastest: 169 million instructions a second natively on the Air against Homebrew's LLVM 23, 141 in its arm64 container, 123 on the Ubuntu box, 68 to 77 on the Mac Pro. It fails 21 of the hart's tests, the same groups as asmjit without the interrupts, plus `flw` and `fsw` through an address above 4 GiB, which M22 mended in the interpreter's generated code only. Under it a debugger finds the host's core elsewhere than where its firmware starts, and in the Air's container six Zephyr examples do not reach their greeting. The extension links LLVM's shared library: 1.5 MB more on macOS, and a dylib the wheel cannot assume.
- **Step 6: QBox, on the Ubuntu box natively and in the Air's container.** It wanted virgl and virCL off (QEMU's bundled virclrenderer does not compile against glibc 2.42, and Homebrew has no epoxy by default) and the NPU spike's fmt patch, since it builds its Hexagon components for every target. Repeatable: 350 to 360 million instructions a second on the counted loop, 580 to 590 on the hits loop, nine times the interpreter, and 23 to 27 million on the walking loop, below every DBT-RISE core but the Mac Pro's interpreter, since QEMU throws its translations away at each `sfence.vma`. Threaded crashed on every loop. Natively on the Macs it stopped at Homebrew's packages, one at a time (`libzip`, `epoxy`, `sdl2`), with the attempt after `sdl2` and the Mac Pro's container build still running when M26 closed.
- **Step 7: the projection, in the hour.** Debian's riscv64 kernel with its 41 MB initrd reaches the initrd's shell in 1,849 million instructions (six boots, QEMU 10.2 on Ubuntu and 11.1 from Homebrew on the Air, all within 1 %), which plain QEMU does in 2 to 3 s. At the Air's interpreter that is 45 s at the counted rate and 78 s at the walking rate; at its LLVM 11 and 35 s; on the Mac Pro's interpreter 111 and 262 s. 💡 A kernel's rate lands between a cell's two columns and nearer the first, since it fences only at a change of address space.
- **Step 8: the report.** [speed-spike.md](speed-spike.md), with the tables from `spikes/speed/collect.py`. The recommendation is the Air, natively, on the interpreter, with LLVM on the same machine when a boot is too long to wait for; the interpreter stays the wheel's CPU; nothing faster is offered beside it until a backend passes the hart's suite. ⚠️ The spike went past M1's allowance for LLVM by one patch, at the maintainer's ask for Homebrew's LLVM on the Air. Still running at the close, each a line to add to `results/` and a cell in the report: QBox natively on the Air (after `sdl2`) and in the Mac Pro's container, QBox's second and third runs, and the Mac Pro's own boot count.

Exit: a report, [speed-spike.md](speed-spike.md), with the table, the projection, and a recommendation on three things, which the maintainer decides from it: the machine, environment and CPU to do the Linux work on, the CPU that ships in the wheel and is the host's default (the row in [Decisions deliberately left open](#decisions-deliberately-left-open)), and whether a faster one is offered beside it.

What Linux will ask: a boot that someone will wait for.

### M27 — QBox, the second hart (optional, built from source)

M26's report timed QBox at 350 to 370 million instructions a second on straight code, nine to twenty times the interpreter, and found that it builds and runs repeatably in three of the seven environments: natively on the Ubuntu box and in the Linux containers on both Macs ([speed-spike.md](speed-spike.md)). It also found what it cannot do today: none of socpuppet's tests can be run against it, because it lives behind the CPU slot M1's spike built for it, in a tree of its own with a SystemC of its own. This milestone makes QBox a CPU of socpuppet's, so that the hart's suite, the bus-master contract and the Python boots hold it to the same marks as the default.

🎓 QBox is [Qualcomm's](https://github.com/qualcomm/qbox) library for putting QEMU's CPUs inside a SystemC simulation. QEMU does not interpret: it translates blocks of the guest's instructions into the host's and runs those, which is where the speed comes from, and it has booted every Linux there is. That is the second reason for this milestone, and the bigger one. The Linux work (M23, M25, M20) will hit bugs that could be the firmware's, a device's or the hart's. With two harts to run the same boot on, a boot that works on QEMU's and not on DBT-RISE's says which, and that is a diagnosis the interpreter alone cannot give. QBox is a diagnostic hart beside the default, not a replacement for it: QEMU is GPL-2.0, so it is built from source and never in the wheel, which M1's gate decided and this milestone does not reopen.

What planning found on 2026-10-14:

- **One process, one kernel.** socpuppet links SystemC 3.0.2 as a static library so that a binary holds exactly one kernel (`cmake/Dependencies.cmake`). QBox forces shared libraries and pins SystemC at commit `febde7a3` (2025-12-02, after the 3.0.2 release), built with `ENABLE_SUSPEND_ALL` and `ENABLE_PHASE_CALLBACKS`, and pins CCI beside it. Its boilerplate uses a SystemC that is already found before it fetches one, so either side can be made to agree. The maintainer's decision: socpuppet moves to QBox's SystemC commit if that is what it takes. This is the gate of the milestone, and everything else waits on it.
- **The two odd sockets have landings in QEMU.** `DbtRiseCpu` reads `time` through a socket to the machine timer, and reaches its IMSIC's registers through `csr`. QEMU's RISC-V CPU reads `time` through a callback the machine sets (`riscv_cpu_set_rdtime_fn`) and routes `miselect`, `mireg`, `mtopei` and the supervisor three through another (`riscv_cpu_set_aia_ireg_rmw_fn`). Neither is exported by QBox's libqemu today, so reaching them is a patch to its export list, in the house manner.
- **The sleeping CPU is the known trap.** A QBox CPU with nothing to do suspends the kernel with it (that is what `ENABLE_SUSPEND_ALL` is for), so simulated time stops and `Platform.run()` with no time limit never returns; M1's wrapper kept a tick going. A `wfi` has to cost time and nothing else.
- **Time is coarse.** QBox's deterministic configuration runs QEMU as a coroutine on the SystemC thread and counts instructions for time, in quanta of its own quantum keeper. M1 found the simulated time at the end of the counted loop did not match one instruction a tick. Tests that pin a timer's interrupt to a simulated time are where this shows.
- **The debugger is QEMU's.** `DbtRiseCpu` has a GDB port of its own. QEMU has a stub of its own, with a protocol of its own, so the debugger tests are the ones most likely to be skipped with a reason rather than passed.
- **Natively on the Macs it is Homebrew whack-a-mole.** `libzip`, `epoxy`, `sdl2`, then `epoxy/egl.h`, which Homebrew's libepoxy has not got on macOS. QEMU's OpenGL display is nothing a CPU needs, and `-Dopengl=disabled` is the next thing to pass through. The containers on the Macs work and are the fallback.
- **Four patches already.** The speed spike's recipe (`spikes/speed/qbox/build.sh`) renames QBox's `semaphore.h` and two `Name<T>(...)` constructors for C++20, takes the NPU spike's Hexagon `fmt` patch, and configures virgl and virCL off. They become files in `cmake/patches/` with entries in [upstream.md](upstream.md) when the build moves out of the spike.
- **CI cannot carry it on every push.** QBox and its QEMU build in four minutes on the Ubuntu box and far longer in the Macs' containers. Its suite runs on demand and nightly, in a job of its own, with the container that M26's Docker wrapper already has.

The steps. Steps 3 to 6 are `/tdd` sessions; the rest is build work and docs. Every change to QBox is a patch file, a line in the build and an entry in [upstream.md](upstream.md).

| # | Step | Where |
|---|---|---|
| M27 0 | ✅ **The plan.** This table and what planning found. Not a `/tdd` step | `docs/plan.md` |
| M27 1 | ✅ **One kernel, the gate.** Boxed at two days. socpuppet's SystemC pin moves to QBox's commit, built with the two options QBox wants, and the build gains a way to link SystemC shared when a QBox is in it. QBox is pointed at that SystemC and CCI rather than fetching its own. Done when one binary holds socpuppet's platform layer and a QBox CPU, elaborates, and runs M26's counted loop with the same number as the spike's. If two days do not get there, the milestone stops and the plan says why | `cmake/Dependencies.cmake`, `cmake/patches/`, `spikes/speed/qbox/` |
| M27 2 | ✅ **The build, from source, behind an option.** `SOCPUPPET_QBOX_DIR` names a QBox built by `tools/build_qbox.sh` (the spike's recipe made a tool, tested in `tests/tooling/`): the pin, the four patches as `cmake/patches/` files, virgl and OpenGL off, RISC-V only. `.devcontainer/Dockerfile` gains the packages. Nothing ships: the wheel's build does not set the option, and the docs say what the option costs and that QEMU is GPL-2.0 | `tools/build_qbox.sh`, `cmake/`, `.devcontainer/Dockerfile`, `docs/upstream.md` |
| M27 3 | ✅ **`QboxCpu` through the bus-master contract.** The adapter in `src/socpuppet/models/qbox_cpu.{h,cpp}`: the bus socket with DMI, `reset`, `irq`, `timer_irq` and `supervisor_irq`, the reset vector and `xlen`, the same shape as `DbtRiseCpu`'s. Held to `tests/cpp/contracts/bus_master_contract.h` first, which is what a CPU is held to now, and built only when the option is set | `src/socpuppet/models/qbox_cpu.{h,cpp}`, `tests/cpp/contracts/bus_master_test.cpp` |
| M27 4 | ✅ **A sleeping hart costs time and nothing else.** `wfi` with nothing pending advances simulated time to the next event and the kernel keeps running; `Platform.run()` with no time limit returns when nothing is left to do, as it does on the default. Then `time` through the socket by QEMU's `rdtime` callback, exported from libqemu by a patch, and the quantum set from the platform's, so that a timer interrupt lands at the simulated time the machine timer says | `qbox_cpu.cpp`, `cmake/patches/`, `tests/cpp/platform/cpu_test.cpp` |
| M27 5 | ✅ **The hart's suite.** `cpu_test.cpp`'s fixtures take the CPU's type, so that the same hundred and ten tests run on both harts with no test touched, and the `speed` suite's QBox row comes from the adapter instead of the spike's slot. The IMSIC's `csr` socket by QEMU's AIA register callback, exported by a patch. Each test's fate on QBox is recorded: a pass, or a skip that says why (the debugger tests, which would be QEMU's stub, are the expected skips). A failure QEMU's RISC-V core is wrong about is an entry in [upstream.md](upstream.md), and a patch if it is small | `tests/cpp/platform/cpu_test.cpp`, `spikes/speed/`, `cmake/patches/` |
| M27 6 | ✅ **Python names it.** A registry entry, `qbox_cpu`, and `socpuppet.QboxCpu` with `DbtRiseCpu`'s fields, refused with a message that says how to build it when the option was not set. The host board takes its CPU's kind, so that `tests/python/test_host_board.py` and the twister run boot Zephyr on QBox with the same tests, selected by a fixture | `src/socpuppet/platform/`, `src/socpuppet/bindings/`, `python/socpuppet/`, `tests/python/` |
| M27 7 | ✅ **On demand and nightly.** A workflow that builds QBox in the container and runs the contract, the hart's suite and the Python boots on it, on a schedule and on request, never on every push; `uv run ctest --preset qbox` runs the same here. Its result is the row the report of M26 could not fill | `.github/workflows/`, `CMakePresets.json` |
| M27 8 | ✅ **The docs and the plan.** `docs/models/qbox-cpu.md`: what it is, what it costs, what it leaves out and what to use it for (the second opinion on a boot). `docs/development.md`: the build recipe and the option. [speed-spike.md](speed-spike.md): the QBox row from the adapter. Here: the marks, "What the steps found", the status, the to-do items, and the open-decision row. Not a `/tdd` step | `docs/` |

What is not in it: making QBox the default, which M1's gate settled; QBox's Hexagon, which is M19's question; QBox natively on the Macs past the next Homebrew package, which gets one boxed hour in step 2 and then the containers; and QEMU's threaded configuration, which crashed everywhere in M26 and is not repeatable.

What the steps found, as they went. All of it was done on the A51, a Ubuntu 26.04 box with an Intel Core Ultra 9 285K (24 cores) and GCC 15, over ssh, on 2026-10-14 and 15.

- **Step 0: the plan.** Written on 2026-10-14.
- **Step 1: the gate, in an afternoon, not two days.** QBox's SystemC and CCI are shared libraries, and socpuppet links them when `SOCPUPPET_QBOX_DIR` names an install; without it, SystemC is fetched at the same commit, `febde7a3`, and linked statically as before. QBox's two options are no-ops at that commit, so the pin moved and nothing else. Three things had to give. QBox installs SystemC's headers beside its fmt 9 and its own `scp/report.h`, so the SystemC targets are pointed at a directory of links to SystemC's and CCI's headers alone, and only the adapter's one source file sees QBox's include directory, first. A shared `libsystemc` refers to `sc_main()`, so every program and the extension get a stub. And SCC skips its own CCI when QBox's is there, then links a target nobody defines unless told CCI is found. 💡 QBox's reporting library and SCC's `scp4scc` both define one function of SCP's logging, and the dynamic linker gives QBox SCC's; the two structs are laid out alike, so it is harmless today ([upstream.md](upstream.md)). In one program with socpuppet's platform, SCC and DBT-RISE, QBox ran the counted loop as fast as alone, and all 928 tests passed on both trees. A tree pointed at a second install kept linking the first: CMake caches where it found each package, and now forgets them when the option changes.
- **Step 2: `tools/build_qbox.sh`.** The pin, the three C++20 patches as files, RISC-V only, virgl and virCL off, each patch applied once whatever reruns, and the packages it stops without named. 453 s at twelve jobs. Tested with stand-ins for git, cmake and pkg-config. The devcontainer's image has QBox's packages. ⚠️ The Mac's hour was not spent then: QBox stayed a container on the Macs, as the plan allowed, until after M27 (below).
- **Step 3: the contract asked for three things QBox does not do.** A CPU asleep in `wfi` held simulated time still: its interrupt event is attached as suspending, which only threads need, and a patch detaches it when QEMU is a coroutine. Every access arrived with QEMU's instruction count as its lead, quantum or none, and a target's delay was forgotten: QBox keeps the CPU's time in QEMU's clock, which never hears of it. The adapter lets the clock catch up when a lead passes the quantum. ⚠️ A delay inside the quantum is still lost, which [the model's page](models/qbox-cpu.md) says. All twelve scenarios pass. A 32-bit `qbox_cpu` is refused; the registry has the name in every build, and a build without QBox says how to get one.
- **Step 4: `time`, by QEMU's hook.** Without one, `rdtime` is an illegal instruction in QEMU. libqemu exports `riscv_cpu_set_rdtime_fn` and QBox's `CpuRiscv` calls it, two patches, and the adapter answers from the timer with QBox's own lead. 💡 M26's 1 ms of simulated time for 33.5 million instructions was QBox run with no quantum: from memory it reaches directly, its CPU never waits for SystemC's clock. At the boards' 100 µs it keeps time.
- **Step 5: the hart's suite, 151 cases, on both harts.** `cpu_test.cpp` adds the CPU `SOCPUPPET_CPU` names. On QBox 123 failed first, most for want of `supervisor_irq`; then the adapter had to let a line its own access changed be heard before the next instruction, as DBT-RISE's patched core does (a handler ran 33,332 times); `csr` came by QEMU's AIA hook, in the same two patches; and QBox's reset handler must be called from a thread, and must be told of a reset that comes back while it waits. What is left is 37 fates in one table (`tests/cpp/support/qbox_fates.h`), skipped on QBox with their reasons and named by what each case is: the 32-bit tests; DBT-RISE's 100 ns an instruction; what the specification allows either way (misaligned loads and stores, accessed and dirty bits set by the hart, `wfi` in user mode, `satp`'s ASID with Sv48 and Sv57); `miselect` and `siselect` with no IMSIC, which QEMU keeps; and three for upstream: atomics to memory without direct access, which spin, the cause of a misaligned atomic, and a reset while busy. The speed spike's loops run on the adapter: about 280 million instructions a second counting, 610 through pages it had walked, 18 walking.
- **Step 6: Python.** `sp.QboxCpu` beside `sp.DbtRiseCpu`, both on `RiscvCpu`, which holds what they have alike. `host(cpu=)` takes the simulator's name, and with none, the one `SOCPUPPET_CPU` names, so one variable runs a whole suite's boots on the other hart, and `socpuppet run --cpu` the same. The debugger is DBT-RISE's, and the GDB tests name that CPU. On QBox the Python suite passes but for one fate, marked `not_on_qbox`: the watchdog's period measured across a boot from the ROM, which copies the image across the link, and which QBox's CPU is not charged the same for each time. QBox's own warnings were quieted, since they came ahead of a run's first line.
- **Twister, which no step planned for, decided the clock.** At QEMU's default of a nanosecond an instruction, Zephyr's timer and scheduler suites ran out of twister's time: firmware that waits by spinning spins a hundred times as many instructions. QEMU counts in powers of two of a nanosecond, and 128 ns, the nearest to DBT-RISE's 100, put the timer suite through in 3 s. Then every scenario of twister's list on the host board passed on QBox's CPU, 1,036 test cases, with the four the list skips skipped. It also put the Python suite through three times as fast, and one of its two timing fates went with it.
- **Step 7: the workflow.** `.github/workflows/qbox.yml`, nightly and by hand, on CI's runner with QBox's packages rather than in the container: the same Ubuntu and GCC, with no image to build. Its first run, started by hand when M27 was pushed on 2026-10-15, passed every step in 45 minutes on CI's GCC 13: QBox built in 15 (cached after), socpuppet in 7, the whole suite on QBox's CPU in 2, clang-tidy in 7, and twister's suites on the host in 8.
- **Step 8: the docs.** [models/qbox-cpu.md](models/qbox-cpu.md), with the fates; [development.md](development.md) and [setup.md](setup.md) with the tool, the preset and the variable; [speed-spike.md](speed-spike.md) with the adapter's figures on the A51; [upstream.md](upstream.md) with twelve new entries for SystemC, QBox and QEMU; and the A51's processor corrected in setup.md, which had it as AMD.
- **After M27: QBox on the Mac Pro, on 2026-10-15, in Docker and natively.** `tools/devcontainer.sh` runs any command in the devcontainer's image, which it names after the Dockerfile's hash so that a change builds it again, and on a Mac it keeps QBox's two trees on Docker volumes where the preset looks for them. With it the whole suite passed on QBox's CPU in the Mac Pro's container: QBox built in 10 minutes and the suite ran in 5½. Then the boxed two hours went on the native build, and took 55 minutes. It wanted QBox's own tests off (they look for LLVM only under Apple silicon's Homebrew, and socpuppet never runs them, so `tools/build_qbox.sh` now turns them off everywhere) and four patches. The first is OpenGL off on Apple machines, with stubs for the nine functions libqemu's SDL display takes from it, since QEMU's OpenGL needs EGL and Homebrew's libepoxy has none on macOS. The next two make rpclib's own asio found before Homebrew's, in QBox's copy and in QEMU's: Apple's clang searches `/usr/local/include`, where an Intel Mac's Homebrew lives, ahead of every `-isystem` directory. The last gives QBox's libraries `@loader_path` where they had `@executable_path`, so that QBox finds QEMU from inside Python. From nothing, QBox built natively in 9 minutes and the whole suite passed in 4, every C++ test and every Python one, with the same 37 fates. Linux builds what it did. Then the Air, an Apple silicon Mac, with nothing changed: natively, QBox built from nothing in 5½ minutes, socpuppet in 2, the whole suite passed in 69 s with the same 37 fates, and twister's suites on the host board on QBox's CPU in under 3 minutes, all 1,036 test cases. In its container, which is arm64 Linux, QBox built in 7 minutes and the suite passed in 2. The nightly workflow became a matrix with `macos-15`, Apple silicon, natively, so that something holds the Mac's four patches to a build; clang-tidy and the header check stay on Linux, as in ci.yml. Its first run, by hand on 2026-10-15, passed both legs: the Mac's in 23 minutes with nothing cached, QBox's build included, and Linux's beside it in 33.
- **After M27: one workflow, on 2026-10-16.** The nightly was folded into `ci.yml`, and QBox's jobs run on every push and pull request. What made the nightly necessary was QBox's build, a quarter of an hour on CI's runner; it is now a job of its own, `qbox-build`, which builds and caches QBox only when its pin or its patches change, so a push pays for socpuppet's QBox tree, the suite and twister on the host board, on Linux and on a Mac. Every job of the workflow is a wrapper around `tools/ci.py`, which runs the same jobs here with one command, and `tests/tooling/test_ci_workflow.py` keeps the two to the same jobs. `firmware/build.sh` became `firmware/build.py` the same day, in parts that fetch the SDK, its LLVM and Zephyr, and build the boot ROMs, OpenSBI, Zephyr's images and EDK2, so that CI builds the four kinds of image side by side, each cached on what it is built from. Then the rest of the bash went too: `tools/build_qbox.py`, `tools/devcontainer.py`, `tools/test_wheel.py` and `firmware/twister.py`, each with its tests, so that only the spikes keep a shell script.

### M23 — OpenSBI boots

**OpenSBI** is the machine-mode firmware a RISC-V kernel sits on. **SBI**, the Supervisor Binary Interface, is the set of calls a kernel makes of it: set the timer, write to the console before it has a driver of its own, send an interrupt to another hart, reset the machine. OpenSBI reads the devicetree, sets up what machine mode must (delegation, the timer, the interrupt controllers' machine-mode halves), and jumps to the next stage in supervisor mode with the hart's id in `a0` and the devicetree's address in `a1`. Its `fw_jump` build jumps to an address where somebody else has put the kernel; its `fw_payload` build carries the kernel inside it. It also ships `test_payload`, a supervisor-mode program of a few hundred bytes that prints through SBI's console call, which makes it the first thing to run in supervisor mode on the puppet.

- **Pinned and built as the firmware is.** OpenSBI's version joins `firmware/versions.sh`, and `firmware/build.sh` builds `fw_jump` and `test_payload` for the host with the Zephyr SDK's gcc, as it builds the ROM.
- **A devicetree in Linux's bindings, from the same description.** `platform.devicetree()` writes what Zephyr's bindings want. Linux's differ where M13 and M14 wrote down: the IMSIC is `riscv,imsics`, one node for each privilege level, with no `riscv,hart-id`; the APLIC's interrupts have two cells where Zephyr's have three; the CPU node needs `mmu-type = "riscv,sv39"`; the system controller wants a `syscon-reboot` node on top; the ACLINT is named by what OpenSBI looks for; `chosen` has `stdout-path` and `bootargs`; and the UART is `ns16550a`. The generator learns a second dialect, chosen by the caller, and nothing in the description changes. How that is shaped (a parameter, a second walk) is for planning to decide.
- **The root complex as Linux sees it.** Its node is `socpuppet,pcie` with a Zephyr driver of ours. If its configuration window and `ranges` are what Linux's `pci-host-ecam-generic` expects, the Linux dialect names that and Linux needs no driver of ours for M24; if they are not, M24 owes Linux a driver, and this milestone says which.
- **RAM.** The host has 64 MiB (`boards/host.py`), and a kernel with a root filesystem in memory wants more. The size becomes a parameter of `host()`, and the devicetree's `memory` node follows it.
- **The ROM passes `a0` and `a1`**, as OpenSBI expects of the stage before it: the hart's id and the devicetree's address. M14 noted the image header has room for an argument; this is the test that asks for it. The quick tier is `load_elf` of `fw_jump` through the CPU and `load_binary` of the payload and the devicetree where `fw_jump` expects them.
- **SBI's calls meet the models.** The timer call sets `mtimecmp` and the payload is woken; the console call comes out of the UART; the reset call goes through the system controller and the hart starts again at the ROM; a misaligned load in the payload is emulated by OpenSBI and the payload runs on. OpenSBI finds no PMP on `rv64gc_msu`, which it allows for, and the test sees it say so.

What planning found:

- **The devicetree generator already writes `riscv,isa-base` and `riscv,isa-extensions`** (`components.py`), which is Linux's binding as well as Zephyr's. The ISA string is not a dialect difference.
- **Two bindings were written down as Linux's by the milestones that met them**: the IMSIC's (M13 step 6) and the APLIC's cells (M13 step 12). The flash as `jedec,spi-nor` is M18's, and Linux's `mtd` is not needed to boot.
- **The 16550 is at QEMU `virt`'s address with `reg-shift = <0>`**, its eight registers a byte each in the adapter's view (the borrowed PULPino model has them four bytes apart), which is what Linux's 8250 driver takes with no `reg-io-width`. Zephyr's driver reads it that way today.
- **`a0` and `a1` are nothing today.** The ROM jumps with whatever the registers held.
- **One APLIC domain, described as supervisor-level, and told where to send** (M22 step 9, read in OpenSBI v1.9 and Linux v7.2 and not run). No second domain and no delegation. The APLIC's node for Linux: `compatible = "riscv,aplic"` after a vendor's, `reg`, `interrupt-controller`, `#interrupt-cells = <2>` where Zephyr's has three (a device's interrupt loses its priority cell), `riscv,num-sources`, and `msi-parent` the supervisor's IMSIC node. ⚠️ `sp.Aplic` gains a setting for the address its messages go to, since neither OpenSBI nor Linux writes one for a supervisor-level domain, and OpenSBI writes `domaincfg` to zero on its way past, which the adapter already holds in message mode.
- **Two IMSIC nodes, and the level is in which interrupt each names.** `compatible = "riscv,imsics"` after a vendor's (today's `riscv,imsic` is matched by neither), one node for each privilege level, `#interrupt-cells = <0>`, `msi-controller`, `#msi-cells = <0>`, `riscv,num-ids`, and `interrupts-extended` naming the CPU's interrupt 11 for the machine-level file and 9 for the supervisor's, which is the only place the level is said. No `riscv,hart-id`. OpenSBI sets up the machine-level one for itself and marks its node disabled before it hands the devicetree on, which is how Linux comes to see only the supervisor's. ⚠️ OpenSBI refuses an IMSIC whose `riscv,group-index-shift` is given and below 24, and a refusal there fails its whole scan of interrupt controllers.
- **What OpenSBI will do with the hart, which M22 ran**: it reads a kernel's memory with `MPRV` (the test after step 8), takes misaligned loads and stores itself from `mepc` and `mtval` (step 8), writes all ones to `mcounteren` (step 5), and is called by the kernel while the kernel's own interrupts are pending (step 4).
- **The hart has Sv39 and no other kind of paging.** M22 step 2 took Sv48 and Sv57 out of what `satp` will keep, since no test runs them. Linux asks for the biggest kind it knows and settles for what reads back, so it needs no telling, and the CPU's node says `mmu-type = "riscv,sv39"` because it is true.

What planning found on 2026-10-15, reading OpenSBI v1.9's sources and not yet running them:

- ⚠️ **OpenSBI decides whether to touch the IMSIC by reading `mtopi`, and this hart has none.** `hart_detect_features` marks a hart as having the AIA's machine-mode half, Smaia, if a read of `mtopi` does not trap, and `imsic_local_irqchip_init` does nothing on a hart without it. DBT-RISE's hart with supervisor mode registers neither `mtopi` nor `stopi`, which M22 step 1 wrote down as left out. OpenSBI would boot and leave the IMSIC alone, which hides nothing here, since its console is polled. Linux would not: its interrupt controller reads `stopi` to learn which interrupt it took. So M23 asks for both.
- **OpenSBI finds out what the hart has by trying it.** It reads and writes a list of registers (`pmpaddr0`, `mcountinhibit`, `menvcfg`, the `mhpmcounter`s, `stimecmp`, `mstateen0`, `tselect`, `mtopi`) under a handler of its own that steps past an illegal instruction. A register this hart does not have is one, so the probe works, and OpenSBI will call the hart version 1.10 of the privileged specification, since it has no `mcountinhibit`. It then writes `mcounteren` with all ones and `scounteren` with the timer's bit alone, delegates the supervisor's three interrupts in `mideleg` and the system call, the page faults and misaligned fetch in `medeleg`, and writes `satp` with zero. A count of no PMP regions fails nothing.
- **The supervisor's timer is a software interrupt.** OpenSBI takes the machine timer's interrupt itself and sets `mip.STIP`, the supervisor's timer bit, by writing it. The hart lets machine mode write that bit and looks again at what is pending when it does.
- ⚠️ **Two properties no reader has asked for yet, and OpenSBI needs both.** `/cpus` must say `timebase-frequency`, or `sbi_timer_init` waits for ever. And a CPU node with no `mmu-type` is marked `status = "disabled"` by `fdt_cpu_fixup` before the tree is handed on, so Linux would see no CPU at all. Both are facts about the hardware rather than a binding, and Zephyr's binding for a RISC-V CPU has `mmu-type` already.
- **The timer is an ACLINT `mtimer`** to OpenSBI: `riscv,aclint-mtimer`, its first `reg` `mtime` and its second `mtimecmp`, read and written 64 bits at a time, with `interrupts-extended` naming the CPU's interrupt 7.
- **The console is found from `stdout-path`**, which loses anything after a colon, and failing that is any node a driver of OpenSBI's knows. Its 8250 driver takes `ns16550` and `ns16550a` both, with `reg-shift` 0 and `reg-io-width` 1 when they are not given.
- **Reset and power-off are two nodes**, `syscon-reboot` and `syscon-poweroff`, each with `regmap` the phandle of a `syscon` node, an `offset` and a `value`. The system controller has a register that resets and none that switches off.
- **`test_payload` ends by switching the machine off.** It prints "Test payload running" through SBI's debug console, calls SBI's reset with a shutdown, and if that comes back says "sbi_ecall_shutdown failed to execute." and sleeps. With nothing to switch off, the second line is what a test would see.
- **`fw_jump` copies the devicetree before it hands it on**, to RAM plus 34 MiB (`FW_JUMP_FDT_OFFSET` on the generic platform), and grows it there by a kilobyte and more for its fixups: the machine-level IMSIC is marked disabled and a `reserved-memory` node keeps the next stage out of OpenSBI's own memory. The original at `a1` stays as it was. The next stage is expected 2 MiB into RAM, where Linux must be.
- **OpenSBI is built position-independent** and runs wherever it is put, and its ELF file says address zero unless `FW_TEXT_START` says otherwise.
- **The boot image's header has no word to spare.** Eight words and a name of 32 bytes fill its 64 bytes, and the ROM jumps with `a0` and `a1` as it found them.
- **The flash is 4 MiB, and the manager's image starts 2 MiB in.** `fw_jump`, 2 MiB of zeros to where the next stage goes, a payload and a devicetree come to a little over 2 MiB, so M23's flash boots a host that has no manager. M28's image is bigger, and the flash or the slot will have to grow then. M28 grew both: a flash of 16 MiB, the manager's image in its last 2 MiB.
- **A reboot in the quick tier would hang.** Nothing loads RAM again, and `fw_jump` entered a second time finds its choice of which hart boots already made, and waits for one that never comes. A reboot is the ROM tier's to test.
- **A `Rom` takes debug writes until its CPU first reads it** (`models/rom.h`), and Python's `load_elf` insists that an image starts where its CPU does, which `fw_jump`'s first stage cannot, because the stage before it has to set two registers.
- **Zephyr's APLIC driver writes where messages go only if it reads zero there**, and takes anything else for an address the hardware was built with. The APLIC's contract pins the zero. So a setting of where messages go cannot be on every host, or Zephyr's interrupts would go to the page machine mode never reads.
- **The SDK's gcc ignores a linker script given as `-Wl,-T`** (`firmware/build.sh`), which is how OpenSBI's Makefile gives its own.
- **`dtc` is installed on CI's Linux test runner and not on its Mac.**
- **The model's UART says it has no FIFO**, which is a 16550 and not a 16550A, so its name stays `ns16550` in both dialects. Linux's 8250 driver and OpenSBI's take that name.
- **QBox's hart differs from DBT-RISE's in four places this milestone meets**: it has PMP, it has Sstc (so OpenSBI sets the supervisor's timer through `stimecmp`), it makes misaligned loads itself, and it has `mtopi` and `stopi` of its own. ⚠️ The nightly found the Sstc one the hard way: QEMU times `stimecmp` with its own ACLINT, which it took the adapter for, and divided by zero. A patch gives QBox's CPU a parameter for Sstc and the adapter switches it off, so both harts set the supervisor's timer through `mtimecmp` ([upstream.md](upstream.md)).

**Decided on 2026-10-15, planning M23.**

- **OpenSBI v1.9, built by `firmware/build.sh`** with the Zephyr SDK's gcc, as the ROM is: the generic platform, `FW_TEXT_START` at the start of RAM, `FW_JUMP` for `fw_jump` and `FW_PAYLOAD` for `test_payload`, which is what that option builds. The generic platform's offsets stay.
- 🎭 **The quick tier is QEMU's reset vector in the ROM**, and not `load_elf`, as the bullets above had it. `fw_jump` wants the hart's id in `a0` and the devicetree's address in `a1`, and nothing in Python can set a hart's registers. QEMU's `virt` machine has the same problem and answers it with six instructions at `0x1000` that read `mhartid`, load two words after them and jump (`reset_vec`, in `hw/riscv/boot.c`). `boot.reset_stub` writes those six instructions for the host's `Rom`, which a test pokes before the first run. Both tiers are then a host with a ROM, and they differ in what the ROM holds and whether the flash is read. `FW_FDT_PATH`, which builds the devicetree into `fw_jump` so that `load_elf` would do, was turned down: it makes the firmware's build depend on the Python that describes the board, and M20 hands its kernel a command line at run time.
- **The hart answers `mtopi` and `stopi`**, by a patch with its entry in [upstream.md](upstream.md).
- **The devicetree's dialect is a parameter of `platform.devicetree()`**, and only names, cells and the shapes of nodes are in it. `mmu-type` and `timebase-frequency` are true of the hardware and are written in both, so the host's checked-in devicetree gains those two lines and nothing else.
- **`sp.Aplic(messages_to=)` says where an APLIC's messages go before anyone writes it, and `host(sbi=True)` uses it**, for a host whose firmware is a monitor with an operating system below it. A host without it is as it was, for Zephyr's sake.
- **The system controller gains `POWER_OFF`**, a register that holds its CPU in reset for good, so that SBI's shutdown has somewhere to go. `test_payload`'s last line is then silence and not a failure. Linux's `poweroff` and EDK2 will want it too.
- **The header gains `ARGUMENT_LOW` and `ARGUMENT_HIGH`**, and the ROM jumps with the hart's id in `a0` and the argument in `a1`. What it says on the console does not change.
- **`host(ram=)`, 64 MiB unless it is said.** With a ROM, less is refused: the ROM's stack is at the top of 64 MiB, and a ROM is written once, for a chip.
- **A payload of ours makes the calls `test_payload` does not**: the timer, a misaligned load, the devicetree and a reboot. It asks for a reboot after a power-on and to be switched off after a reboot, so the story from the flash ends.
- **The devicetree is compiled when the test runs**, by `devicetree.compile`, which runs `dtc`. CI's Mac installs it.

The steps, each one `/tdd` session unless marked. A fix to the borrowed core is a patch in `cmake/patches/`, a line in `cmake/Dependencies.cmake` and an entry in [upstream.md](upstream.md). A test that passes the first time is checked with a mutation.

| # | Step | Where the behaviour goes |
|---|---|---|
| M23 0 | ✅ **The plan.** This table and what planning found and decided. Not a `/tdd` step | `docs/plan.md` |
| M23 1 | ✅ **OpenSBI is pinned and built as the ROM is.** `opensbi_version` in `firmware/versions.sh`; `firmware/build.sh` fetches it and builds the generic platform with the SDK's gcc, and leaves `fw_jump` and `test_payload`, each an `.elf` and a `.bin`, beside the other images, where CI's lists find them. Tests of what it leaves: `fw_jump` is a 64-bit image that starts at the start of RAM, `test_payload` starts 2 MiB in, and `fw_jump`'s bytes end before that. ⚠️ The entry is what would say the linker script was ignored. CI's Mac installs `dtc` | `firmware/versions.sh`, `firmware/build.sh`, `tests/python/test_opensbi_images.py`, `.github/workflows/ci.yml` |
| M23 2 | ✅ **The hart answers `mtopi` and `stopi`.** Each is the highest-priority interrupt that is both pending and enabled, in the order the specification gives (11, 3, 7, 9, 1, 5), as its number sixteen bits up and a priority of one, or zero. Machine mode with its external interrupt pending and enabled reads 11 (red: illegal instruction); with nothing enabled, zero; supervisor mode with its external interrupt pending, enabled and delegated reads 9 in `stopi`, and still 9 with its timer's pending as well; user mode's read is an illegal instruction; the core with machine mode only has neither | `cmake/patches/dbt-rise-riscv-topi.patch`, `cpu_test.cpp`, `riscv_program.h` |
| M23 3 | ✅ **An APLIC can be built knowing where its messages go, and the host can be described for a monitor and with more RAM.** `sp.Aplic(messages_to=address)` is what `mmsiaddrcfg` and `mmsiaddrcfgh` hold out of reset, zero unless it is said: a source that fires with nothing written there sends to that address (red: to zero), the registers read it, and a write still wins; an address that is not a whole page is refused. `host(sbi=True)` builds both APLICs to send to the supervisor's page, and a host without it is as it was. `host(ram=)` sizes the RAM and its node, and with a ROM, less than 64 MiB is refused | `components.py`, `models/aplic.*`, `boards/host.py`, `aplic_test.cpp`, `test_aplic.py`, `test_host_board.py` |

In both: `timebase-frequency` in `cpus` from the timer the CPU reads `time` from, and `mmu-type` for a CPU with supervisor mode; the host's devicetree is generated again. In Linux's: the root's `model`; `chosen` with `stdout-path` and `bootargs` and nothing of Zephyr's; the UART a `serial` node; the timer `riscv,aclint-mtimer`; the APLIC's interrupts with two cells. `devicetree.compile` gives the blob, by `dtc`, and says how to install it when there is none | `devicetree.py`, `components.py`, `platform.py`, `cli.py`, `test_devicetree.py`, `test_linux_devicetree.py`, `socpuppet_host.dts` |

The host's tree in Linux's dialect passes `dtc` | `components.py`, `devicetree.py`, `test_linux_devicetree.py`, `test_host_board.py` |

A program that stores its two registers finds the hart's id and the argument there, started by the ROM (red: what the registers held) and by the stub, on a 64-bit CPU and a 32-bit one; an image with no argument hands over zero | `regs/boot_image_header.rdl`, `boot_image.py`, `firmware/rom/main.c`, `boards/boot.py`, `cli.py`, `test_boot_image.py`, `test_rom_handover.py` |

The exit's quick tier: the banner; the platform named after the devicetree's `model`; the UART and the ACLINT at 10 MHz as its console and timer; the hart's extensions with `smaia` and `ssaia`; no PMP; "Test payload running"; then the CPU switched off and nothing more said. Each is a line a test waits for. From the flash: the ROM and the banner twice, a software reset the second time, and then off | `firmware/sbi_payload/`, `firmware/build.sh`, `test_m23_exit.py` |
