Here: the marks, what the steps found, the map, the open decisions and Status. Not a `/tdd` step | docs |

What the steps found, as they went. All of it was done on the Intel Mac, with the firmware built in the devcontainer's image, on 2026-10-15.

- **Step 0: the plan.** Written on 2026-10-15.
- ⚠️ **Step 1: the SDK's linker cannot make OpenSBI.** OpenSBI runs wherever it is put by being linked as a position-independent executable, refuses to build without a linker that can make one, and the Zephyr SDK's GNU linker is built for bare-metal programs and cannot. The same SDK release has an LLVM toolchain whose linker can, for any target, so OpenSBI is built with that: 260 MB to download and two gigabytes unpacked, kept beside the SDK and not in it, so that what twister copies and CI caches of the SDK does not carry it. The SDK's clang refuses flags it has no C library for, and OpenSBI links none, so the refusal is turned off. 💡 The generic platform builds `fw_jump`, `fw_payload` and `test_payload` without being asked, and links the payload at address zero unless `FW_PAYLOAD_OFFSET` says otherwise; with it, the payload's ELF file says 2 MiB into RAM, where `fw_jump` jumps. `fw_jump.bin` is 271 KiB.
- **Step 2: the two registers were missing, as planning read.** Seven rows, each a program in machine mode that hands interrupts down, enables them, sets them pending and reads both registers: all red, an illegal instruction. The patch reads them from `mip`, `mie` and `mideleg` in the specification's order, and a mutation that swaps two places in the order fails two rows. The tests read only the interrupt's number, since QEMU's hart may give another priority than this one's 1. ⚠️ **Found by the design review**: the hart itself takes the lowest-numbered of a mode's interrupts first, against its own comment, so with the supervisor's software and external interrupts both pending it takes the software one while `stopi` names the external. OpenSBI and Linux each ask the register until it reads zero, so nothing is lost, and [upstream.md](upstream.md) has it.
- **Step 3: as planned.** The register's high half is held by an address above 16 TiB, at the test review's asking, since the host's page would have read zero there whatever the adapter did. `host(ram=)` refuses less than 64 MiB with a ROM.
- **Step 4: the dialect is a keyword the hooks take, and two facts went into both.** `mmu-type` and `timebase-frequency` changed the three checked-in boards by four lines in all, and Zephyr's binding takes `riscv,sv39`. The UART is `serial@` in Linux's dialect, its node's generic name, and Linux's `chosen` finds it by path. ⚠️ The PLIC's interrupts keep Zephyr's two cells in both dialects, where Linux's binding has one: nothing that reads Linux's has a PLIC yet, and the code says so.
- **Step 5: as planned**, and two tests that probed the address just past the last register moved past the new one. The register map says `woset`, so that it is the map that says a zero changes nothing.
- **Step 6: one hook more, not fourteen.** A component that is more than one node in a dialect says so in `device_nodes`, which is `device_node`'s one node unless overridden, and only the IMSIC and the system controller override it. `msi-parent` names a page's node by the address the messages reach. The host's tree in Linux's dialect passes `dtc`.
- **Step 7: as planned.** QEMU's reset vector is copied less its line for `fw_dynamic`, which `fw_jump` does not read. A test whose answer is zero had the program's results filled with ones first, so that a program that never ran could not pass it.
- ⚠️ **Step 8: OpenSBI booted, its shutdown did nothing, and the bug was OpenSBI's.** The first boot by hand printed nothing in 200 ms, and a debugger with breakpoints along OpenSBI's boot (the tests' own GDB client and OpenSBI's symbols) showed it working through `sbi_hart_init`: it needed a second of the CPU's time, most of it the banner, a character at a time through the UART on the other die. The banner says what planning read: the platform named from the devicetree, the UART, the ACLINT at 10 MHz, `syscon-reboot` and `syscon-poweroff`, `smaia` found by `mtopi`, no PMP, version 1.10 of the privileged specification, and the IMSIC as the device it interrupts other harts with. Then `test_payload` asked to be switched off and nothing happened, not even its failure line. Breakpoints again: the shutdown reached `regmap_update_bits` and hung the hart. **OpenSBI's syscon regmap counts a block's registers and compares a register's offset in bytes with that count**, so with registers of four bytes only the first quarter of a block can be written, and the system controller's reset and switch are at 0xC and 0x10 of 32 bytes. QEMU's syscon has its one register at offset 0. `cmake/patches/opensbi-syscon-regmap-size.patch` mends it, with its entry in [upstream.md](upstream.md), and `firmware/build.sh` applies OpenSBI's patches to a clean tree every time. ⚠️ `ssaia` is not in OpenSBI's list of extensions, which has no name for it, so the exit test looks for `smaia` alone.
- **Step 9: the ROM checks OpenSBI's 2 MiB in 4.2 s of the CPU's time**, 3.2 s on the clock on the wall, and the whole boot from the flash to the payload is 5.3 s and 4.1 s. Every test of the exit runs both ways, and the Python suite on the Intel Mac went from three minutes to five.
- **Step 10: every call worked the first time it was made.** The timer's call has OpenSBI set the supervisor's timer bit, which the payload's handler takes in supervisor mode; the misaligned load is made by OpenSBI from machine mode through the payload's mapping (M22's test after step 8); the reboot goes through `syscon-reboot`, the ROM copies OpenSBI afresh, and the payload reads a software reset; then it is switched off. From the flash, with the reboot, that is 10.6 s of the CPU's time and 8.6 s on the wall. **Found by the design review**: the payload's wait for its timer could have slept for ever had the interrupt come between its look at the flag and its `wfi`, and the interrupt is now let in only after the `wfi`.
- **Twister after the last patch to the hart and the boards' new devicetrees**: every scenario of the list on the host board passed, all 1,036 test cases, with the four the list skips skipped, as before.
- **Step 11: the docs.** How to boot OpenSBI in [boot-your-firmware.md](boot-your-firmware.md); the pages of the CPU, the APLIC, the IMSIC, the UART, the timer, the root complex, the system controller, the ROM and the boot image; [development.md](development.md) and [setup.md](setup.md) for OpenSBI and the SDK's LLVM; the ROM's README.

Order: 0 is done; 1 first; 2, 3, 5 and 7 any time after, each on its own; 4 before 6, and 5 before 6, whose node for switching off names 5's register; 8 when 1 to 7 are done, since it is the first time all of them run together; then 9, 10 and 11. Zephyr's suites are run again under twister after 2, the last patch to the hart, and after 4, which changes the board's devicetree.

Exit: OpenSBI's banner on the console, naming the platform from the devicetree, and `test_payload`'s output after it, from the ROM and from the quick tier; the devicetree the generator wrote validates with `dtc` and is the one OpenSBI read; and the SBI timer, console and reset each seen to reach their model.

What Linux will ask: `fw_jump` with the kernel at the address it jumps to, and the devicetree that this milestone made. What UEFI will ask first: the same, with EDK2 where the kernel would be.

### M28 — UEFI boots

🎓 **UEFI**, the Unified Extensible Firmware Interface, is the firmware a PC or a server kernel is loaded by: it finds a kernel on a disk or the network, loads it, hands it a memory map and the devicetree (or ACPI tables), and stays resident for a few services, such as variables and the clock. **EDK2** is TianoCore's reference implementation, the one in every PC and the one QEMU boots as OVMF. Its RISC-V port (`OvmfPkg/RiscVVirt`, written for QEMU's `virt`) runs in supervisor mode as OpenSBI's payload, with the hart's id in `a0` and the devicetree's address in `a1`, which is exactly the contract a kernel takes. It has no PEI phase: a small **SEC** stage sets up a stack and the memory map, and the **DXE** core then loads a few dozen drivers, each saying so on the console, until the boot manager finds nothing to boot and lands in the **UEFI shell**, `Shell>`, which takes commands. Linux's kernel is itself a UEFI application when it is built with the **EFI stub**, which is how a server's firmware starts it.

What it rehearses that OpenSBI's test payload does not, and the kernel will, each read in EDK2's sources on 2026-10-15 and not run:

- **Paging, turned on from supervisor mode, by a program that was not written here.** `BaseRiscVMmuLib` builds an identity map of every region of RAM and MMIO it knows, with blocks of 1 GiB and 2 MiB where they fit, and turns it on by writing `satp` with Sv57, then Sv48, then Sv39, keeping the first that reads back. That probe is the one M22 step 2 patched the hart to answer, and the 2 MiB block is the page M22 left undone. It then runs the rest of its boot, and the shell, through those tables, with `sfence.vma` where it changes them.
- **A supervisor's trap handler with interrupts on.** `CpuDxeRiscV64` installs `stvec` and takes the timer's interrupt in supervisor mode, with the timer set through SBI's call (`CpuTimerDxeRiscV64`) and `time` read from supervisor mode by `mcounteren`'s leave (M22 step 5). Every tick is an interrupt delegated by OpenSBI, taken by the mode it belongs to (M22 step 4), and returned from with `sret`. ⚠️ Nothing of the AIA: EDK2 has no APLIC or IMSIC driver, so interrupts as messages wait for Linux.
- **The devicetree read whole.** `FdtClientDxe` takes the tree from `a1` and the drivers ask it: `memory` for RAM, `cpus` for `timebase-frequency`, `virtio,mmio` for devices, `chosen` for the console. The tree is M23's, in Linux's dialect, read by its second reader.
- **The 16550, through a driver that has only its address.** `SerialDxe` is built for a UART at `0x1000_0000` with a byte to a register, which is where socpuppet's is and how it reads (M23, what planning found). The console is `TerminalDxe` on top, which is what `Shell>` is typed at.
- **SBI's reset, from a shell command.** `reset` goes through `ResetSystemRuntimeDxe` to SBI's system reset, to the system controller, and the ROM starts again. `reset -s`, a shutdown, is the call that has no model to reach yet.
- **A boot timed.** M26 counted a kernel's boot at 1,849 million instructions and timed the interpreter at 17 to 41 million a second. Nobody has counted EDK2's; this milestone does, on the interpreter and on QBox, and writes it beside M26's figures. It is the first boot long enough to say whether the interpreter is where the Linux work can live.

What it needs of the platform that is not there, read in `RiscVVirtQemu.dsc` and `.fdf`:

- **An address.** EDK2 is linked to run from where it is loaded, a build-time constant (`CODE_BASE_ADDRESS` in `RiscVVirt.fdf.inc`), and QEMU's port has it at QEMU's flash, `0x2000_0000`. Here it is where `fw_jump` jumps, in RAM, which is one define. If the changes to QEMU's port grow past a handful, a platform description of our own (`Platform/Socpuppet/`, the shape every real board has in `edk2-platforms`) is the honest one, and this milestone says which.
- **Somewhere for variables.** UEFI keeps its boot entries and settings in a writable flash, and `VirtNorFlashDxe` wants a memory-mapped NOR flash to write them to. `sp.SpiFlash`'s window refuses a write (M14 step 8). The first answer is the store in RAM (`EmuVariableFvbRuntimeDxe`), which forgets at reset; the exit test needs nothing to survive one. 🎭 A NOR flash stand-in, or a write path through the SPI controller, is the step that makes a boot entry persist, and it is not this milestone's.
- **No `fw_cfg`.** QEMU's port takes its kernel, its ACPI tables and a few settings from QEMU through `fw_cfg`, a device no real board has. `QemuKernelLoaderFsDxe` and `AcpiPlatformDxe` must find none and say so quietly. 🚧 ACPI is for later: the devicetree goes to the kernel as a UEFI configuration table, which is what the Linux dialect is for, and what a RISC-V server without ACPI does today.
- **RAM.** The host has 64 MiB and M23 makes it a parameter. EDK2's own examples run with a gigabyte; the least it boots with is found here and written down.
- **A toolchain, and the build.** EDK2's `GCC5` build for RISC-V takes a cross-compiler prefix; whether the Zephyr SDK's gcc serves or the devcontainer wants Ubuntu's `gcc-riscv64-linux-gnu` is found first. `BaseTools` must be built, and the version is a tag (`edk2-stable*`, quarterly) in `firmware/versions.sh`, built by `firmware/build.sh` as the ROM and OpenSBI are: a fourth thing in it that is no Zephyr.

And how it is run:

- **Both tiers, as M23 has them.** The quick tier is `load_elf` of `fw_jump` through the CPU and `load_binary` of EDK2's flash image and the devicetree where `fw_jump` expects them. The ROM tier is as M23 settled it, and runs on demand: the image is a few megabytes, and the ROM checks them a byte at a time.
- 🧵 **Python at the keyboard.** `uart.output` and a write to the UART are what the Zephyr shell test uses (M13), and the UEFI shell is typed at the same way. `ver` says which EDK2; `memmap` says what RAM the devicetree gave and what paging mapped; `dh -p` and `devices` list the drivers that bound, which from M25 is where a virtio device first shows up to anyone but Python.
- **Every fault in the borrowed CPU is a patch and an entry**, as M22's were; every fault in EDK2's port that is ours (the address, the variables, `fw_cfg`) is a patch in `cmake/patches/` with its entry in [upstream.md](upstream.md), as every borrowed thing's is.

What planning found:

- **The devicetree is already the right one.** EDK2 wants the same bindings as Linux, and the one place it is stricter is none: the UART's address is a build-time constant and not read from the tree at all.
- **EDK2 brings drivers the later milestones can use as a second opinion.** `VirtioBlkDxe` and `VirtioNetDxe` over `virtio-mmio` from the devicetree (`VirtioFdtDxe`), for M25's stand-ins; `NvmExpressDxe` over `PciHostBridgeDxe`, which finds the drive through the root complex before Linux does, for M24, and is a second answer to M23's question about ECAM; a FAT driver and the EFI stub, which together are how a server loads its kernel from the **EFI System Partition**, the FAT partition every UEFI machine boots from.
- **What it leaves out of the rehearsal**: user mode (nothing in UEFI runs below the firmware), the AIA, floating point, and the second hart. Those stay the kernel's to find.

What planning found on 2026-10-15, reading `edk2-stable202608`, the latest stable tag, and building its `BaseTools`:

- **The port is PEI-less, and its SEC is generic.** `OvmfPkg/RiscVVirt` starts in `UefiCpuPkg/SecCore/SecCoreNative.inf` with a platform library of the port's own (`OvmfPkg/RiscVVirt/Library/PlatformSecLib`), and takes the hart's id in `a0` and the devicetree in `a1`, which is `fw_jump`'s contract exactly. Its stack and its first memory map are in 32 MiB at `0x8200_0000` (`PcdOvmfSecPeiTempRamBase`), the top half of the host's 64 MiB, so 64 MiB is the least it boots with unchanged. `fw_jump`'s copy of the devicetree, 34 MiB in, falls inside that, and SEC copies the tree out of it before it allocates anything large.
- **The address is a define on the command line.** `RiscVVirt.fdf.inc` says `DEFINE PFLASH0_BASE = 0x20000000`, QEMU's flash, and EDK2's `build` lets a `-D` override a define in an FDF. So `-D PFLASH0_BASE=0x80200000` links EDK2 where `fw_jump` jumps, and no platform of our own is needed: QEMU's port, built with a different number.
- **Variables in RAM are a PCD, not a driver.** `PcdEmuVariableNvModeEnable` makes the variable driver keep its store in memory and install both of its protocols, so the boot manager runs with no NOR flash. `VirtNorFlashDxe` finds no `cfi-flash` in the devicetree and installs nothing.
- ⚠️ **Two faults in the port when it runs from RAM on this board.** SEC puts a UART's registers in the memory map only if its node says `ns16550a`, and the host's says `ns16550` (M23: the model has no FIFO), so the page tables would leave the console out and the first print after `satp` would fault. And SEC hands DXE all of RAM as free, the 8 MiB EDK2 is running from among it, since on QEMU it runs from a flash outside RAM; ArmVirt's port reserves its own image, and this one must too. Each is a patch in `cmake/patches/` and an entry in [upstream.md](upstream.md). Step 3 found the second needs no patch yet, and it is an entry alone.
- **The console and the timer need nothing of ours.** The serial library is built for a 16550 at `0x1000_0000` with a byte to a register, and waits for the transmitter to be empty before it writes up to 16 bytes, which the model, always ready, takes. The timer library reads `timebase-frequency` from the devicetree, and the timer driver sets the next tick through SBI, since the port turns Sstc off whatever the hart has.
- **Paging is tried biggest first.** `BaseRiscVMmuLib` writes `satp` with Sv57, Sv48 and Sv39 and keeps the first that reads back: Sv39 on DBT-RISE, which M22 patched to keep nothing else, and Sv57 on QBox. RAM is mapped in 2 MiB blocks, the page size M22 never ran.
- **No `fw_cfg`, and no root complex without a drive.** The drivers that ask QEMU for a kernel, ACPI tables or a boot order find no `qemu,fw-cfg-mmio` and return; the devicetree is installed as a configuration table. `host()` with no drive has no root complex node, so the PCI host bridge finds nothing. NVMe under UEFI stays M24's.
- **The way to the prompt is two countdowns of simulated time.** The boot manager says "Press ESCAPE within 3 seconds for boot options", boots the shell, which is its only boot option, and the shell says "Press ESC in 5 seconds to skip startup.nsh" before `Shell>`. Eight seconds of the CPU's time spent in `wfi`, woken by the timer's interrupt in supervisor mode, which is exactly the rehearsal wanted. The console is a terminal, and its cursor moves are escape sequences in `uart.output`, which a test takes out before it reads.
- **The build.** The toolchain's name at this tag is `GCC`, not `GCC5`, with the cross-compiler's prefix in `GCC_RISCV64_PREFIX`; the Zephyr SDK's gcc is tried first, and the SDK's LLVM, which builds OpenSBI, is the second (`CLANGDWARF`). `BaseTools` is built with the machine's own gcc and wants `uuid-dev`. EDK2's `build` reads the include directories of every submodule a package declares, used or not, so all of them are fetched, each a single commit deep.
- **The image is 8 MiB**, an FD (a flash device image) padded to the size the FDF gives. With `fw_jump` and the 2 MiB before the next stage, and the devicetree after, that is about 10 MiB, which does not fit the 4 MiB flash, whose second half is the manager's. Step 1 found EDK2 fills 1.3 MiB of it, and builds it as 2 MiB.
- **Nothing counts instructions.** Neither CPU's adapter has a count, and each charges a fixed time an instruction: 100 ns on DBT-RISE, and on QBox 128 ns, its clock being the count (M27). The boot's count is then the CPU's time to `Shell>`, with both countdowns at zero, over that time an instruction, which is a count of instructions only if `wfi` is not waited in, and the countdowns are what wait.

**Decided on 2026-10-15, planning M28.**

- **EDK2 `edk2-stable202608`, QEMU's RISC-V port built at `0x8020_0000`**, with variables in RAM, by `firmware/build.sh` with its patches applied to a clean tree every time, as OpenSBI's are. `build/firmware/uefi.fd` is the image.
- **The boot flash is 16 MiB, and the manager's image moves to its last 2 MiB**, 14 MiB in. The host's image stays at the first byte.
- **Developed on QBox first, and tested on both.** The exit test runs on whichever hart `SOCPUPPET_CPU` names; CI's every-push job runs it on the interpreter in the quick tier, and the nightly runs it on QBox.

The steps, each one `/tdd` session unless marked.

| # | Step | Where the behaviour goes |
|---|---|---|
| M28 0 | ✅ **The plan.** This table and what planning found and decided. Not a `/tdd` step | `docs/plan.md` |
| M28 1 | ✅ **EDK2 is pinned and built as OpenSBI is.** `edk2_version` in `firmware/versions.sh`; `firmware/build.sh` fetches it with its submodules, applies its patches, builds `BaseTools` and the port, and leaves `uefi.fd` beside the other images. Tests of what it leaves: a firmware volume at the image's start, whose first stage is linked for where `opensbi.lay_out` puts it. CI's jobs that build firmware and the devcontainer install `uuid-dev` and `acpica-tools` | `firmware/versions.sh`, `firmware/build.sh`, `tests/python/test_uefi_image.py`, `.github/workflows/ci.yml`, `.devcontainer/Dockerfile` |
| M28 2 | ✅ **The flash holds OpenSBI and EDK2.** Their image is over 4 MiB. The boot flash is 16 MiB and the manager's slot 14 MiB in, and the image fits before it. The IO die's window is 32 MiB and the PCIe memory window after the flash, at `0x1120_0000`; the address map is generated again | `boards/cpu_kit.py`, `boards/host.py`, `test_uefi_image.py`, the drive's shield, `docs/address-map.md` |
| M28 3 | ✅ **EDK2 reaches its shell from the quick tier.** OpenSBI with EDK2 as its next stage, run until `Shell>`: SEC's first line, the drivers loading, paging configured, the boot manager's countdown and the shell's, then the prompt, at least as long after the first countdown as the two said they would wait. QBox first, then the interpreter. Each fault in EDK2 or the hart is a patch and an entry | `tests/python/test_m28_exit.py`, `cmake/patches/edk2-*.patch`, [upstream.md](upstream.md) |
| M28 4 | ✅ **Python types at the shell.** `ver` names EDK2; `memmap` gives the RAM the devicetree said | `test_m28_exit.py` |
| M28 5 | ✅ **And from the flash, with `reset` bringing the ROM back.** The layout as one image; the ROM's line, then `Shell>`; `reset`, and the ROM's line again | `test_m28_exit.py` |
| M28 6 | ✅ **The boot timed on both harts.** The CPU's time to `Shell>` with both countdowns at zero, the instructions that makes, and the wall time, beside M26's. Not a `/tdd` step | `docs/plan.md`, [speed-spike.md](speed-spike.md) |
| M28 7 | ✅ **The docs.** How to boot UEFI in [boot-your-firmware.md](boot-your-firmware.md); [development.md](development.md), [setup.md](setup.md), the CPUs' pages, [upstream.md](upstream.md); here, the marks, what the steps found and Status. Not a `/tdd` step | docs |

Order: 0 first; 1 and 2 each on its own; 3 after 1; 4 after 3; 5 after 2 and 3; 6 after 3; 7 last.

What the steps found, as they went. All of it was done on the A51, a Ubuntu 26.04 box with an Intel Core Ultra 9 285K, on 2026-10-15 and 16.

- **Step 0: the plan.** Written on 2026-10-15.
- ⚠️ **Step 1: the SDK's GNU linker cannot link EDK2 either**, as it could not link OpenSBI: EDK2 links every module with `-z notext`, which a linker built for bare-metal programs refuses. The SDK's LLVM links it, with EDK2's `CLANGDWARF` toolchain, in two minutes from nothing. Two more things the machine must have: libuuid's header for `BaseTools`, and `iasl`, ACPICA's compiler, for a RAM disk driver's ACPI table, which the script asks for by name before it downloads anything. The FD is 8 MiB as the port has it, and EDK2 fills 1.3 MiB of it, so it is built as 2 MiB, which is 6 MiB less for the ROM to check. The test of where it runs reads SEC's PE image out of the firmware volume: linked for `0x8020_0120`, 0x120 into the FD, where QEMU's build has `0x2000_0120`.
- **Step 2: a 16 MiB flash did not fit the IO die's window of 16 MiB**, which the PCIe memory window shared at 8 MiB in. The maintainer chose to keep the flash at 16 MiB and grow the window to 32 MiB, with the memory window moved past the flash to `0x1120_0000`. The flash is in no Zephyr devicetree; the drive's shield names the memory window, and a test compares it with the one generated. OpenSBI, EDK2 and the devicetree come to 4.2 MB as one image.
- **Step 3: QBox first, and one fault in EDK2's port.** The first boot stopped after `InitializeCpu`, with paging about to go on: SEC maps the UART only if it says `ns16550a` (`edk2-sec-maps-ns16550.patch`). With it, QBox's hart turned paging on in Sv57 and the interpreter's in Sv39, and both reached `Shell>`. ⚠️ What looked like a second hang on QBox, after BDS's first line, was not one: QBox counts 128 ns an instruction where the interpreter counts 100, and its boot is longer in the CPU's time, so it had not got there yet. The second fault planning read, the FD in RAM handed out as free, needs no patch yet: DXE allocates from the top of RAM down, and `memmap` shows the FD's 2 MiB inside a region marked available ([upstream.md](upstream.md) has both, and a third, the 64-byte section alignment that keeps DXE from protecting any image). The countdowns are 5 s each, the boot manager's coming from the `Timeout` variable's default. Nothing in the hart needed a patch.
- **Step 4: both commands answered the first time.** `ver` says `UEFI v2.70 (EDK II, 0x00010000)`, and `memmap`'s regions run from `0x8000_0000` to the end of the 64 MiB, OpenSBI's own 384 KiB at the start marked reserved. Its `Total Memory` leaves reserved pages out, so the test reads the regions. `memmap` takes 22 s of the interpreter's time, being 150 rows through the terminal driver, so both commands are typed in one boot.
- **Step 5: `reset` worked the first time**, from the shell through SBI's reset call to `syscon-reboot` and the system controller, and the ROM's line comes again. M23's helpers for describing and loading the host were moved to `tests/python/opensbi_boots.py` for both tests to share.
- **Step 6: 550 million instructions to the prompt**, counted on both harts to within one percent: 344 million of them SEC unpacking the rest of EDK2 with LZMA, 112 million DXE's drivers, and 84 million BDS and the shell. The interpreter ran them in 34 s on the wall, at 16 million a second; QBox in 1.6 s, at 343 million. A kernel's boot, M26's 1849 million, is three and a third times as long. The table is in [speed-spike.md](speed-spike.md#a-boot-that-ran-opensbi-and-uefi-to-the-shell-m28). The exit test's three boots take 2.6 minutes of the interpreter's Python suite and 11 s of QBox's.
- **Step 7: the docs.** How to boot UEFI in [boot-your-firmware.md](boot-your-firmware.md); the two CPUs' pages; [development.md](development.md) and [setup.md](setup.md) for EDK2, `uuid-dev` and `acpica-tools`; [upstream.md](upstream.md); the speed report. The nightly runs the exit test on QBox with the rest of the Python suite, and will be the first to build EDK2 on a Mac.

Exit: `Shell>` on the console with paging on, from the ROM and the quick tier; Python types `ver` and reads EDK2's version, types `memmap` and reads the RAM the devicetree gave, and types `reset` and sees the ROM's first line again; in CI, in the quick tier. The boot's instruction count and wall time are written beside M26's.

What Linux will ask: nothing it did not ask already. What M20 and M24 may take: the EFI stub, from a FAT image on the virtio block device or on the drive's own partition, as a tier.

### M25 — Virtio spike (only M20 waits on it)

🎓 **Virtio** is the family of devices a virtual machine offers its guest when it does not pretend to be real hardware: a block device, a network card, a console and a few more, each a small register block and rings of descriptors in the guest's memory. The **virtqueue** is the part they share: the driver puts buffers in a ring, rings a doorbell, and the device fills them and says so with an interrupt. **`virtio-mmio`** is the transport where the register block sits on the bus at an address the devicetree gives, which is the one that fits a SoC with no PCIe between the CPU and the device. Every Linux drives all of it, and so does QEMU's `virt` machine, which is what makes a virtio device the thing to reach for when the question is "is it the kernel, or is it my hardware?"

No real SoC has one, which is why none is on the road to Linux as a block: the console is the 16550, the first root filesystem is in memory, the drive is the real NVMe under Linux's own driver (M24), and the network is the Minres MAC with a driver of ours (M17). They are here as 🎭 stand-ins, in the sense the plan has used since M0: they hold a block's place so that the rest of the cast can rehearse. A kernel that boots with a virtio console and not with the 16550 has said where to look. A root filesystem on `virtio-blk` lets M24 take its time over the root complex. A `virtio-net` gives Linux a network before M17's driver exists. And a board whose console, disk and network are all virtio is the shape of QEMU's `virt`, which boots a distribution's kernel as it is; whether socpuppet should offer that board beside the host is the second question this milestone answers.

- **Five devices behind one transport**, each a model of ours: `virtio-mmio` as the register block and the virtqueue walker, written once, and `virtio-rng`, `virtio-console`, `virtio-blk`, `virtio-net` and `virtio-fs` as what hangs off it. 🧵 Python is at the far end of each: the entropy device's bytes come from a seed Python gives, the console's text is `uart.output`'s twin, the block device is backed by bytes Python gives it, the network hands Python each frame as M17's `sp.EthernetCable` will, and the file system is a directory on the machine running the simulation, served by Python.
- **Python is the first driver of every one.** Each device's contract is written from the specification and run with Python at the keyboard, through the scripted bus master, as M2's Python host drove the NVMe stand-in before any firmware existed. Then the drivers nobody here wrote: Zephyr 4.4.2's `virtio-mmio` transport with its entropy, console and network drivers and its virtiofs client, and EDK2's `VirtioBlkDxe`, `VirtioNetDxe`, `VirtioRngDxe` and `VirtioFsDxe` from M28's image, which find every `virtio,mmio` node in the devicetree. Zephyr has no block driver, and EDK2's image has no console driver; the table below says which driver meets which device. A FAT image Python made shows up as `fs0:` in the UEFI shell, and `ls` reads it. Linux's drivers are each device's third.
- **The devicetree in both dialects, and they differ.** ⚠️ Planning had them the same node; they are not. Linux's binding is the bare `virtio,mmio` node with `reg` and `interrupts`, and the device says what it is in a register. Zephyr's drivers bind to a child node under it (`virtio-console { compatible = "virtio,console"; }`, `virtio-net`, `virtio-entropy { compatible = "virtio,device4"; }`), and its virtiofs client takes the transport's node by its label. The slots sit where QEMU's `virt` has them, `0x1000_1000` and every 4 KiB after, which is inside the IO die's window, so each line is a source of the IO die's APLIC.
- **Borrowing was looked at.** VCML has the transport and the three devices, and was ruled out in M2 for ending the process on an error. QBox has QEMU's, from source and GPL. rust-vmm's are not SystemC. If the work finds one worth the adapter, it says so.
- **A report.** `docs/virtio-spike.md`: what each device took, what each borrowed driver found, which devices stay on the host board as stand-ins for M20, and what a virt-shaped board would take, with the rest of virtio's catalogue (SCSI, GPU, input, 9p, sound, balloon, vsock, IOMMU and the others Linux drives) read and costed. Then a stop for a decision.

**Decided on 2026-10-16, planning M25.**

- **The models are built in the tree, test-first, and the spike is the report.** M25 was written as a spike with the code to be thrown away. Asked by the maintainer: nothing under `spikes/` can be on the host board, in Python or under a Zephyr driver on the real board, since nothing in `src/`, `python/` or `tests/` may depend on a spike, and the exit asked for all three. With five devices wanted and no time box, writing them twice is the wrong price. So each device is a `/tdd` step with its contract first, and `spikes/virtio/` holds only the one-off scripts of step 18, deleted at the end. The report still decides what stays on the board.
- **Five devices, and the file system is virtio-fs, not 9p.** Asked by the maintainer: a device that shares a directory of the machine with the guest is what makes a development loop on Linux pleasant, with no disk image to rebuild. Zephyr 4.4.2 has a virtiofs client and EDK2's image has `VirtioFsDxe`, so virtio-fs meets three drivers nobody here wrote where 9p would meet Linux's alone. The entropy device goes first because it is the smallest, one queue the device only writes to, which makes it the walker's smoke test.
- **No Linux and no QEMU boot in this milestone.** M20 boots the kernel. The report reasons about Linux from its sources, its bindings and its configurations, which were read when this was planned.
- **Not time-boxed.** Every device finishes before the report.
- **QBox's hart for the runs, the interpreter as the fallback.** Asked by the maintainer, for speed: every run of borrowed firmware and every boot test is developed under `uv run ctest --preset qbox`, whose `SOCPUPPET_CPU=qbox_cpu` the host follows, and falls back to `--preset dev` when QBox stalls. A run that differs between the two harts is a finding for the report. CI stays as it is: both harts, as `ci.yml` has it since M27.
- **The slots are off the host by default.** `host(virtio=Virtio(...))` adds the five; without it the board's checked-in devicetree, every Zephyr image, twister's run and the M28 boot are byte for byte what they were. The Zephyr images for the runs get a shield, as the drive has one.
- **One registry name per device**, `virtio_rng` to `virtio_fs`, each with its own class in the catalogue and its own page, with the transport as shared code the way `CommandDevice` is. A reader of the address map wants "virtio-blk", not "virtio-mmio, device 2"; the five have different parameters; and the registry's parameters are numbers, so a device named by a number would have made the wrong thing a parameter.
- **The transport's registers are an `.rdl`.** They are the specification's and not ours, but three readers want them by name (the C++ logic, the Python driver and the docs table), which is what the rule for register maps exists for. Feature bits, device IDs, the block request, the network header and the FUSE structures are formats in memory, not registers, and are written by hand with the specification's section beside them, as the sideband packet is.

What planning found on 2026-10-12, corrected on 2026-10-16 against the trees on disk:

- **Zephyr's virtio is young and partial.** The transport (`drivers/virtio/virtio_mmio.c`, `virtio_pci.c`, `virtqueue.c`) came with 4.2, the console (`drivers/serial/uart_virtio_console.c`) and the network (`drivers/ethernet/eth_virtio_net.c`) with 4.3, and with `entropy_virtio.c` and the virtiofs client (`subsys/fs/virtiofs/`) those are the only devices. Its only test of them is build-only, on `qemu_cortex_a53`. No board in Zephyr's tree runs them on RISC-V. A first-time run again, as the AIA drivers were.
- **The virtqueue is the whole of the work.** The split virtqueue (descriptor table, available ring, used ring) is the part every device shares and the part that is easy to get subtly wrong; a device is then a few registers and a loop. Version 2 of the `virtio-mmio` register layout is what Linux and Zephyr both speak (`VIRTIO_MMIO_SUPPORTED_VERSION 2` in Zephyr's driver, which refuses any other), and the legacy layout is not needed. Zephyr's driver negotiates `VIRTIO_F_VERSION_1`, sets `FEATURES_OK` and reads it back, uses split rings and nothing else (no indirect descriptors, no event index), and writes the queue's size, its three addresses in halves and `QUEUE_READY`.
- **What each borrowed driver wants of the device.** Zephyr's network driver takes its MAC from the devicetree and never from the device; EDK2's `VirtioNetDxe` asserts `VIRTIO_NET_F_MAC`, so the device offers it and the Zephyr child node carries the same address. EDK2's `VirtioBlkDxe` wants a queue of three or more. Both FUSE clients want FUSE 7.31 with `READDIRPLUS` on offer and use queue 1 as the request queue. Zephyr's console driver has a transmit queue of one descriptor and waits for it to be used before the next character, so every character crosses the APLIC and the IMSIC; and it depends on the boot banner being off. EDK2 polls and never takes the interrupt. `VirtioMmioDeviceLib` takes versions 1 and 2.
- **Linux's own `defconfig` for RISC-V has every virtio driver built in and NVMe as a module** (`VIRTIO_MMIO`, `VIRTIO_BLK`, `VIRTIO_NET`, `VIRTIO_CONSOLE`, `HW_RANDOM_VIRTIO`, `SCSI_VIRTIO`, `NET_9P_VIRTIO` on; `BLK_DEV_NVME=m`), and `arch/riscv/Kconfig` selects the APLIC and IMSIC drivers. So a stock kernel mounts its root on `virtio-blk` with no initramfs, and on NVMe only with one that carries the module, or with a configuration of ours. That is the heart of the virt-shaped question.
- **The host is already most of QEMU's `virt`.** `hw/riscv/virt.c` has the UART at `0x1000_0000`, eight virtio slots at `0x1000_1000` every 4 KiB, the APLIC at `0x0C00_0000`, the IMSICs at `0x2400_0000` and `0x2800_0000` and RAM at `0x8000_0000`, and so does `host()`. What differs is the interrupt numbers (QEMU's slots take 1 to 8, which Zephyr keeps for the CPU's own lines, so ours take the IO APLIC's 18 to 22), the system controller and watchdog where `virt` has its test device and RTC, and the flash and ECAM addresses.
- **The exit test is on the 16550, the stand-ins beside it.** Zephyr's console driver exists, so the first run of `virtio-console` on the puppet is Zephyr's `hello_world` saying hello through it, with the 16550 silent.

The steps. Each is a `/tdd` session unless it says otherwise; a run of borrowed firmware is not one, and its pytest is written beside the run as the run teaches what to wait for. A fault in a borrowed driver or in EDK2 is a patch in `cmake/patches/` and an entry in [upstream.md](upstream.md); a fault in a Zephyr driver that the pinned tree cannot carry is an entry alone. A test that passes the first time is checked with a mutation, as M22's were.

| # | Step | Where |
|---|---|---|
| M25 0 | ✅ **The plan.** This table, what planning found and what was decided. Not a `/tdd` step | `docs/plan.md` |
| M25 1 | ✅ **The transport's register map.** `regs/virtio_mmio.rdl`, every register of version 2 with its fields and the device's configuration at `0x100`; the C header, the Python module and the table on `docs/models/virtio-mmio.md` generated, and the lint sees it compile. Not a `/tdd` step beyond the lint | `regs/virtio_mmio.rdl`, the generated files |
| M25 2 | ✅ **The virtqueue walker.** `Virtqueue` over a `MemoryPort`: a chain of one readable descriptor is popped with its bytes; a chain of three follows `next` and splits readable from writable; `Used` writes the element and then the index, which wraps at the queue's size; nothing is popped while `avail.idx` has not moved; rings above 4 GiB are read with both halves; a chain that loops is refused; a write across two writable buffers fills the first before the second and says how many bytes went | `core/virtqueue.h`, `tests/cpp/unit/virtqueue_test.cpp` |
| M25 3 | ✅ **The transport's logic, with a fake device.** `VirtioDevice` and `VirtioMmioLogic`: magic, version 2, the fake's id, the vendor; features read in halves with `VERSION_1` added to the fake's; `FEATURES_OK` kept only for a subset of what was offered that has bit 32; `STATUS` 0 resets queues, features, the interrupt and the fake; queue setup through `QUEUE_SEL` and the six halves, a size above the most refused; a notify on a ready queue hands every available chain to the fake's `Handle`, a used chain sets `INTERRUPT_STATUS` bit 0 and `Interrupting()`, an ack of that bit lowers it; the configuration is read and written at any width, the registers below it at 32 bits aligned only; `SHM_LEN` reads all ones; a debug read sees the registers | `core/virtio_device.h`, `core/virtio_mmio_logic.h`, `tests/cpp/unit/virtio_mmio_logic_test.cpp` |

Each later device adds its own slot | `components.py`, `devicetree.py`, `boards/virtio.py`, `boards/host.py`, `zephyr_module/boards/shields/socpuppet_host_virtio/`, `test_devicetree.py`, `test_linux_devicetree.py`, `test_host_board.py` |

A run, not a `/tdd` step | `zephyr_module/boards/shields/socpuppet_host_virtio_console/`, `firmware/build.py`, `test_virtio_zephyr.py` |
| M25 10 | ✅ **The block device.** `VirtioBlkLogic` (id 2, one queue, `capacity` in sectors over `BlockStore`): `IN` fills the data buffers and writes status 0; `OUT` stores; a sector past the end is `IOERR`; `GET_ID` answers a name and `FLUSH` status 0; an unknown type is `UNSUPP`; `Load(bytes)` and `Contents()` through the bindings, `PlacedBlock`, `sp.VirtioBlk(blocks=)`, slot 2 with no child; `VirtioBlkHost` writes two sectors and reads them back, and `blk.contents` shows them | `core/virtio_blk_logic.h`, bindings, `components.py`, `boards/virtio.py`, the unit test, `test_virtio_host.py` |

What the boot manager does with a disk before the shell is measured and written down. A run, not a `/tdd` step | `firmware/virtiofs/`, `firmware/build.py`, `test_virtio_zephyr.py` |

And the catalogue: every virtio device Linux has a driver for, whether `defconfig` builds it, and what each would cost on this transport. Not a `/tdd` step | `spikes/virtio/compare_virt.py`, the report |
| M25 19 | **The report, the docs and the plan.** `docs/virtio-spike.md` in [npu-spike.md](npu-spike.md)'s shape with the virt-shaped table and the catalogue; the pages of the transport, the five devices and the Python driver; [architecture.md](architecture.md)'s table and list; [address-map.md](address-map.md) drawn with the slots; [boot-your-firmware.md](boot-your-firmware.md) on `host(virtio=)` and the shields; here: the marks, what the steps found, Status, the to-do and the open decision; `spikes/virtio/` deleted. Then a stop for the maintainer's decision. Not a `/tdd` step | docs, `tools/address_map_docs.py`, `docs/plan.md` |

Order: 0; 1 and 2 before 3; 3 before 4; 4 before 5 and 6; 5 and 6 before 7; then 8 and 9, 10 and 11, 12, 13 and 14, 15, 16 and 17, each pair in order and each driver's run before the next device is started; 18 any time after 6; 19 last. Twister's board-compliance run once after 6, since the module gains a shield and the bindings grow, though the board's own devicetree does not change.

What the steps found, as they went:

- **Step 0: the plan.** Written on 2026-10-16.
- **Step 2: the walker, eight tests.** `Virtqueue` over a `MemoryPort`, with the driver's side of the rings written by the test the way the specification lays them out, in a memory above 4 GiB so that every address needs its 64 bits. A looping chain is told by a chain longer than the table it is in, and the first test of it hung the test binary rather than fail, which is the fault it guards against. Two tests passed first time and were checked with a mutation: the wrap without its `% size`, and the sticky refusal without its `broken_`. 💡 The design review had the two branches the first test did not need taken out and put back by their own tests.
- **Step 3: the transport, eighteen tests.** `VirtioDevice` is the five things the transport asks of a device (its id, its features, its queue count, its configuration, and `Handle` for a chain, with `Reset`), and `VirtioMmioLogic` has the four methods `CommandDevice` wants, so step 4's shell can be that. A used chain is told by the queue's used count, so a device may give a chain back inside `Handle` or later from wherever its answer comes, and the driver hears of it the same way. ⚠️ A `std::optional<Virtqueue>` cannot be assigned over, since the queue holds a reference to the memory, so a reset clears a queue's fields one by one. 🎓 The mutation of the alignment rule did not compile: GCC's array-bounds check refuses a two-byte buffer where a word is stored, which is a check at compile time for free. The reviews had the duplicated `ready` flag dropped for the queue itself, a stale doorbell on a queue not yet set up refused, and the configuration's bound written so an offset off the bus cannot overflow it.
- **Step 4: the entropy device, the shell, the slot and the contract.** `VirtioMmio<Device>` is `CommandDevice` with a `dma` socket, so the register block is `cpu` as the DMA engine's is, and the plan's `socket` was wrong. The contract is typed over a rig that names the device and describes a chain that kind of device answers at once, and builds the platform from the registry with a router, since a memory's socket takes one binding and both the driver and the device's DMA want it. Its last item, that the device touches nothing until the doorbell write has returned, is the one the mutation of the shell (carrying out inside the access) failed. ⚠️ The kernel tests run one to a process, so a contract is run through `ctest -R`, not the binary. The design review had the bare `device()` accessor taken out before a far end could change a device with nothing making the shell look again, which step 8 settles.
- **Step 5: Python is the first driver.** `sp.VirtioHost` in `NvmeHost`'s shape, every step a generator over the scripted bus master's operations, with each ring on a page of its own and the buffers after; `post` and `take` under `submit`, so that a later device can leave a chain with the device and collect it when the device is done. Four tests: the entropy device on a scripted host, and three against a fake device with no simulator (a wrong magic, features beyond what was offered, FEATURES_OK not kept).
- **Step 6: the node, the slots and the shield.** `DeviceNode.children`, and the roles and aliases can name a child, which is how `zephyr,entropy` names the entropy device's child; a `virtio_node` built from a device's kind and number, so the four devices to come add a line each. `host(virtio=Virtio())` puts the slots on the IO die at `0x1000_1000`, where QEMU's `virt` has its first, with lines from the IO APLIC's source 18, and the board without them is byte for byte what it was. ⚠️ A scripted bus master chooses nothing, so the test of `chosen` wants a CPU. The design review moved the host's numbers and the overlay next to the drive's, which also undid an import cycle.
- **Step 7: Zephyr's entropy driver passes, and two things stood between it and that, one for each hart.** The image is `entropy_api_socpuppet_host`, Zephyr's `tests/drivers/entropy/api` built with the shield, and its one case passes through Zephyr's own `virtio_mmio` transport, with the device's bytes reaching the test. (1) Zephyr's transport sets a feature bit by reading `DRIVER_FEATURES` back, a register the specification has the driver only write, so the transport's refusal of the read was a load access fault at `0x1000_1020`. The register reads now, the half that `DRIVER_FEATURES_SEL` names, as QEMU's device has it: a `/tdd` item in `VirtioMmioLogic` and `sw = rw` in the `.rdl`. (2) Zephyr's `virtq_create` puts its table of callbacks straight after the used ring, whose size is `8 * N + 6`, so the pointers in it sit 6 bytes past a multiple of 8. QEMU's hart does the misaligned store, and the image passed on QBox as it was, in 0.42 s of wall clock. DBT-RISE's interpreter traps on it, as the specification allows a hart to (cause 6, at `virtqueue.c:160`). `cmake/patches/zephyr-virtqueue-callbacks-aligned.patch` pads the table up to the pointers' alignment, and `firmware/build.py` now applies `zephyr-*.patch` to the clone as it applies OpenSBI's and EDK2's, with a tooling test. Both are in [upstream.md](upstream.md), and the second is the first patch to Zephyr's tree. 💡 The difference between the harts is the kind M28 said to write down: QEMU's hart hides a fault in a driver that a stricter hart, or a real one without Zicclsm, would show.
- **Step 8: the console, and the first device that keeps a chain.** `VirtioConsoleLogic` answers the transmit queue as it is handed over and holds a receive chain, with its ring, until the far end types; `VirtioDevice::Deliver` is the transport's one call for that, made at every carry-out, so a chain given back with no doorbell still raises the interrupt, and `CommandDevice::Nudge` is how the shell has the logic carry out again when the far end hands something in, a delta cycle on, since whoever types may be a script between two runs. The contract now takes the queue a device answers on (`kAnswersOn`), since the console's is its second. The design review had two things made one: the UART and the console share `FarEnd` (one binding pair, `PlacedFarEnd` under `PlacedUart` and `PlacedConsole`), and a virtio host's `start` is the shared part's, from `DEVICE_ID`, `QUEUES` and `KIND`. ⚠️ The child node's label is the transport's `_device` now, not its kind, which spared the host an `io_console_console` and is why the shield's overlay changed under the entropy device too. Zephyr's console binds to `virtio,console` by name where its entropy driver binds to `virtio,device4` by number, so `virtio_node` takes the binding. The console is not chosen as `zephyr,console`: step 9's shield says that, and the 16550 stays the console otherwise.
- **Step 9: Zephyr's greeting through its own virtio console, one interrupt a character.** The shield `socpuppet_host_virtio_console` is three lines of `chosen` and one of `.conf` (`CONFIG_BOOT_BANNER=n`, since the driver's Kconfig depends on no banner and no early console), stacked on the slots' shield as `"socpuppet_host_virtio;socpuppet_host_virtio_console"`, and the image is `hello_virtio_console`. It passed the first time on both harts, in 0.1 s of wall time each. 💡 Zephyr's console asks for queues of one entry, so each character after the first waits for the used-buffer interrupt to give its descriptor back: with the transport's interrupt taken out, the console says `H` and nothing more. So the run is about forty interrupts through the IO APLIC, across the link and into the IMSIC, which is the slot's interrupts seen without a probe at the IMSIC. ⚠️ The driver blocks for that descriptor inside a spinlock, which works on one hart because the idle thread runs with interrupts on; that is Zephyr's to answer, not ours, and is in the report. ⚠️ `socpuppet run` (and so twister) knows the shield `socpuppet_host_drive` and neither virtio shield, so these images run from pytest only; a to-do for the report.
- **Step 10: the disk, and the first configuration a driver must not change.** `VirtioBlkLogic` is a disk of 512-byte sectors over the `RamBlockStore` the NVMe models use, one queue, `FLUSH` offered and answered at once, `GET_ID` answering `socpuppet`. 💡 It is the first device with a configuration, and the transport had been writing a driver's config writes straight into it, so a driver could change the capacity it reads back; the spec makes it read-only. `VirtioDevice::Config()` is now read-only and `WriteConfig` drops a write unless a device takes it, as QEMU's devices do; none of the five has a field a driver may write. The design review moved the refusal of an image larger than the disk into the model, where every C++ caller gets it, and gave `blocks` a range. ⚠️ With no `SEG_MAX` offered, Linux sends one data segment a request, which is slower and still correct; a finding for the report, not a fix. Zephyr has no virtio disk driver, so the node has no child, and Zephyr's transport still probes the slot at boot and leaves it alone: both Zephyr runs pass with the disk in the tree.
- **Step 11: UEFI reads and writes the disk, and would boot from it.** Everything worked the first time, on both harts: `VirtioFdtDxe` found the three slots (`VirtioMmioInit virtio 1.0, id 4`, `3`, `2`), `VirtioRngDxe` and `VirtioBlkDxe` bound, the FAT driver mounted the image as `FS0:`, `ls` and `type` read the file Python made, and `echo >a` wrote one Python reads back out of `blk.contents`. The console's slot has no driver in EDK2's image and is left alone. 💡 The boot manager tries the disk before the shell: `Boot0001 "UEFI Non-Block Boot Device"` on the disk's path, `Not Found` because there is no `\EFI\BOOT\BOOTRISCV64.EFI`. So a disk with that file on it, a bootloader or a kernel's EFI stub, is booted with no shell and no typing, which is the report's road to Linux. The FAT image is pyfatfs's, borrowed and held to `fsck.fat`: its volume label and PyFilesystem2's `pkg_resources` import are two entries in [upstream.md](upstream.md). Each device has a boot of its own, not one shared boot as this table first said, since the kernel allows one platform to a process and a shared boot would make one test of them all: on QBox a boot to `Shell>` and the commands take 3 s of wall time, on the interpreter 49 s, and xdist runs them side by side. M28's helpers for typing at the shell moved to `tests/python/uefi_shell.py`.

Exit: each of the five devices behind the one transport taking chains from a virtqueue and saying so with an interrupt, driven first by Python; Zephyr's entropy, console, network and virtiofs drivers and EDK2's block, network, entropy and file system drivers each working against the model as they are, or the entry in [upstream.md](upstream.md) saying why not; the report with its recommendation and the virt-shaped table. Then a stop. What the decision keeps is on `host(virtio=)` for M20 to reach for.

What Linux will ask: the bare `virtio,mmio` nodes in the Linux dialect, which `host(sbi=True, virtio=Virtio(...))` writes, and nothing else: its drivers are in every kernel, and in the stock configuration.

### M20 — Linux boots

The host's firmware becomes what a RISC-V server runs: OpenSBI, which M23 booted, and then Linux in supervisor mode. Everything Linux needs was built in Phases 4 and 5 with Zephyr and OpenSBI as its first users, and each milestone there ends with what Linux will ask of it. Those lists are where this milestone's plan starts. The root filesystem is in memory, an initramfs with busybox in it, so that nothing but the console stands between the kernel and its prompt: the drive is M24's, the network M17's and the AI processor M19's.

- **A kernel of 6.10 or later**, where the IMSIC and APLIC drivers landed, pinned in `firmware/versions.toml` and built in `firmware/build.py`'s Docker flow: one hart, no SMP, the 8250 console, the AIA drivers, and an initramfs built in.
- **Interrupts through AIA**, with the supervisor's interrupt file and its registers (M22 c) and the fix to supervisor interrupts in DBT-RISE that M13 made ([upstream.md](upstream.md)).
- **The quick tier in CI.** Python puts `fw_jump`, the kernel and the devicetree where they go, as M23's quick tier does. The ROM tier, which checks a kernel's megabytes a byte at a time, is M14's honest price and runs on demand; its time is measured and written down.
- **What a kernel's boot shows that a payload's does not**: paging on for good, user programs, the scheduler on the timer, and a thousand devicetree properties read. GDB on the host with the kernel's symbols, and the instruction trace M13 step 4 used, are the tools for what goes wrong.
- **The server's path, as a tier.** UEFI (M28) loads the same kernel through its EFI stub from a FAT image on the virtio block device, the way a server's firmware loads it from an EFI System Partition, and hands it the devicetree as a configuration table. On demand, not in the quick tier; and a kernel that boots one way and not the other has said which side the fault is on.
- 🎭 **The virtio stand-ins M25 kept are the second console, the spare disk and the only network.** A kernel that speaks on `virtio-console` and not on the 16550 has said which side the fault is on. The exit test is on the 16550 with the initramfs, as the real SoC would be; the stand-ins are what the plan for this milestone reaches for when a step stalls, and what M24 and M17 swap out for the real thing.

Exit: a shell prompt on the console; Python types `uname -a` and reads the answer, with the kernel's version in it; in CI, in the quick tier.

### M24 — A root filesystem on the drive

The drive has been a Zephyr host's since M3b and a Linux kernel's never. This milestone mounts Linux's root filesystem on it, under Linux's own NVMe driver, across the link, with the SSD's own Zephyr firmware being the drive, which is M8's bootchain with Linux on the host.

- **The root complex, as M23 decided**: Linux's generic ECAM host driver, or a driver of ours for `socpuppet,pcie`.
- **MSI-X as messages to the IMSIC**, which is how the drive's interrupts already travel: the root complex's node says `msi-parent`, and Linux's PCI MSI code aims each vector at the IMSIC's page as socpuppet's Zephyr driver does.
- **A filesystem Python wrote.** A disk image with a root filesystem on it is loaded into the drive from Python, as the drive's contents are today, and `root=/dev/nvme0n1p1` takes it from there.
- **Data checked from outside.** A file written from Linux's shell is read back by Python through the NVMe host stand-in (`bring_up_the_drive`), as the exit tests of M6 to M8 check the Zephyr host's writes.
- **UEFI's own NVMe driver, as a second opinion.** EDK2's `NvmExpressDxe` (M28) finds the drive through the root complex before Linux does, which is the second driver to answer the question about ECAM, and a kernel on an EFI System Partition on the drive is how a server really boots. A tier, on demand; the exit is Linux's driver and `root=`.

Exit: Linux boots to a prompt with its root filesystem on the drive; a file written at the prompt is read back by Python, byte for byte, from the drive the Zephyr SSD firmware runs.

What Linux will ask of the IOMMU, next: nothing, until M15 puts one in the drive's path.

## Phase 6: Linux grows

Linux boots, and the rest of the cast of a real SoC arrives under its own drivers. Each milestone here was planned in Phase 4 with Zephyr as its first user, and keeps that: a slot and its contract suite first, a Zephyr driver, a Zephyr sample or test that passes. What each gains from coming after M20 is a second customer, Linux's own driver for the block, as the last line of its exit test. The order is the order a server's firmware developer would ask for them: the network, then memory protection for the devices, then the accelerator, then the small parts.

### M17 — Ethernet

VPV-Peripherals has an Ethernet MAC, the block that sends and receives frames, and it is the simplest kind: firmware pushes a frame into a register a word at a time, length first, and pulls a received one out the same way. There are no rings of descriptors and no DMA. It follows RTL of Minres's own.

- **The borrowed MAC, behind an adapter and held to a contract written first**, as the UART, the timer and the PLIC were in M3a.
- 🧵 **Python is the far end of the cable.** `sp.EthernetCable` hands Python each frame the MAC sends and takes frames to deliver. The model can write what it carried to a capture file that Wireshark opens. 🚧 A bridge to the host machine's own network is a tier for later: the one VPV-Peripherals has is Linux-only.
- **The driver is ours**, in socpuppet's Zephyr module, since no driver for this MAC was found anywhere. Zephyr's driver for the Stellaris MAC is the nearest in shape, a FIFO with the length in its first word. The MAC's management registers store what is written and do nothing, so there is no PHY to talk to, and the driver says the link is fixed.
- **Its interrupt is a line to the APLIC**, which is why this waits for M13.

What planning found: SCC's library of interfaces has the Ethernet sockets the MAC uses, and nothing in socpuppet's build links that library yet. Only the first of the MAC's two interrupt outputs is ever driven.

Exit: Zephyr's `echo_server` sample on the host answers a datagram Python sends down the cable, having answered Python's ARP request first, and the capture of the run opens in Wireshark. Then, with the host running Linux (M20), Linux answers Python's `ping`, which was the first Linux exit test's second line until 2026-10-12 and is this milestone's last.

What Linux will ask: a driver, which will be ours as well, and is offered upstream with the Zephyr one. 📮 Since this milestone comes after M20, the Linux driver is written here and not left for later.

### M15 — IOMMU

A device that does DMA writes wherever it is told to, and a driver with a bug, or a device with a grudge, tells it wrong. An **IOMMU** is to a device what the MMU is to a program: every address a device uses goes through page tables that the operating system owns, and an access that is not mapped is refused and reported. This one follows the RISC-V IOMMU specification. A **device context** says which page table a device's addresses go through, a table of them is found by the device's ID, and two rings in memory carry commands to the IOMMU and fault records from it.

- **Ours, and written to go upstream.** It is a plain class with a thin model around it, as the NVMe frontend is, with its contract suite first. It is also shaped for VPV-Peripherals' `rvi/` directory, beside the APLIC and the PLIC: the registers in SystemRDL the way that project writes them, and its register classes. socpuppet uses it through an adapter like any other borrowed model. 📮 The offer is an entry in [upstream.md](upstream.md).
- ⚠️ **This bends a rule, on purpose.** "Only socpuppet's own blocks have a register map, not what a specification lays out" was decided on 2026-10-09. A block laid out by a specification and written for another project has one in that project's style, and nothing of ours is generated from it.
- **The subset is what the drivers touch**: the capabilities, the device directory with one to three levels, the command queue with its three commands, the fault queue, the interrupt registers, and translation through one stage of Sv39, the three-level page table a RISC-V CPU's own MMU uses. No second stage for virtual machines, no ATS, no page requests, no remapping of interrupt messages.
- **It sits on the IO die between the root complex and the link**, with its registers on the IO die's bus and its own interrupt a wire to the APLIC. A second one sits on the compute die in front of the AI processor, in M19.
- **A device says who it is.** A PCIe endpoint knows its own bus, device and function numbers once the host has enumerated it, and marks its DMA with them in the link's extension. A master with no PCIe behind it is given an ID as a parameter.
- **Zephyr has no IOMMU subsystem, so the driver's interface is our own**, as the frontend's and the DMA engine's are: let a device through untranslated, map a page for it, read a fault. An IOMMU in the DMA path is a function handed to the host.
- **Linux's driver is the second customer, and the last line of the exit.** Since 2026-10-12 this milestone comes after Linux boots with its root filesystem on the drive (M24), so the drive's DMA goes untranslated under Linux first, as it does under Zephyr, and the IOMMU is then put in its path under Linux's own `riscv,iommu` driver, which is the real test of a block written to the specification.

What planning found:

- ⚠️ **Nothing on the PCIe link says which device sent an access.** M2 wrote that down and M7 worked round it by telling traffic apart by address. An IOMMU cannot: the device's ID is the first thing it looks up. The root complex sees one way up and cannot supply it, so the endpoint has to.
- **The page tables are in the host's memory, a die away.** Every walk the IOMMU makes is a read across the link, through a bus master of its own, and shows in the link's trace. That is true of the hardware too, and it is why a real IOMMU caches what it walked.
- **The Ethernet MAC is not a customer.** It moves frames through its registers and does no DMA (M17). The drive is the only one until the AI processor, so the contract suite is run with a scripted DMA master as well.

Exit: Zephyr's disk test with the drive behind a device context the host's firmware programmed, first untranslated and then through a page table. And a transfer aimed at a page that is not mapped: a record in the fault queue, an interrupt, and the host's memory as it was. Then M24's root filesystem mounted with the drive behind Linux's own IOMMU driver.

What Linux will ask: the registers and commands its driver uses, which is where the subset above comes from. A `riscv,iommu` node and an `iommu-map` on the root complex's. And the IOMMU's own interrupts as messages, where Zephyr is given a wire.

### M19 — AI processor

M12's gate chose 🎭 the behavioral NPU of ours, on the compute die, with its arithmetic in Python ([npu-spike.md](npu-spike.md), under Decisions). What the host's firmware has to do is what it would do with any NPU: describe the work, ring a doorbell, and wait for an interrupt.

- **The block**: a register map of ours, a queue of descriptors in memory (an operation, where its operands are, their shapes and their type), a handful of operations (multiply matrices, add, clamp at zero, perhaps convolve), its data moved by DMA through an IOMMU of its own, and its completion a message to the IMSIC. A Zephyr driver, and `firmware/mlp`, which runs a small multi-layer perceptron on an input both sides know.
- 🧵 **The arithmetic is a Python callable, and the library behind it is interchangeable.** The block hands each operation's operands to Python as buffers and takes the product back as one; numpy is what the default callable is written with, and torch or another library is a different callable, not a C++ change. Planning M19 says where the callable is set (on the board, or from the strings at run time) and whether numpy is a dependency of the wheel or an extra. The spike's `Multiplier` seam in C++ is the draft of it.
- 🦜 **Not QBox's Hexagon**, which the spike also ran. It stays a candidate for an optional, from-source component later, behind the same doorbell.

Exit: an inference whose weights were read from flash, whose data went through the IOMMU, whose completion was an interrupt message, and whose answer Python checks against numpy's. Then the same inference from Linux user space, which was the first Linux exit test's last line until 2026-10-12 and is this milestone's.

What Linux will ask: one memory region and one interrupt, so that a user-space driver (UIO) can run the same inference. Since this milestone comes after M20, that driver is written here.

### M18 — The small essentials

The parts every board has and no milestone has needed. Each is one `/tdd` session: a contract or unit tests first, a model page, a Zephyr sample that passes, and Python at the far end of the wires.

- **GPIO and SPI**: VPV-Peripherals' SiFive blocks behind adapters. Zephyr and Linux both have drivers for them already. With a driver that speaks SPI, the boot flash gets the read command it lacks.
- **I2C**: ours and small, with the registers of the OpenCores controller, which Linux drives. 🎭 A Python script plays the device at the other end of the bus.
- **RTC**: ours, with the registers of the Goldfish clock, which Linux drives. It tells the time Python gives it.
- **TRNG**, a true random number generator: one register, read through Linux's driver for a random number in a memory-mapped register. It is seeded from Python, so that a run can be had again.

For each of ours, look for a Zephyr driver of that layout before writing one.

What Linux will ask: nothing more. Choosing layouts it already drives is the whole of the plan for these, and since this milestone comes after M20, the RTC and the TRNG are exercised by Linux's own drivers as well as Zephyr's: `date` reads the time Python gave the RTC, and `/dev/hwrng` reads the seeded stream.

## Phase 7: The rest of the cast, and fidelity

What nothing on the road to Linux needs: a second socket and the memory it lends, a NAND that wears, the scenarios that make a drive interesting, and the IO die's telemetry. Each can be pulled forward when somebody wants it, and M9 could have been since M4.

### M16 — SHRM and the two-socket board

**SHRM** is the shared-memory subsystem: memory on one SoC that another SoC on the same board can use as if it were its own. It is how a server with several sockets, or a tray of AI accelerators, pools memory, and a real one is a small computer in its own right, with a controller core and firmware that decide who is lent what. Here it is a pool of memory on the IO die, a set of decoders that put pieces of the pool into somebody's address map, a port to the other SoC, a mailbox between the two controllers, and a 32-bit core with its kit.

It also brings a second SoC into the simulation, which the plan had left open as "a second compute die".

- **a) One SoC's SHRM, with 🎭 a script for its firmware.** The decoders have the shape of CXL's for host-managed device memory: a base, a size, a target and an enable, in a register map of ours. `sp.ShrmFirmware` is the stand-in, as `sp.SsdFirmware` was for the SSD. Exit: the host maps a piece of its own SHRM's pool and uses it.
- **b) Two sockets on a board, over 🎭 the pass-through.** `host()` becomes a function that adds a host to a platform under a name, as `add_ssd` and `add_manager` are, in a change of its own with no change in behaviour. Then `two_socket_board()` places two. The port between sockets is the link slot again, with the stand-in link first. Exit: one host writes into the piece of the other's pool it was lent, and the other reads it.
- **c) The firmware, and a link that has to be brought up.** `firmware/shrm`, a Zephyr image on the board `socpuppet_shrm`, lends and publishes, and the two of them agree over the mailbox. The real link between sockets is `sp.D2dLink` with a board's latency. Exit: the message crosses the board with every CPU running its own firmware, and `sp.Transcript` tells all their consoles as one story.
- ⚠️ **No protocol is modelled between the sockets.** What crosses is memory-mapped TLM, as on the die-to-die mainband. A real board would speak CXL or something like it there, and the model's page says that this one does not. Whether it ever should is in the open decisions below.

What planning found:

- ⚠️ **A decoder must hide what is behind it.** The walk that makes a devicetree does not clip to a window's size, which M7 found, so a host would be told about everything on the other SoC's bus. The decoder describes itself as one memory and stops the walk, as the root complex does for what is beyond it.
- ⚠️ **Six to eight CPUs in one kernel.** Two sockets, each with a host, a manager and a SHRM, and a drive or two. M8 measured three. The quantum's figures and the wall time are measured again in step b, not guessed at.

What Linux will ask: the lent memory as a reserved-memory node it can map, and the mailbox as a mailbox controller.

### M9 — Realistic NAND

Geometry, tR/tPROG/tBERS delays, erase-before-write, sparse or file-backed storage, bad-block and bit-error hooks, NAND contract suite; FTL gains garbage collection.

### M10 — Validation scenarios

Boot-sequencing variants and failures, link down/retrain during I/O, cross-die data integrity, bad blocks, power loss, IOPS/latency stats.

Nothing in this list is on the road to Linux, which is why it waits. A scenario is pulled forward on its own when a Linux milestone needs it to verify something, and is then that milestone's test and not this one's.

### M11 — Stretch: power/telemetry

A power/telemetry model on the IO die. 🦜 The Verilator RTL block behind a TLM-to-signal adapter that this milestone used to list was taken out on 2026-10-12: nothing on the road to Linux wants it, and no block of ours has RTL to put there. If one ever does, it comes back as a milestone of its own.

## Decisions deliberately left open

| Decision | Must be settled by | Default until then |
|---|---|---|
| `native_sim` firmware tier | Optional, any time after M3 | not built |
| Does the boot ROM check an image's signature, which is what makes a boot secure? | After M14 | a CRC and nothing more |
| A protocol between sockets, CXL or one like it, where M16 has memory-mapped TLM | After M16 | none |
| A die of its own for the AI processor; host DRAM on the IO die | After M16 | one compute die a socket, DRAM on the compute die |
| Which CPU the host runs by default and ships in the wheel, and whether a faster one is offered beside it | M26's report recommends the interpreter in the wheel, and nothing beside it until a translating backend passes the hart's suite, LLVM first ([speed-spike.md](speed-spike.md)); the maintainer decides | DBT-RISE's interpreter. QBox is offered beside it since M27, built from source, as a second opinion on a boot rather than a faster default ([models/qbox-cpu.md](models/qbox-cpu.md)) |
| Which kernel to pin | M20 | a kernel of 6.10 or later, where the AIA drivers landed |

Settled at M28, on 2026-10-15: EDK2 is `edk2-stable202608`, its latest stable tag. Settled at M23, on 2026-10-15: OpenSBI is v1.9, its latest release; and the root complex is a generic ECAM host to Linux, `pci-host-ecam-generic` in Linux's dialect, with no driver of ours, which M24 runs. Settled on 2026-10-10, planning Phase 4, having been left open until after M8: the bootchain has a ROM stage for each image ([M14](#m14--boot-rom-flash-system-controller-watchdog)), and a second SoC, which is more than the second compute die this table used to ask about, comes with [M16](#m16--shrm-and-the-two-socket-board). Settled the same day at M12's gate: the AI processor is 🎭 a behavioral one of ours, on the compute die, with its arithmetic a Python function written with numpy for now and the library interchangeable ([npu-spike.md](npu-spike.md), under Decisions). The row about a die of its own stays open: the gate kept it on the compute die for want of a reason to move it, not for good.

## Verification

- Every milestone ends with the automated end-to-end test in its "Exit test" column, run in GitHub Actions on Ubuntu LTS.
- Every slot interface has one gtest contract suite; a stand-in and its full model both pass it before either is used in an integration milestone.
- Stand-in configurations from earlier milestones stay in CI, so the standalone kits from M3 to M5 keep working after M6 to M8 land.
- Model logic lives in plain C++ classes, unit-tested without the SystemC kernel. A borrowed model is the exception: it is tested through its contract suite, behind its adapter.
- From M21, Zephyr's own test suites run on every board under twister, Zephyr's test runner, with socpuppet as the emulator: a board-compliance suite in CI, and on demand on a developer's machine. A suite that is excluded is in a quarantine list with its reason. It is not part of the inner loop: an image under work is run natively with `socpuppet run`.
- From M20, Linux boots to its prompt in CI, in the quick tier (the image put in RAM by Python). The ROM tier, which checks a kernel's megabytes a byte at a time, is run on demand.

## Status

**M0 to M8 are done.** Every subsystem has booted its own firmware standalone, which was Phase 2. All three now boot together, which was Phase 3: the manager's firmware trains the die-to-die link and lets the host go, the host's firmware finds its drive across the link, and the SSD's firmware is the drive. Each CPU can have a debugger of its own. Phase 4 is next, the cast of a real SoC, which was added on 2026-10-10 and starts with M12's spike. **M12 is done**: both candidates took the exit test's multiply from a descriptor and raised the host's interrupt, the report is [npu-spike.md](npu-spike.md), and the gate chose 🎭 the behavioral NPU on the compute die, with its arithmetic in Python (numpy for now, the library interchangeable). **M13 is done**: the host's core is RV64GC with supervisor mode, its interrupt controller is an IMSIC that is sent messages, and an APLIC on the IO die sends the console's interrupt across the link as one. Zephyr's shell on the host answers what Python types. **M14 is done**: every CPU can boot as silicon does, from a ROM that finds its image in a flash, with nothing loaded into RAM; a bad image is a sentence on the console; and a watchdog that is not fed resets its CPU, which reads why from its system controller. Python loading an image straight into RAM is still the default and the quick way. **M21 is done**: Zephyr's own test suites run on all three boards under twister, Zephyr's test runner, with socpuppet as the emulator, and so do suites of socpuppet's own for each of its drivers and for the SSD firmware's logic. 113 scenarios pass and none fails, 2153 test cases, and what is left out is in a quarantine list with its reasons ([twister.md](twister.md)). Its spike found two bugs in borrowed models that no socpuppet test had, and both are mended. The `twister` job in CI runs them for every pull request and every push to `main`, in twenty minutes on a quick runner and thirty-three on a slow one. **M22 is done**, the first milestone of the road to Linux (Phase 5): the host's hart has been run the way Linux will run it, with paging, user mode, the supervisor's interrupt registers, the counters, floating-point state, atomics and misaligned accesses, by a hundred and ten tests, nearly all of them a small program that does one of those things. Some forty-five were red on the borrowed core as it came, and fifteen patches mend what they found, among them that it had never translated an instruction fetch, that it took a kernel's interrupt inside its monitor's handler, and that it hid the floating-point state from supervisor mode. Its item about speed was made a spike of its own when it was planned, M26. **M26 is done**: the host's core timed on three loops on three machines in seven environments, DBT-RISE's asmjit and LLVM backends built behind an option and timed and tested beside it, QBox timed where it built, a kernel's boot counted on QEMU, and the report, [speed-spike.md](speed-spike.md), recommends the MacBook Air natively on the interpreter for the Linux work, with LLVM there when a boot is too long to wait for, and no change to what ships. The report's QBox row became a milestone, [M27](#m27--qbox-the-second-hart-optional-built-from-source), on 2026-10-14: a second hart to run the same boot on, so that the Linux work can tell a bug of the hart's from one of the firmware's or a device's. **M27 is done**: QEMU's RISC-V CPU, by way of QBox, is the `qbox_cpu`, built from source behind an option, in one program and one SystemC kernel with the rest of socpuppet, held to the bus-master contract and the hart's suite, and the host's CPU in every Python boot and twister run when `SOCPUPPET_CPU` says so, nightly in CI. **M23 is done**: OpenSBI v1.9 boots on the host, from the flash through the boot ROM and the quick way, on a devicetree in Linux's dialect that the same description generates, and its test payload and one of ours run on it in supervisor mode. SBI's console, timer and reset calls reach the UART, the ACLINT and the system controller, a reboot comes back through the ROM, and a shutdown switches the CPU off. It took a patch to the hart for `mtopi` and `stopi` and one to OpenSBI, whose syscon could not write past the first quarter of its block. **M28 is done**: UEFI, EDK2's RISC-V port, boots on OpenSBI to its shell, from the flash and the quick way, on both harts, with paging on and the timer's interrupt taken in supervisor mode, and Python types `ver` and `memmap` at it and resets it. It took one patch to EDK2, whose SEC left a 16550 without a FIFO out of the page tables, and none to either hart.
